### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

The current IFOMPT terminology is **vascular pathologies of the neck**. The historical label "cervical artery dysfunction" is retained in this route for continuity, but should not be read as a single diagnostic entity. Internal carotid and vertebral artery pathology can masquerade as musculoskeletal neck pain or headache and requires careful history, neurological examination and clinical reasoning.

**This is a critical safety topic for all physiotherapists performing cervical assessment and treatment.**

### Vertebrobasilar Insufficiency (VBI) / Vertebral Artery Dissection (VAD)
- **Vertebral arteries (VA)**: Arise from subclavian arteries; enter transverse foramina at C6, ascend through C6–C1, pierce dura, enter skull via foramen magnum, and unite to form basilar artery
- **Internal carotid arteries (ICA)**: Ascend anterior to VA; enter petrous canal; supply anterior circulation
- VA supplies posterior brain circulation: brainstem, cerebellum, occipital lobe, medial temporal lobe

**Pathology:**
1. **Atherosclerotic VBI**: Progressive stenosis of VA/basilar artery; older patients (>55); embolic or haemodynamic ischaemia; symptoms often positional but mechanical compression less important than atheroma/embolism
2. **Vertebral Artery Dissection (VAD)**: Intimal tear → intramural haematoma → stenosis, occlusion, or thromboembolism; may be spontaneous or trauma-related (whiplash, manipulation, trivial trauma); peak incidence 40s
3. **Internal Carotid Artery Dissection (ICAD)**: Similar mechanism; anterior circulation TIA/stroke; classically presents with neck pain + ipsilateral Horner's syndrome + contralateral focal deficit
4. **Bow Hunter's Syndrome**: Dynamic VA occlusion with head rotation, usually at C1/2

**Risk factors for dissection:**
- Connective tissue disorders (Ehlers-Danlos, Marfan's, fibromuscular dysplasia) — strongest risk factors
- Hypertension
- Recent infection (especially URTI — inflammatory arteriopathy)
- Oral contraceptive use
- Migraine with aura
- Prior dissection
- Recent trauma (whiplash, manipulation, trivial neck strain)

**Incidence:**
- Spontaneous VAD: ~1–1.5 per 100,000/year
- ICAD: ~2.6 per 100,000/year
- The incidence of a vascular event attributable to cervical manipulation cannot be stated reliably from the cited observational evidence; consultation bias and a possible prodromal dissection are important limitations.
- VAD accounts for ~20% of strokes in patients aged <45 years (Dziewas et al., 2003)

---

## Special Tests

**Critical caveat: Pre-manipulative screening tests for CAD have poor diagnostic accuracy and should NOT be used as a sole safety screen. Clinical decision-making must be based on comprehensive history and risk assessment.**

| Test | Role | Safety limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Vertebral Artery Test / DeKleyn's / Hautant's | Historical positional tests | Do not use to clear a patient for cervical treatment |
| Hallpike-Dix Test | BPPV assessment | Not a cervical-artery test |
| Sustained end-range rotation | Historical positional provocation | Not recommended as a vascular safety screen |
| Rancourt's Test | Historical positional test | No established role in vascular clearance |

**International IFOMPT Cervical Framework (accepted October 2020):**
- Pre-manipulative testing for VBI (VAT, Hautant's, DeKleyn's) **should not be used** as a means of clearing patients for cervical manipulation — insufficient sensitivity/specificity
- Comprehensive **clinical history and risk factor analysis** is the cornerstone of pre-treatment screening

**What TO use instead — IFOMPT Framework:**

**Historical symptom mnemonic (5 D's + 3 N's) and additional features:**

> This mnemonic is not a diagnostic rule and is not a substitute for the 2020 IFOMPT framework's patient-specific history, risk assessment and neurological examination.

| Symptom | Clinical Significance |
|---------|-----------------------|
| **Dizziness** | Vertebrobasilar ischaemia; differentiate from cervicogenic (positional, brief, associated with neck movement) |
| **Diplopia** | Brainstem ischaemia (CN III, IV, VI involvement) |
| **Dysarthria** | Bulbar ischaemia |
| **Dysphagia** | Medullary involvement |
| **Drop attacks** | Sudden loss of postural tone — vertebrobasilar |
| **Nausea** | Dorsal vagal complex ischaemia |
| **Numbness** (face/body) | Lateral medullary/trigeminal involvement |
| **Nystagmus** | Vestibulocerebellum ischaemia |
| **Ataxia** | Cerebellar ischaemia |
| **Headache** (new, severe, "thunderclap") | Subarachnoid haemorrhage or arterial dissection |
| **Horner's Syndrome** (ptosis, miosis, anhidrosis) | ICA or VA dissection |
| **Neck pain** (new, severe, ipsilateral occipital) | Dissection prodrome |

---

## Red Flags

Use the International IFOMPT Cervical Framework (2020) to reason about possible vascular pathology. Concerning presentations include new severe or unusual neck pain or headache together with focal neurological features, ataxia, new cranial nerve dysfunction, Horner syndrome or other acute stroke features.

- If acute cervical artery dissection or stroke is suspected, stop cervical treatment and arrange emergency medical assessment.
- Call 999 for acute focal neurological or stroke features. Do not delay emergency care to complete a musculoskeletal examination.
- Do not use symptom provocation in sustained rotation or extension, or the absence of the historical “5Ds/3Ns”, as vascular clearance.
- Do not proceed with cervical manipulation when vascular pathology, instability, myelopathy, fracture or another serious contraindication is suspected.

The “5Ds/3Ns” may help explain historical teaching but they are not a diagnostic or clearance rule.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### International IFOMPT Cervical Framework (2020)
The *International Framework for Examination of the Cervical Region for potential of vascular pathologies of the neck prior to Orthopaedic Manual Therapy Intervention*, accepted by IFOMPT in October 2020, replaces the earlier CAD terminology and removes provocative positional testing from the framework.

**Three-stage clinical decision process:**

1. **Stage 1 — Pre-examination screen:**
   - Comprehensive history of dizziness, headache, neurological symptoms
   - CVD risk factors, connective tissue disorders, medications (anticoagulants)
   - Recent trauma

2. **Stage 2 — Physical examination:**
   - Select examination procedures from the history and evolving clinical hypothesis
   - Perform an appropriate neurological examination and other indicated tests
   - Do not use provocative positional testing to infer vascular safety; its predictive ability is lacking and the 2020 framework does not recommend it

3. **Stage 3 — Decision:**
   - If ANY concern → do NOT perform cervical HVLA; consider alternative techniques
   - Document reasoning
   - Share decision-making with patient (informed consent including CAD risk)

### AHA/ASA Scientific Statement (Biller et al., 2014)
- Clinicians should inform patients of the extremely small risk of stroke associated with cervical manipulation
- Shared decision-making and informed consent required
- Prefer cervical mobilisation over HVLA where risk factors present

### Differential Vertebrogenic Dizziness Framework
Distinguish cervicogenic dizziness from vestibular causes:

| Feature | Cervicogenic Dizziness | Vestibular (BPPV/Labyrinthitis) | VBI |
|---------|----------------------|--------------------------------|-----|
| Onset | With neck movement | Head position change | Sustained rotation/extension |
| Duration | Proportional to neck movement | Seconds (BPPV) or days (labyrinthitis) | Seconds–minutes |
| Associated | Neck pain, stiffness | Nausea, positional (Hallpike positive) | Diplopia, dysarthria, ataxia |
| Neurological | None | None | Positive CNS signs |

---

## Outcome Measures

**Primary goal: SAFETY — accurate identification and appropriate management of CAD risk**

Formal validated outcome measures for the management of CAD are limited — this is primarily a triage/screening condition rather than a primary physiotherapy treatment condition.

**Tools used in assessment:**

### Dizziness Handicap Inventory (DHI)
- 25 items; 0–100 (0=no handicap, 100=maximum)
- Used for dizziness-related disability (differentiate from cervicogenic vs. vestibular)
- **MCID**: 11 points (Whitney et al., 2004)

### Neck Disability Index (NDI)
- For associated neck pain disability tracking
- **MCID**: 7.5/50 points

### NPRS (0–10)
- For headache and neck pain severity
- **MCID**: 2.1 points

### Neurological Examination Documentation
- Serial documentation of cranial nerve findings, upper and lower limb neurology
- Essential for monitoring improvement or deterioration

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

**Investigation of Suspected CAD:**

**MRI + MRA (Magnetic Resonance Angiography):**
- May identify arterial-wall and luminal features of dissection; modality choice is an acute medical/radiology decision rather than a universal first-line rule
- T1 fat-saturated axial images: intramural haematoma appears as crescentic hyperintensity surrounding lumen
- MRA: demonstrates stenosis, occlusion, or "flame sign" of dissection

**CTA (CT Angiography):**
- Faster than MRI; better availability in ED
- Provides rapid high-resolution assessment of the arterial lumen; limitations include ionising radiation, iodinated contrast and possible false-positive or false-negative appearances

**Doppler Ultrasound:**
- Limited sensitivity for VA dissection (poor vessel visualisation at skull base)
- Useful for ICA stenosis screening (carotid atheromatous disease)
- Cannot exclude cervical artery dissection

**Conventional DSA (Digital Subtraction Angiography):**
- Historically used as a reference luminal technique; current use is specialist-led because it is invasive and provides limited arterial-wall information

**Clinical criteria for diagnosis (Brandt criteria for VAD):**
1. Local pain (neck/occipital/periorbital)
2. New neurological deficit
3. Imaging evidence of dissection

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Key Distinguishing Features |
|-----------|----------------------------|
| BPPV | Positive Hallpike-Dix, brief (<60 sec) rotational vertigo, no brainstem signs |
| Vestibular neuritis / Labyrinthitis | Sustained vertigo, hearing loss (labyrinthitis), no neck pain, normal MRI |
| Cervicogenic dizziness | Neck pain + dizziness proportional to cervical ROM, positive FRT, no brainstem signs |
| Subarachnoid haemorrhage | Thunderclap headache, worst of life, neck stiffness, photophobia, CT/LP |
| Posterior fossa tumour | Progressive headache, ataxia, cranial nerve deficit, MRI |
| Migraine with aura | Headache + aura (visual, sensory), family history, no focal deficit, recurrent |
| Multiple sclerosis | Relapsing-remitting, MRI white matter lesions, young adult |
| Brainstem TIA | Transient (<24h), multiple deficits, DWI-MRI, cardiac source |
| Mechanical neck pain | No neurological signs, ROM limited but symptoms peripheral, predictable |
| Orthostatic hypotension | Dizziness with positional change (lying → standing), HR/BP response |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Acute Safety Pathway

**Suspected acute cervical artery dissection or stroke**

- Stop cervical treatment and arrange emergency medical assessment.
- Call 999 for acute focal neurological or stroke features.
- Emergency destination, investigation and treatment are determined by the emergency stroke/vascular pathway.

**Confirmed dissection**

Antithrombotic choice, duration, imaging and any endovascular intervention are specialist medical decisions. Physiotherapy may contribute to subsequent neurological rehabilitation. Later cervical treatment requires individual neurovascular review and shared planning; this page does not prescribe a fixed interval or technique.

---

### Conservative Management

**For VBI from atherosclerosis (stable, not acute dissection):**
- Medical management (antiplatelet, statin, BP management) is primary
- Physiotherapy: avoid provocative cervical positions; treat associated neck pain with GENTLE Grade I–II mobilisation
- Vestibular rehabilitation if chronic vertiginous symptoms (with medical clearance)

**For cervicogenic dizziness (not VBI):**
- Manual therapy to C1/2 (IFOMPT-compliant, non-HVLA technique)
- Proprioceptive retraining, gaze stability exercises
- Cervical flexion-rotation test (FRT) to confirm C1/2 involvement (Hall et al., 2010; Sens. 91%, Spec. 90%)

**Post-dissection rehabilitation (neurology/physio liaison):**
- Gradual return of cervical mobility (pain-free range)
- Cervical intervention and return to activity require individual neurovascular clearance; no universal imaging or time threshold is asserted here
- Core, scapular, and peripheral strengthening safe from early stage
- Address psychological impact (anxiety, PTSD) — dissection can be traumatic event

### Pharmacological (Medical Team-Led)

- Drug choice, dose and duration are determined by the stroke/neurovascular team using the individual diagnosis, imaging, ischaemic or haemorrhagic risk and comorbidities. This physiotherapy reference intentionally does not prescribe an antiplatelet, anticoagulant, statin, antihypertensive or analgesic regimen.
- NICE NG128 (Stroke): comprehensive stroke/TIA management guidance

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency:** suspected acute dissection or stroke, especially with acute focal neurological features, new cranial nerve dysfunction, ataxia or Horner syndrome with concerning head/neck pain.
- **Prompt medical assessment:** a concerning vascular presentation without acute stroke features, with urgency determined by the clinical presentation.
- **Specialist follow-up:** confirmed dissection, chronic vascular symptoms or post-stroke rehabilitation needs.

Do not use positional testing or a negative historical symptom mnemonic to reduce concern when the history or examination suggests possible cervical vascular pathology.

---

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Cervical myelopathy (CM) — more precisely **degenerative cervical myelopathy (DCM)** — is the most common cause of spinal cord dysfunction in adults over 50 years, with an estimated prevalence of 60 per 100,000 in North America/Europe (Nouri et al., 2015). It is the leading cause of acquired spinal cord disability in adults worldwide.

**Pathophysiology:**
DCM results from progressive narrowing of the cervical spinal canal leading to spinal cord compression and ischaemia:
- **Static compression**: osteophytes, hypertrophied ligamentum flavum (especially in extension), ossification of posterior longitudinal ligament (OPLL), degenerative disc bulges, facet joint hypertrophy
- **Dynamic compression**: cord compression exacerbated by flexion/extension movement; cord is stretched over ventral osteophytes in flexion and folded by buckled ligamentum flavum in extension
- **Vascular compromise**: compression of anterior spinal artery and intrinsic cord microvasculature → ischaemia → central grey matter necrosis
- **Congenital canal narrowing**: canal diameter <13 mm (critical stenosis <10 mm) → Torg-Pavlov ratio; predisposes to earlier/more severe myelopathy
- Spinal cord injury pattern: central cord syndrome most common pattern

**Canal dimensions:**
- Normal C3–7 canal diameter: 17–18 mm
- Relative stenosis: <13 mm
- Absolute stenosis: <10 mm
- Torg-Pavlov ratio (canal diameter / vertebral body AP diameter): <0.8 = stenosis

**Clinical presentation:**
- Insidious onset; often subclinical initially
- **Upper extremities**: clumsy hands, grip weakness, difficulty with fine motor tasks (buttons, writing, chopsticks — "myelopathy hand"), wasting of intrinsic hand muscles
- **Lower extremities**: spastic gait (wide-based, shuffling), lower limb weakness, falls
- **Bladder**: urgency, frequency, hesitancy, incomplete emptying
- **Sensory**: burning dysaesthesia in hands, loss of proprioception, L'hermitte's sign (electric shock down spine with neck flexion)
- Natural history: variable — stepwise deterioration most common (~36%), gradual decline (~20%), stable with episodes of deterioration (~40%)

---

## Special Tests

| Test | Sensitivity | Specificity | +LR | −LR | Evidence Level |
|------|-------------|-------------|-----|-----|----------------|
| Hoffman's Sign | 0.58 | 0.78 | 2.6 | 0.54 | High (Cook et al., 2009) |
| Inverted Supinator Sign | 0.62 | 0.73 | 2.3 | 0.52 | Moderate |
| Babinski Sign | 0.13 | 0.99 | 13.0 | 0.88 | High (pathognomonic if positive) |
| Hyperreflexia (lower limb) | 0.80 | 0.73 | 3.0 | 0.27 | Moderate |
| Gait ataxia (clinical observation) | 0.46 | 0.92 | 5.8 | 0.59 | Moderate (Cook et al., 2009) |
| Romberg test (positive) | 0.38 | 0.88 | 3.2 | 0.70 | Moderate |
| Grip and Release Test (≥20 cycles/10s abnormal) | 0.70 | 0.68 | 2.2 | 0.44 | Moderate (Yukawa et al., 2012) |
| 10-second Step Test (finger walk test) | 0.77 | 0.74 | 3.0 | 0.31 | Moderate |
| Lhermitte's Sign | 0.27 | 0.96 | 6.8 | 0.76 | Moderate |
| Clonus (ankle) | 0.18 | 0.97 | 6.0 | 0.85 | Moderate |
| Multivariable examination cluster (3+ UMN signs) | 0.94 | 0.57 | 2.2 | 0.10 | High (Cook et al., 2009) |

**Cook et al. (2009) Cluster for DCM:**
- ≥2 of 5 signs: Hoffman's, Inverted supinator, Babinski, Age >45, gait deviation
- This cluster may raise concern but is not a standalone diagnostic or referral rule; interpret it with the neurological examination and specialist assessment

**Key examination findings:**

- **Hoffman's Sign**: flick middle finger distal phalanx — positive if IP flexion of thumb/index; indicates UMN dysfunction
- **Finger Escape Sign**: patient holds fingers extended, adducted — ulnar 2–3 fingers spontaneously abduct/flex; positive in DCM
- **10-Second Step Test**: patient taps each foot alternately ≥20 times in 10 seconds — <18 taps considered abnormal (Bohlman 1977)
- **Grip and Release Test**: open and close fist as fast as possible for 10 seconds — normal ≥20 cycles; <20 suggests hand dysfunction (Yukawa et al., 2012)
- **Tandem Gait Test**: inability to tandem walk (heel-to-toe) 2–3 steps; sensitive for gait ataxia in early myelopathy
- **Modified Japanese Orthopaedic Association Scale (mJOA)**: functional severity scoring used to guide surgical timing

---

## Red Flags

Suspected degenerative cervical myelopathy (DCM) requires prompt medical or spinal assessment. Warning features include worsening hand function, gait disturbance, upper motor neuron findings, progressive limb weakness and new bladder or bowel disturbance.

- Arrange emergency medical assessment for acute neurological deterioration, acute cord-compression features or neurological deficit after trauma.
- Do not apply cervical manipulation or aggressive mobilisation where DCM or cord compression is suspected.
- Do not delay assessment when neurological function is deteriorating.

DCM severity and rate of change determine management. Mild DCM is not automatically an emergency and may, following specialist assessment and shared decision-making, be managed with a supervised structured rehabilitation trial. Moderate/severe DCM and deterioration during non-operative care require surgical assessment under the AO Spine guideline.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Severity Classification (mJOA Scale)
The modified Japanese Orthopaedic Association (mJOA) scale is the most widely used tool for DCM severity:

| mJOA Score | Severity |
|------------|----------|
| 18 | Normal |
| 15–17 | Mild |
| 12–14 | Moderate |
| <12 | Severe |

**mJOA domains:** Upper extremity motor function (0–5), Lower extremity motor function (0–7), Sensory (0–3), Sphincter function (0–3)

### Maitland (with significant caveats)
- Maitland mobilisation is NOT appropriate as primary treatment for confirmed DCM
- Grade I–II soft tissue work to peripheral joints (shoulder, elbow) may be used if confirmed nerve root + cord involvement coexist — only under specialist physiotherapy with neurosurgical knowledge
- Post-surgical rehabilitation: Maitland principles applicable once surgical clearance obtained

### Validated DCM severity measures
Use the mJOA severity bands above for the AO Spine/Global Spine Journal guideline. The separate 0–5 scale is the **Nurick walking grade**, described under Outcome Measures; it must not be labelled as an AO Spine DCM grading system.

### Physiotherapy Role Post-Operatively (ACDF/Laminoplasty/Laminectomy)
- 80% of patients improve post-decompression surgery (Fehlings et al., 2013)
- Post-op physiotherapy: gentle ROM, neurological recovery monitoring, functional gait retraining, ADL rehabilitation
- Inpatient neuro-physio → community follow-up pathway

---

## Outcome Measures

### Modified Japanese Orthopaedic Association Scale (mJOA)
- 18-point scale; **MCID**: 2.0 points (Tetreault et al., 2015)
- Most validated for surgical outcome measurement in DCM
- Recovery rate = [(post-op mJOA − pre-op mJOA) / (18 − pre-op mJOA)] × 100

### Neck Disability Index (NDI)
- **MCID**: 7.5/50 points (15%)
- Useful for tracking functional disability alongside neurological severity

### Nurick Grade
- 0–5 functional walking classification; less sensitive to small changes
- Grade 0: No root/cord signs; Grade 5: Chair-bound or bedridden

### European Myelopathy Score (EMS)
- 5-domain, 5–18 scale; validated European alternative to mJOA
- **MCID**: approximately 2 points

### Patient-Specific Functional Scale (PSFS)
- **MCID**: 2.0 points average
- Appropriate for post-surgical rehabilitation goal-setting

### GROC (Global Rating of Change)
- 15-point scale; use at post-op physiotherapy review points

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

**MRI (principal structural investigation):**
MRI is the principal imaging modality for suspected DCM because diagnosis requires clinical–radiological correlation.
- T2 hyperintensity in spinal cord at level of compression = **poor prognostic marker** (indicates myelomalacia)
- T1 hypointensity = cavitation/necrosis — even worse prognosis
- STIR sequences for inflammatory/infective pathology
- Suspected DCM requires prompt medical assessment and appropriate imaging under the relevant specialist pathway

**Alternative imaging:**
- If MRI cannot be performed or does not answer the clinical question, alternative imaging such as CT ± myelography may be considered by the specialist/radiology pathway.
- The choice depends on the clinical question; it is not an automatic routine second-line step.

**Electrodiagnostics:**
- Motor Evoked Potentials (MEPs) and Somatosensory Evoked Potentials (SSEPs) may contribute to specialist assessment of cord conduction
- EMG/NCS to differentiate coexisting radiculopathy

**Clinical Diagnosis:**
- No single pathognomonic test; diagnosis requires combination of:
  1. Consistent symptom profile
  2. ≥2 UMN signs on examination
  3. MRI evidence of cord compression ± signal change
- Cook et al.'s multivariable cluster and the individual test-accuracy estimates require human verification against the original study before publication; they do not replace imaging and specialist assessment

**AO Spine / AOSpine North America Clinical Practice Guidelines (Fehlings et al., 2017):**
- Recommend surgery for moderate-severe DCM (mJOA ≤14)
- For mild DCM (mJOA 15–17): structured rehabilitation trial with watchful waiting; surgery if deterioration

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Key Distinguishing Features |
|-----------|----------------------------|
| Multiple sclerosis | Age <40, relapsing-remitting course, MRI white matter lesions, oligoclonal bands |
| Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS/MND) | UMN + LMN mixed, bulbar involvement, no sensory deficit, EMG fasciculations |
| Subacute combined degeneration (B12 deficiency) | Posterior column signs, B12 low, macrocytic anaemia, no bony compression on MRI |
| Normal pressure hydrocephalus | Triad: gait apraxia, cognitive decline, urinary incontinence; no cervical compression |
| Syringomyelia | Cape distribution sensory loss, dissociated sensory deficit, often young adults |
| Peripheral neuropathy | No UMN signs, glove-stocking distribution, NCS abnormal |
| Cervical radiculopathy | Unilateral radicular symptoms, no UMN signs, no gait disturbance |
| Parkinson's disease | Tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia, pill-rolling tremor; no cord compression |
| Hereditary spastic paraplegia | Family history, gradual onset, lower extremity predominant, genetic testing |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

- Suspected DCM: arrange prompt spinal/specialist assessment through the current local pathway.
- Acute neurological deterioration or neurological deficit after trauma: use the emergency medical/spinal pathway.
- Moderate or severe DCM: the AO Spine guideline recommends surgery.
- Mild DCM: specialist-led shared decision-making may include surgery or a supervised structured rehabilitation trial. Deterioration during non-operative care requires operative assessment; lack of improvement prompts reconsideration.

Physiotherapy may support function, gait and hand use within the specialist plan. It must not delay escalation or include cervical manipulation where cord compromise is suspected.

---

### Conservative Management

**Physiotherapy for mild DCM (watchful waiting) — Grade C evidence:**
- **Cervical collar**: no fixed collar regimen is asserted here; any use requires specialist direction and an individual risk–benefit decision
- **Balance and proprioceptive retraining**: Standing balance, tandem stance, Wii Fit / perturbation training
- **Upper extremity fine motor retraining**: Occupational therapy-led ADL retraining
- **Strengthening**: Gentle scapular/shoulder stabilisation; avoid heavy loading through cervical spine
- **Gait and mobility:** where rehabilitation is appropriate, it should be individualised and may include gait/balance retraining and mobility aids according to functional need; orthotic assessment may be considered when clinically indicated.
- **Neurological monitoring**: Document serial mJOA, grip strength, 10-second step/grip-release test at each visit

**Post-surgical rehabilitation — Grade B evidence:**
- Most improvement in first 6 months post-decompression
- Targeted: grip/pinch strength, fine motor retraining, gait normalisation, return to ADLs
- Core stability and cervical muscle strengthening from 6–8 weeks post-fusion
- 80% of patients gain ≥2 mJOA points (Fehlings et al., 2013)

### Pharmacological

- **Neuropathic pain** (associated radiculopathy): prescribing is clinician-led; NICE CG173 may apply only after confirming a neuropathic-pain indication
- **NSAIDs**: Short-term for pain; avoid long-term in elderly given GI/CV/renal risk
- **Corticosteroids**: High-dose methylprednisolone for acute traumatic SCI; NOT recommended for degenerative CM (Fehlings AANS 2013)
- **Baclofen/Tizanidine**: For spasticity management in confirmed CM; specialist-initiated
- **Bladder management**: Anticholinergics (oxybutynin) for urgency if confirmed; refer urology

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency medical/spinal assessment:** acute deterioration, new bladder or bowel disturbance, or neurological deficit after trauma.
- **Prompt spinal assessment:** suspected DCM, progressive neurological change, worsening hand function, gait disturbance or upper motor neuron findings.
- **Surgical assessment:** moderate/severe DCM, or deterioration during supervised non-operative care.
- **Shared specialist decision:** mild DCM; a supervised structured rehabilitation trial may be considered, with reassessment if the person does not improve.

No fixed local route or universal conservative-treatment duration is asserted.

---

## Key References

1. Nouri A, Tetreault L, Singh A, Karadimas SK, Fehlings MG. Degenerative cervical myelopathy: epidemiology, genetics, and pathogenesis. *Spine*. 2015;40(12):E675–E693.

2. Cook CE, Wilhelm M, Cook AE, Petrosino C, Isaacs R. Clinical tests for screening and diagnosis of cervical spine myelopathy: a systematic review. *J Manipulative Physiol Ther*. 2011;34(8):539–546.

3. Fehlings MG, Tetreault LA, Riew KD, et al. A clinical practice guideline for the management of patients with degenerative cervical myelopathy: recommendations for patients with mild, moderate, and severe disease and nonmyelopathic patients with evidence of cord compression. *Global Spine J*. 2017;7(3 Suppl):70S–83S.

4. Fehlings MG, Wilson JR, Kopjar B, et al. Efficacy and safety of surgical decompression in patients with cervical spondylotic myelopathy: results of the AOSpine North America prospective multi-centre study. *J Bone Joint Surg Am*. 2013;95(18):1651–1658.

5. Tetreault L, Kopjar B, Côté P, Arnold P, Fehlings MG. A clinical prediction rule for functional outcomes in patients undergoing surgery for degenerative cervical myelopathy: analysis of an international prospective multicenter data set of 757 subjects. *J Bone Joint Surg Am*. 2015;97(24):2038–2046.

6. Yukawa Y, Kato F, Ito K, et al. Validity and reliability of the 10-second grip and release test for degenerative cervical myelopathy. *J Neurosurg Spine*. 2012;17(5):421–426.

7. Bohlman HH. Cervical spondylosis with moderate to severe myelopathy. *Spine*. 1977;2(2):151–162.

8. Karadimas SK, Erwin WM, Ely CG, Dettori JR, Fehlings MG. Pathophysiology and natural history of cervical spondylotic myelopathy. *Spine*. 2013;38(22 Suppl 1):S21–S36.

9. Fehlings MG, Tetreault LA, Riew KD, et al. A clinical practice guideline for the management of patients with degenerative cervical myelopathy. *Global Spine J*. 2017;7(3 Suppl):70S–83S. doi: [10.1177/2192568217701914](https://doi.org/10.1177/2192568217701914).

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Cervical radiculopathy (CR) is a clinical syndrome characterised by pain, neurological deficits, or both, caused by compression or irritation of a cervical nerve root. Annual incidence is approximately 83 per 100,000 population, with peak onset between 50–54 years of age (Radhakrishnan et al., 1994).

**Aetiology:**
- **Disc herniation** (~20–25%): acute onset, more common in younger patients (<45 years); posterolateral herniation impinges the exiting nerve root
- **Spondylosis/osteophytes** (~70–75%): degenerative foraminal stenosis, more common in older patients
- **Combined disc + spondylosis:** most frequent overall

**Most affected levels:**
- C6 (60%): affects C5–6 disc, compression of C6 root
- C7 (25–30%): C6–7 disc, C7 root
- C5, C8: less common

**Relevant anatomy:**
- Cervical nerve roots exit above their corresponding vertebra (C1 exits above C1, C8 exits below C7)
- Intervertebral foramina bounded anteriorly by the uncovertebral joint and disc, posteriorly by the facet joint — both sites of spondylotic change
- Dorsal root ganglion lies within or just outside the foramen — highly sensitive to mechanical and chemical irritation
- Nucleus pulposus chemical mediators (phospholipase A2, TNF-α, IL-6) contribute to radicular pain independent of mechanical compression

**Dermatomal/myotomal distribution:**

| Root | Dermatomal Pain | Weakness | Reflex |
|------|-----------------|----------|--------|
| C4 | Neck, upper trapezius | Shoulder shrug | — |
| C5 | Lateral arm to elbow | Deltoid, biceps | Biceps (C5/6) |
| C6 | Lateral forearm, thumb, index finger | Wrist extensors, biceps | Brachioradialis, biceps |
| C7 | Middle finger, dorsal forearm | Triceps, wrist flexors | Triceps |
| C8 | Medial forearm, ring/little finger | Finger flexors, intrinsics | — |

**Natural history:** Often favourable, with many stable presentations improving under conservative management. Progressive, disabling or uncontrolled symptoms require reassessment under the referral guidance below.

---

## Special Tests

| Test | Sensitivity | Specificity | +LR | −LR | Evidence Level |
|------|-------------|-------------|-----|-----|----------------|
| Spurling's (cervical compression/foraminal compression) | 0.50–0.60 | 0.86–0.97 | 3.5–6.1 | 0.47–0.54 | High (Rubinstein et al., 2007) |
| Shoulder Abduction Relief Sign (SARS) | 0.43–0.78 | 0.75–0.90 | 2.8 | 0.39 | Moderate (Wainner et al., 2003) |
| Neck Distraction Test | 0.40–0.44 | 0.90–0.97 | 4.0 | 0.60 | Moderate (Wainner et al., 2003) |
| Upper Limb Neurodynamic Test 1 (ULNT1/median) | 0.72–0.97 | 0.33–0.22 | 1.1–1.3 | 0.12 | Moderate (Wainner et al., 2003) |
| Valsalva Manoeuvre | 0.22 | 0.94 | 3.7 | 0.83 | Low |
| Cervical Rotation <60° to ipsilateral side | 0.48 | 0.69 | 1.6 | 0.75 | Low–Moderate |
| Neurological examination (motor deficit) | 0.61 | 0.89 | 5.5 | 0.44 | Moderate |
| Neurological examination (reflex deficit) | 0.71 | 0.80 | 3.6 | 0.36 | Moderate |
| Dermatomal sensory deficit | 0.67 | 0.75 | 2.7 | 0.44 | Moderate |

**Wainner et al. (2003) Clinical Prediction Rule for CR** (4-variable cluster):
Positive if ≥3 of 4:
1. Ipsilateral cervical rotation <60°
2. ULNT1 positive
3. Distraction test positive
4. Spurling's test positive

- The cluster can increase diagnostic confidence when the findings agree, but it is not a treatment-selection rule and should not replace neurological assessment or differential diagnosis

**Administration notes:**
- **Spurling's**: cervical lateral flexion + axial compression toward symptomatic side — positive if reproduces or peripheralises arm symptoms
- **SARS**: patient places ipsilateral hand on head — positive if arm/shoulder pain reduces
- **Neck Distraction**: examiner provides manual cervical traction (~10–15 kg) — positive if symptoms centralise or reduce
- **ULNT1**: shoulder depression, abduction 110°, forearm supination, wrist/finger extension, elbow extension, contralateral cervical lateral flexion — positive if reproduces concordant arm pain

---

## Red Flags

Screen separately for:

- **DCM/cord involvement:** gait disturbance, clumsy or weak hands or legs, brisk reflexes, extensor plantar responses, progressive neurological change, or new bladder/bowel disturbance. Arrange prompt specialist assessment; use emergency assessment for acute deterioration.
- **Trauma:** apply the Canadian C-Spine Rule in an appropriate trauma presentation. NICE NG41 recommends CT for adults when imaging is indicated by that rule.
- **Vascular pathology:** use the IFOMPT 2020 risk-based history and examination. Do not use positional testing or the historical “5Ds/3Ns” as clearance.
- **Other serious pathology:** suspected fracture, serious infection or malignancy requires medical assessment with urgency determined by the presentation.

Do not prescribe a specific imaging modality from a general red-flag label unless the relevant guideline supports it.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Maitland Concept
- Grade I–II oscillations for pain modulation in acute/irritable presentations
- Grade III–IV for resistance-dominant presentations in subacute/chronic
- Passive accessory intervertebral movements (PAIVMs) at C5/6, C6/7 to identify comparable signs
- Passive physiological intervertebral movements (PPIVMs) to assess segmental mobility
- Reassess neurological symptoms after each treatment — peripheralisation = contraindication to further technique

### McKenzie / MDT (Mechanical Diagnosis and Therapy)
- Repeated movement assessment: flexion/extension/lateral flexion/rotation in standing and supine
- Identify directional preference: centralisation of radicular symptoms with repeated extension suggests disc pathology with good prognosis
- Classification: Derangement (most common, 80–90%), Dysfunction, Postural
- Derangement: centralisation phenomenon guides treatment direction
- MDT retraction ± extension exercises as first-line for Derangement syndrome
- Centralisation as prognostic indicator: associated with better outcomes (Werneke & Hart, 2003)

### Clinical Prediction Rules
- Wainner CPR (2003): see Special Tests above — for diagnosis
- Fritz & Brennan (2007) CPR for manipulation benefit: ≥3 of 5 predictors associated with success
  1. Symptom duration <38 days
  2. Positive expectation of manipulation
  3. Side-to-side difference in cervical rotation >10°
  4. Pain with PA spring testing at C3–5
  5. Not performing sedentary work

---

## Outcome Measures

### Neck Disability Index (NDI)
- 10 items, 0–5 scale each, total 0–50 (or 0–100%)
- Scoring: 0–8% = no disability; 10–28% = mild; 30–48% = moderate; 50–64% = severe; ≥64% = complete
- **MCID**: 7.5/50 points (15%) for CR (Young et al., 2009)
- Reliability: ICC 0.89–0.93; Strong validity for neck pain populations

### Numeric Pain Rating Scale (NPRS)
- 0–10 NRS; **MCID**: 2.1 points for cervical radiculopathy (Cleland et al., 2007)
- Use for arm pain AND neck pain separately

### Patient-Specific Functional Scale (PSFS)
- 3–5 patient-identified activities, scored 0–10 each (0=unable, 10=normal)
- **MCID**: 2.0 points (per activity average) for neck pain (Cleland et al., 2006)
- Responsive to clinically important change in CR

### Global Rating of Change (GROC)
- 15-point scale (−7 to +7) or 7-point Likert
- +5 ("quite a bit better") or greater = successful outcome
- Use at 4 weeks and discharge

### Arm Pain NRS
- Separate from neck NRS — important to track radicular symptom change
- Reduction in arm pain more clinically significant than neck pain in CR

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

**Diagnostic investigations:** MRI and electrodiagnostic studies answer different questions and neither is a universal standalone gold standard. MRI evaluates structural causes; EMG/NCS may support localisation or identify a peripheral neuropathy when the diagnosis is uncertain.

**MRI:**
- Preferred imaging for suspected CR
- Imaging is not routine for stable uncomplicated symptoms; request it when the result is expected to change management or when serious/progressive pathology or a specialist procedure is being considered
- Do NOT routinely offer imaging in first 6 weeks of uncomplicated CR
- MRI has high false positive rate — degenerative changes common in asymptomatic adults (>50% over age 40 have disc bulges)

**Electrodiagnostics:**
- EMG/NCS may support localisation or identify peripheral entrapment when the diagnosis remains uncertain
- Findings may lag clinical presentation by 3–4 weeks

**Wainner test cluster:**
- May support diagnostic reasoning when the component findings agree; do not use it as a current validated treatment-selection rule

**IASP Diagnostic Criteria for CR:**
- Unilateral arm pain in a dermatomal distribution
- Corresponding sensory changes (hypoaesthesia/paraesthesia)
- Motor deficit or reflex change at corresponding level
- Positive provocative tests (Spurling's, distraction)
- Imaging correlation (but not required for clinical diagnosis)

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Key Distinguishing Features |
|-----------|----------------------------|
| Rotator cuff pathology | Local shoulder pain, painful arc, negative Spurling's, positive Hawkins/Neer |
| Thoracic outlet syndrome | Provoked by arm elevation, positive Roos test, neurogenic/vascular |
| Carpal tunnel syndrome | Nocturnal wrist/hand pain, thenar wasting, positive Phalen/Tinel at wrist |
| Cubital tunnel syndrome | Medial elbow + ring/little finger symptoms, positive elbow flexion test |
| Brachial neuritis (Parsonage-Turner) | Acute severe shoulder pain followed by rapid weakness, no spinal correlation |
| Peripheral neuropathy | Bilateral, glove distribution, diabetes history |
| Cervical myelopathy | Bilateral symptoms, UMN signs, gait disturbance, bladder/bowel dysfunction |
| Shoulder impingement | Shoulder-localised pain, arc sign positive, negative neurodynamic tests |
| Complex regional pain syndrome | Autonomic changes, disproportionate pain, allodynia |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Stable cervical radiculopathy can usually begin with conservative care. Under NICE NG127, symptoms stable for 6 weeks or more are not routinely referred unless pain is uncontrolled, symptoms are disabling, or specified neurological warning features are present.

Progressive motor or neurological deficit requires prompt reassessment and condition-appropriate specialist assessment. Atypical cord, vascular, trauma, infection or malignancy features follow their own pathways.

---

### Conservative Management

**Exercise Therapy (Grade A evidence):**
- Specific cervical and scapulothoracic strengthening reduces radicular symptoms
- Deep neck flexor strengthening (craniocervical flexion test biofeedback — CCFT protocol): Jull et al. (2009)
- Neural mobilisation (slider vs. tensioner techniques): Moderate evidence for symptom reduction
  - Median nerve slider: ULNT1-based; gentler, preferred in acute/irritable presentations
  - Radial nerve mobilisation for C6 root involvement
- McKenzie-based exercises: centralisation protocol (see Clinical Frameworks)
- Exercise alone equivalent to combined treatment at 12 months (Ylinen et al., 2003)

**Manual Therapy (Grade B evidence):**
- Cervical manipulation and mobilisation: Short-term pain relief; NCEPOD and NICE caution regarding serious adverse events — shared decision-making essential
- Thoracic spine manipulation: Level B evidence for short-term reduction of neck pain and disability in CR (Cleland et al., 2005)
- HVLA to thoracic spine considered lower risk than cervical HVLA
- Soft tissue techniques: Limited evidence as standalone; useful adjunct

**Education & Self-Management:**
- Pain neuroscience education (PNE): addresses catastrophisation and kinesiophobia
- Postural advice: ergonomic assessment (desk/screen setup), pillow selection
- Activity modification: avoid sustained cervical flexion, repetitive overhead loading
- Reassurance regarding favourable natural history

**Traction:**
- Mechanical/intermittent cervical traction: Moderate evidence for CR (Raney et al., 2009)
- CPR for traction (Raney et al.): ≥3 of 5 predictors:
  1. Age ≥55
  2. Positive shoulder abduction test
  3. Positive ULNT1
  4. Positive neck distraction test
  5. Peripheralisation with lower cervical PA spring testing

### Pharmacological (Medical/Prescriber-Led)

- **Analgesia:** Any medicine choice is prescriber-led and should follow the established pain mechanism, current prescribing guidance and individual risk assessment
- NICE **CG173** offers a choice of amitriptyline, duloxetine, gabapentin or pregabalin for confirmed neuropathic pain. It does not turn the dose ranges previously shown here into cervical-radiculopathy recommendations.
- Drug selection, dosing, oral corticosteroids, benzodiazepines and any injection are prescriber/specialist decisions using current prescribing guidance and patient-specific risks; this learner page does not prescribe them.

### Onward Referral Criteria

Follow NICE NG127 recommendation 1.10.11 for stable cervical radiculopathy of 6 weeks or more. Refer when pain is not controlled, symptoms are disabling, or there is a specified neurological feature such as gait disturbance, clumsy/weak hands or legs, brisk reflexes, extensor plantar responses, or new bladder/bowel disturbance.

- **Prompt specialist assessment:** progressive motor or neurological deficit, atypical presentation or suspected cord involvement.
- **Emergency assessment:** acute neurological deterioration or an applicable trauma/vascular emergency.

This Version 1 page does not prescribe generic pain-clinic, injection, epidural, spinal-cord-stimulation or fixed 6–12-week surgical pathways.

---

## Key References

1. Radhakrishnan K, Litchy WJ, O'Fallon WM, Kurland LT. Epidemiology of cervical radiculopathy: a population-based study from Rochester, Minnesota, 1976 through 1990. *Brain*. 1994;117(Pt 2):325–335.

2. Wainner RS, Fritz JM, Irrgang JJ, Boninger ML, Delitto A, Allison S. Reliability and diagnostic accuracy of the clinical examination and patient self-report measures for cervical radiculopathy. *Spine*. 2003;28(1):52–62.

3. Rubinstein SM, Pool JJ, van Tulder MW, Riphagen II, de Vet HC. A systematic review of the diagnostic accuracy of provocative tests of the neck for diagnosing cervical radiculopathy. *Eur Spine J*. 2007;16(3):307–319.

4. Spurling RG, Scoville WB. Lateral rupture of the cervical intervertebral discs. *Surg Gynecol Obstet*. 1944;78:350–358.

5. Werneke M, Hart DL. Centralisation phenomenon as a prognostic factor for chronic low back pain and disability. *Spine*. 2001;26(7):758–765.

6. Jull GA, Falla D, Vicenzino B, Hodges PW. The effect of therapeutic exercise on activation of the deep cervical flexor muscles in people with chronic neck pain. *Man Ther*. 2009;14(6):696–701.

7. Cleland JA, Childs JD, McRae M, Palmer JA, Stowell T. Immediate effects of thoracic manipulation in patients with neck pain: a randomized clinical trial. *Man Ther*. 2005;10(2):127–135.

8. Raney NH, Petersen EJ, Smith TA, et al. Development of a clinical prediction rule to identify patients with neck pain likely to benefit from cervical traction and exercise. *Eur Spine J*. 2009;18(3):382–391.

9. Young IA, Cleland JA, Michener LA, Brown C. Reliability, construct validity, and responsiveness of the neck disability index, patient-specific functional scale, and numeric pain rating scale in patients with cervical radiculopathy. *Am J Phys Med Rehabil*. 2009;88(10):831–839.

10. NICE. Neck pain — cervical radiculopathy. Clinical Knowledge Summary. 2023. Available at: cks.nice.org.uk/topics/neck-pain-cervical-radiculopathy/

11. NICE. *Neuropathic pain in adults: pharmacological management in non-specialist settings*. CG173. Updated 2020. [Recommendations](https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg173/chapter/Recommendations).

12. NICE. *Suspected neurological conditions: recognition and referral*. NG127, recommendations 1.10.11–1.10.12. [Recommendations for adults](https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng127/chapter/Recommendations-for-adults-aged-over-16#cervical-or-lumbar-radiculopathy).

12. Persson LC, Carlsson CA, Carlsson JY. Long-lasting cervical radicular pain managed with surgery, physiotherapy, or a cervical collar. *Spine*. 1997;22(7):751–758.

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
import { ClinicalNote, RedFlag, EvidenceBadge, ReferralBox } from "@/components/clinical"

<EvidenceBadge level="moderate" />

> **Clinical Summary:** Cervicogenic headache (CGH) is a secondary headache attributed to a disorder or lesion of the cervical spine or neck soft tissues. Under ICHD-3, imaging alone is not firm evidence of causation, distinguishing features are not unique, cervical myofascial pain may overlap with tension-type headache, and migrainous features may occur.

---

## Overview & Pathophysiology

### Mechanism

CGH arises from nociceptive input from the upper cervical spine structures (joints, muscles, ligaments, dura mater) that converges with trigeminal afferents within the **trigeminocervical nucleus (TCN)** in the brainstem.

The TCN extends caudally from the trigeminal nucleus caudalis to approximately C3–C4. Afferents from:
- **C1 (suboccipital / atlantoaxial)** — via the dorsal root of C1 (where present) and the spino-trigeminal tract
- **C2 (atlantoaxial, C2-3 zygapophyseal joints, C2 dorsal root ganglion)**
- **C3 (C2-3 and C3-4 zygapophyseal joints, third occipital nerve)**

...all converge with trigeminal afferents in the TCN. This anatomical convergence creates referred pain to the head and face from cervical structures — **central sensitisation** at the TCN level underpins the perceived headache.

**Primary pain generators** include:
- C1-2 and C2-3 zygapophyseal (facet) joints
- Atlantoaxial and atlanto-occipital joints
- Suboccipital muscles (rectus capitis posterior minor/major, obliquus capitis inferior)
- C2-3 intervertebral disc
- Upper cervical dura mater
- Third occipital nerve (branch of C3 dorsal ramus)

### Prevalence

- **Population prevalence:** 0.4–4% (general population); up to 17–20% in chronic headache clinic populations
- **Post-whiplash:** up to 53% of whiplash patients develop CGH (Bogduk & Govind, 2009)
- **Gender:** female predominance (F:M ≈ 4:1)
- **Age:** peak 30–44 years; increases with age
- **Third occipital headache** (C2-3 facet-mediated) accounts for ~27% of chronic post-whiplash headache (Lord et al, 1994)

### Typical Clinical Presentation

- Unilateral headache without side-shift (though bilateral variants exist)
- Starts in the neck/suboccipital region, radiates to frontotemporal region, orbit, or vertex
- Provoked or aggravated by neck movement or sustained neck posture
- Reduced cervical range of motion (particularly rotation)
- Ipsilateral shoulder/arm symptoms may coexist
- Moderate, non-pulsating quality; not typically severe
- Duration: hours to days (episodic) or continuous with fluctuation (chronic)

---

## Special Tests

<ClinicalNote>
CFRT and upper-cervical examination can contribute to the assessment, but published performance estimates vary with the population and reference standard. Neither is definitive in isolation; apply the ICHD-3 criteria and a full headache and neck assessment.
</ClinicalNote>

| Assessment component | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| **Cervical Flexion-Rotation Test (CFRT)** | Assesses upper-cervical movement restriction and familiar symptom reproduction | Not a standalone diagnosis |
| **Upper-cervical segmental assessment** | Assesses familiar pain reproduction and movement findings | Examiner-dependent and not definitive |

### Cervical Flexion-Rotation Test (CFRT) — Detail

**Procedure:**
1. Patient supine; examiner passively flexes cervix to full end-range (locks sub-C2 segments)
2. Examiner rotates the head passively left and right whilst maintaining full flexion
3. Normal: ≥44° rotation bilaterally; mean normal ~45°
4. **Positive:** <32° rotation to symptomatic side, OR ≥10° side-to-side asymmetry, AND reproduction of familiar headache symptoms

**Interpretation:** Range restriction together with reproduction of familiar headache may increase diagnostic confidence, but the finding remains one part of the full assessment.

### Upper Cervical Segmental Assessment

Passive intervertebral motion (PIVM) and passive accessory intervertebral motion (PAIVM) of C0-C3 assessed in prone or supine:
- Assess for **hypomobility, pain provocation, and reproduction of familiar headache**
- Unilateral posterior-anterior (UPA) pressure over C1-C2-C3 articular pillars
- Positive: reproduction of headache ± referred pain pattern

---

## Red Flags

Evaluate headache presentations for current NICE CG150 secondary-headache warning features, including:

- worsening headache with fever;
- sudden-onset headache reaching maximum intensity within 5 minutes;
- new neurological deficit, cognitive dysfunction, altered consciousness or substantial change in headache characteristics;
- recent head trauma;
- headache triggered by cough, Valsalva or exercise, or an orthostatic headache;
- features suggesting giant cell arteritis or acute glaucoma.

Urgency depends on the actual feature; not every red flag has the same pathway. Suspected meningitis, stroke, arterial dissection or another acute neurological emergency requires emergency medical assessment.

Before upper-cervical manual therapy, use the IFOMPT 2020 risk-based history and examination. Do not document a negative “5Ds/3Ns” checklist or sustained positional test as vascular clearance.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### 1. Cervicogenic Headache International Study Group (CHISG) Criteria — Sjaastad et al, 1998

Major criteria (one or more required):

**Group I — Symptoms/signs of neck involvement:**
- Ia: Precipitation of head pain by neck movement or sustained neck posture
- Ib: Precipitation by external pressure over the upper cervical or occipital region
- Ic: Unilateral head pain without side-shift
- Id: Ipsilateral neck, shoulder, and arm pain of non-radicular character

**Group II — Confirmatory evidence:**
- IIa: Diagnostic blockade of cervical structures or their nerve supply abolishes pain
  *(One method of demonstrating causation within the ICHD-3 criteria)*

**Group III — Other characteristics:**
- IIIa: Moderate-severe, non-excruciating, non-throbbing, non-lancinating pain
- IIIb: Headache starts in the neck
- IIIc: Episodes of varying duration, or fluctuating continuous pain

**Exclusion:** Migraine features (aura, severe throbbing, nausea/vomiting, photophobia) do not exclude CGH but should prompt differential diagnosis consideration.

---

### 2. ICHD-3 Criteria for Cervicogenic Headache (11.2.1)

*(International Classification of Headache Disorders, 3rd Edition, 2018)*

**A.** Any headache fulfilling criterion C
**B.** Clinical, laboratory, and/or imaging evidence of a disorder or lesion within the cervical spine or soft tissues of the neck known to be able to cause headache
**C.** Evidence of causation demonstrated by **at least two** of the following:
- 1. Headache has developed in temporal relation to onset of cervical disorder or lesion
- 2. Headache has significantly improved or remitted in parallel with improvement or resolution of cervical disorder
- 3. Cervical ROM is reduced, and headache is significantly worsened by provocative movements
- 4. Headache is abolished following diagnostic blockade of a cervical structure or nerve supply

**D.** Not better accounted for by another ICHD-3 diagnosis

**ICHD-3 caveats:** upper-cervical imaging findings are common in people without headache and are suggestive rather than firm evidence of causation. Side-locked pain, neck-provoked pain and posterior-to-anterior radiation may help differentiation but are not unique or proof of causation. Migrainous features can occur, and cervical myofascial pain may overlap with tension-type headache.

---

### 3. Jull's Assessment Approach (Jull, 1997; Jull et al, 2007)

A structured physiotherapy assessment framework:

**Phase 1 — Subjective assessment:**
- Headache characteristics (frequency, intensity, duration, location, associated symptoms)
- Aggravating/easing factors — identify cervical mechanical contribution
- Headache diary (minimum 4-week baseline)
- Medication use and medication overuse screening
- Psychosocial screening (PHQ-4, pain catastrophising scale)

**Phase 2 — Physical examination:**
- Active and passive cervical ROM (CROM device preferred)
- CFRT (detailed above)
- Upper cervical segmental examination (C0-C3 PAIVM/PIVM)
- Cervicothoracic junction examination (C4-T4)
- Craniocervical flexion test (CCF test) — deep cervical flexor assessment
- Postural assessment — forward head posture, thoracic kyphosis
- Neurological screening

**Craniocervical Flexion Test (CCFT):**
- Assesses neuromuscular control of deep cervical flexors (longus colli, longus capitis)
- Using pressure biofeedback unit (Stabilizer™): baseline 20 mmHg; progressive target 22→30 mmHg
- CGH patients typically show: reduced activation at lower pressure levels, increased activity of superficial flexors (SCM, anterior scalene) as compensation
- Target: achieve and hold 22–26 mmHg without superficial muscle substitution (Jull et al, 2008)

---

## Outcome Measures

| Measure | Domain | MCID | Frequency | Notes |
|---------|--------|------|-----------|-------|
| **Headache Frequency** (days/month) | Primary | Track an individual, agreed meaningful change | Headache diary | Core monitoring measure |
| **NPRS (0–10 Numeric Pain Rating Scale)** | Pain intensity | 1.5–2.0 points | Per episode or average weekly | Validated in headache populations |
| **HDI — Headache Disability Inventory** | Disability/function | 29 points (total, 0–100) | Baseline, 6 weeks, 3 months | Emotional + functional subscales; Jacobson et al, 1994 |
| **NDI — Neck Disability Index** | Neck-related disability | 7.5 points (0–50 scale) | Baseline, discharge | 10 items; Vernon & Mior, 1991 |
| **Headache Duration** (hours/episode) | Primary | No established MCID — track trend | Headache diary | Document alongside frequency |
| **Medication use** (days/month) | Screening context | NICE CG150 uses ≥10 days/month for triptans, opioids, ergots or combination analgesics for ≥3 months as a threshold to suspect medication-overuse headache | Review in the clinical context | This is not a taper target or treatment rule |
| **PSFS — Patient-Specific Functional Scale** | Functional goals | 2 points (0–10) | Baseline, discharge | 3 patient-defined activities |
| **Global Rating of Change (GRC)** | Overall change | +3 (on −7 to +7 scale) | 6 weeks, discharge | Patient-perceived change |
| **Tampa Scale of Kinesiophobia (TSK-11)** | Fear-avoidance | 6 points | Baseline; if >33/44, address | Relevant in chronic CGH |

### Headache Diary

Minimum **4-week prospective diary** recommended at baseline:
- Record: date, time of onset/offset, pain intensity (0–10), location, medication taken, possible triggers
- Digital options: Migraine Buddy, N1-Headache (validated electronic diary)
- Required to establish baseline frequency and identify patterns before initiating treatment

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### Cervical Flexion-Rotation Test as One Component of Assessment

Published studies use different populations and reference standards. Use CFRT findings to inform, not determine, the diagnosis; consider both movement restriction and familiar symptom reproduction alongside the ICHD-3 criteria.

### Cervical Joint Blocks as Reference Standard

Anaesthetic blockade of cervical structures is one specialist method of demonstrating causation under the ICHD-3 criteria:
- Third occipital nerve (TON) block — for C2-3 facet-mediated headache
- C1-2 and C2-3 medial branch blocks
- The specialist service determines the technique, response threshold and local pathway

Clinical reasoning should use the ICHD-3 criteria and a full headache/neck assessment. CFRT and upper-cervical examination may contribute but are not definitive. Diagnostic blockade is one possible ICHD-3 item demonstrating causation and is a specialist procedure, not a routine physiotherapy confirmation test.

### Differentiation from Migraine and Tension-Type Headache

| Feature | Cervicogenic Headache | Migraine (without aura) | Tension-Type Headache |
|---------|----------------------|-------------------------|-----------------------|
| **Laterality** | Unilateral (consistent side) | Unilateral (variable) | Bilateral |
| **Quality** | Dull, pressing, non-pulsating | Pulsating/throbbing | Pressing, tightening |
| **Severity** | Moderate | Moderate–severe | Mild–moderate |
| **Location** | Starts posterior/occipital → frontotemporal | Frontotemporal ± orbital | Band-like, vertex |
| **Cervical trigger** | Yes — consistent and reproducible | Occasional (trigger) | Occasional |
| **CFRT** | Positive (<32°, symptom reproduction) | Normal (>44°) | Normal or minimally reduced |
| **Nausea/vomiting** | Rarely | Common | Rare |
| **Photophobia/phonophobia** | Mild, if present | Moderate–severe | Mild |
| **Aura** | No | Yes (in 1/3 of migraineurs) | No |
| **Response to NSAIDs** | Partial | Partial–good | Good |
| **Response to triptans** | Poor | Good | No |
| **Response to cervical manual therapy** | Variable; not diagnostic | Variable | Variable |
| **Duration** | Hours–days (variable) | 4–72 hours | 30 min–7 days |
| **Physical activity** | May worsen | Worsens | Not worsened |

<ClinicalNote>
Migraine features can coexist and do not automatically exclude a cervical contribution. Apply the diagnostic criteria for each headache disorder and avoid relying on one cervical test.
</ClinicalNote>

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Headache Type | Key Distinguishing Features | CFRT | Cervical Signs | First-Line Management |
|---------------|----------------------------|------|---------------|-----------------------|
| **Cervicogenic Headache** | Unilateral, consistent side; cervical trigger; restricted CFRT; suboccipital onset | **Positive** | **Present** | Cervical physio |
| **Migraine** | Apply the current ICHD criteria; migraine features may coexist | Variable | Cervical tenderness is non-specific | Medical/headache management according to the established diagnosis |
| **Tension-Type Headache** | Apply the current ICHD criteria | Variable | Pericranial tenderness is non-specific | Management according to the established diagnosis |
| **Medication Overuse Headache** | Consider when headache and acute-medicine use are frequent; apply current headache guidance | Variable | Variable | Medical review; no withdrawal regimen is prescribed here |
| **Cluster Headache** | Severe unilateral orbital/temporal headache with autonomic features | Variable | Cervical findings do not establish the diagnosis | Urgent medical/headache assessment where indicated |
| **Occipital Neuralgia** | Paroxysmal pain in an occipital-nerve distribution with possible allodynia | Variable | Local tenderness is not definitive | Specialist assessment where indicated |
| **Giant Cell Arteritis** | >50 years; temporal artery tenderness; jaw claudication; ESR >50; visual symptoms | Negative | Absent | Same-day emergency medical assessment; treatment is medical-team-led |
| **TMJ Dysfunction** | Pain at TMJ; jaw movement provocation; ear pain; clicking | Negative | Absent | Dental/maxillofacial |
| **Idiopathic Intracranial Hypertension** | Obese female; pulsatile tinnitus; visual obscurations; papilloedema; worse lying | Negative | Absent | Urgent neurology |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

First determine whether the headache presentation is compatible with ICHD-3 cervicogenic headache and whether a secondary-headache warning feature requires medical assessment. Physiotherapy may address a clinically supported cervical contribution after serious pathology has been considered.

Manual therapy, exercise and self-management choices should be individualised. Procedural blocks, radiofrequency treatment, preventive medicines and other specialist interventions require separate specialist assessment and evidence; they are not Version 1 physiotherapy referral thresholds.

---

### Conservative Management

#### Evidence Base

**Jull et al (2002) — Landmark RCT** *(Spine, 27(17): 1835–1843)*
- n=200; 4 groups: manual therapy (MT) alone, specific exercise alone, combined MT+exercise, control
- **Key finding:** Combined MT + specific cervical exercise produced significantly better outcomes than either alone or control
- 10-week intervention; outcomes at 12 months
- Combined group: **76% had ≥50% reduction in headache frequency** at 12 months
- Manual therapy: targeted upper cervical manipulation and mobilisation
- Exercise: specific craniocervical flexion training (deep cervical flexors)

#### C1-2 SNAG (Sustained Natural Apophyseal Glide) — Mulligan Concept

**Technique:**
- Patient seated; therapist applies sustained posteroanterior glide to C1-2 on the symptomatic side using thumb/pisiform contact on the C2 spinous process / articular pillar
- Patient performs active cervical rotation in direction of restriction whilst glide is sustained
- If pain-free rotation improves → positive response, proceed with treatment
- 3 sets × 10 repetitions; can be self-applied with towel (home SNAG)

**Evidence:**
- Hall et al (2007, 2008): C1-2 SNAG significantly reduces headache frequency and intensity in CGH
- Paungmali et al (2003): SNAG produces hypoalgesic effect — pressure pain threshold increases
- Significant improvement in CFRT range of motion following C1-2 SNAG (Hall et al, 2007)

#### Upper Cervical Mobilisation

- Passive accessory intervertebral movements (PAIVMs) at C0-C1, C1-C2, C2-C3
- Grades III–IV (Maitland grading) to restricted, painful segments
- May include: atlanto-occipital decompression, C1-2 rotation mobilisation in flexion
- **Dose:** 3–5 sets of 30–60 second sustained holds or oscillatory grades; reassess after each set
- Evidence: Jull et al (1997, 2002); Zito et al (2006) — upper cervical manual examination and mobilisation reliably identifies and treats CGH

#### Specific Cervical Flexor Retraining

**Deep Cervical Flexor (DCF) Training Protocol** (Jull et al, 2008):

Stage 1 — Activation (Weeks 1–2):
- Craniocervical flexion (nodding) in supine
- Pressure biofeedback: target 22 mmHg; 10 × 10-second holds
- Avoid SCM/anterior scalene substitution

Stage 2 — Endurance (Weeks 3–4):
- Progress biofeedback target: 24–26 mmHg
- 10 × 10-second holds at each level; 3 levels progressed sequentially

Stage 3 — Functional Integration (Weeks 5–10):
- DCF activation during functional tasks
- Sitting posture training
- Progress to 28–30 mmHg as tolerated

Stage 4 — Load/Strength (Weeks 8–12):
- Resistance exercises: supine neck flexion with gravity resistance
- Integration with cervical rotation and lateral flexion

#### Postural Management

- Identify and correct **forward head posture (FHP)** — increases loading on upper cervical joints and suboccipital muscles
- Thoracic mobility: thoracic extension exercises, foam roller mobilisation (T4–T8)
- Workstation ergonomics assessment — screen height, chair position
- Sleep posture: single pillow maintaining neutral cervical alignment; cervical pillow if indicated
- Evidence: Ruivo et al (2017) — FHP correction reduces frequency of cervicogenic-type headache; correlation between FHP and CGH prevalence

#### Exercise Prescription Summary

| Component | Exercise | Sets × Reps | Frequency |
|-----------|----------|-------------|-----------|
| DCF activation | CCF exercise (biofeedback) | 3 × 10 × 10s holds | Daily |
| Cervical mobility | CFRT self-mobilisation (SNAG in flexion) | 3 × 10 | Daily |
| Suboccipital stretching | Self-suboccipital release / chin tuck stretch | 3 × 30s | 2× daily |
| Thoracic extension | Foam roller extension T4-T8 | 3 × 60s | Daily |
| Scapular stabilisation | Wall angels / low row | 3 × 15 | 3×/week |
| Postural re-education | Chin tuck + retraction in sitting | Hourly (work breaks) | Daily |

---

### Pharmacological Management

<ClinicalNote>
Physiotherapy is the **first-line recommended treatment** for CGH. Pharmacological management is adjunctive and should be coordinated with the patient's GP.
</ClinicalNote>

#### Medicines and diagnostic uncertainty

NICE CG150 covers recognised primary headache disorders and must not be presented as a treatment guideline for cervicogenic headache. Analgesic or preventive medication is prescriber-led using the established diagnosis and current guidance. A response or non-response to a triptan does not by itself establish or exclude CGH.

#### Nerve Blocks

Occipital or cervical diagnostic/therapeutic blocks are specialist procedures. Indication, agent, dose, anatomical approach and timing require an appropriate pain/neurology service and are not prescribed by this page.

#### Radiofrequency Neurotomy

- Third occipital nerve RFN (pulsed or conventional)
- **Indication:** Confirmed C2-3 facet-mediated CGH (positive diagnostic block); failed conservative management
- Evidence: Lord et al (1996) — conventional RFN of TON: median pain-free period 297 days vs 8 days sham
- Available via: Belfast City Hospital Pain Management / Neurosurgery

#### Botulinum Toxin (Botox)

- Limited evidence specifically for CGH (stronger evidence base in chronic migraine)
- May be considered for refractory CGH via neurology/pain clinic
- NICE TA260 (2012): Botox approved for chronic migraine (≥15 headache days/month); not specifically for CGH
- Use in CGH remains off-label / specialist decision

#### Preventive Pharmacology (for refractory/chronic presentations)

There is no NICE CG150 preventive-drug recommendation for CGH. Any medication requires confirmation of the headache or neuropathic-pain diagnosis and individual GP/neurology prescribing review.

---

### Onward Referral Criteria

- Use NICE CG150 to identify headache features that warrant further investigation or referral.
- Arrange emergency assessment for an acute neurological emergency, suspected meningitis, stroke or cervical artery dissection.
- Seek medical/specialist review when diagnosis remains uncertain, the headache pattern changes substantially, or symptoms remain disabling despite an appropriate management plan.

Version 1 does not use fixed treatment-session percentages, fixed physiotherapy-failure intervals, diagnostic-block thresholds, spinal-cord stimulation or other procedure criteria as referral rules.

---

## Key References

> All references are from peer-reviewed literature. Access via PubMed, CINAHL, or HSC Library NI (Athens login via nhs.net credentials).

1. **Jull G, Trott P, Potter H, et al.** (2002). A randomized controlled trial of exercise and manipulative therapy for cervicogenic headache. *Spine*, 27(17), 1835–1843. [PMID: 12195063] — *Landmark RCT establishing combined manual therapy + exercise as gold standard treatment.*

2. **Ogince M, Hall T, Robinson K, Blackmore AM.** (2007). The diagnostic validity of the cervical flexion–rotation test in C1/2-related cervicogenic headache. *Manual Therapy*, 12(3), 256–262. [PMID: 16973387] — *CFRT Sn 91%, Sp 90% vs Sjaastad criteria.*

3. **Hall TM, Briffa K, Hopper D, Robinson K.** (2010). The relationship between cervicogenic headache and impairment determined by the flexion-rotation test. *Journal of Manipulative and Physiological Therapeutics*, 33(9), 666–671. [PMID: 21109057] — *CFRT validation vs ICHD-2 criteria; Sn 85%, Sp 83%.*

4. **Sjaastad O, Fredriksen TA, Pfaffenrath V.** (1998). Cervicogenic headache: Diagnostic criteria. *Headache*, 38(6), 442–445. [PMID: 9664748] — *CHISG diagnostic criteria, 1998 revision.*

5. **Headache Classification Committee of the International Headache Society (IHS).** (2018). The International Classification of Headache Disorders, 3rd edition. *Cephalalgia*, 38(1), 1–211. [PMID: 29368949] — *ICHD-3; CGH classified as 11.2.1.*

6. **Hall TM, Briffa K, Hopper D.** (2008). Clinical evaluation of cervicogenic headache: a clinical perspective. *Journal of Manual and Manipulative Therapy*, 16(2), 73–80. — *CFRT cut-off <32°; assessment framework.*

7. **Jull GA.** (1997). Management of cervicogenic headache. In: Boyling JD, Palastanga N (Eds). *Grieve's Modern Manual Therapy* (2nd ed.). Edinburgh: Churchill Livingstone. — *Jull's physiotherapy assessment framework.*

8. **Jull G, Barrett C, Magee R, Ho P.** (1999). Further clinical clarification of the muscle dysfunction in cervical headache. *Cephalalgia*, 19(3), 179–185. [PMID: 10234466] — *DCF dysfunction in CGH.*

9. **Lord SM, Barnsley L, Wallis BJ, McDonald GJ, Bogduk N.** (1996). Percutaneous radio-frequency neurotomy for chronic cervical zygapophyseal-joint pain. *NEJM*, 335(23), 1721–1726. [PMID: 8929263] — *RFN evidence for facet-mediated headache.*

10. **Bogduk N, Govind J.** (2009). Cervicogenic headache: an assessment of the evidence on clinical diagnosis, invasive tests, and treatment. *Lancet Neurology*, 8(10), 959–968. [PMID: 19747657] — *Comprehensive evidence review; prevalence, diagnosis, treatment.*

11. **Zito G, Jull G, Story I.** (2006). Clinical tests of musculoskeletal dysfunction in the diagnosis of cervicogenic headache. *Manual Therapy*, 11(2), 118–129. [PMID: 16290243] — *Upper cervical segmental assessment; Sn 82%, Sp 79%.*

12. **Hall TM, Chan HT, Christensen L, Odenthal B, Wells C, Robinson K.** (2007). Efficacy of a C1-C2 self-sustained natural apophyseal glide (SNAG) in the management of cervicogenic headache. *Journal of Orthopaedic and Sports Physical Therapy*, 37(3), 100–107. [PMID: 17416183] — *C1-2 SNAG evidence.*

13. **NICE CG150.** (2012, updated 2021). *Headaches in over 12s: diagnosis and management*. National Institute for Health and Care Excellence. Available at: nice.org.uk/guidance/cg150 — *UK clinical guideline; pharmacological management.*

14. **Knackstedt H, Bansevicius D, Aaseth K, Grande RB, Lundqvist C, Russell MB.** (2010). Cervicogenic headache in the general population: the Akershus study of chronic headache. *Cephalalgia*, 30(12), 1468–1476. [PMID: 20974604] — *Prevalence and overlap with migraine.*

15. **IFOMPT.** (2020). *International Framework for Examination of the Cervical Region for potential of vascular pathologies of the neck prior to Orthopaedic Manual Therapy Intervention*. [Framework PDF](https://www.ifompt.org/site/ifompt/IFOMPT%20Cervical%20Framework%20final%20September%202020.pdf).

16. **Aprill C, Axinn MJ, Bogduk N.** (1992). Occipital headaches stemming from the lateral atlanto-axial (C1-2) joint. *Cephalalgia*, 12(6), 313–316. [PMID: 1473373] — *C1-2 joint as pain generator; diagnostic specificity.*
