### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Mechanical neck pain (MNP) is the most prevalent cervical spine condition, with a point prevalence of ~14–15% globally and lifetime prevalence up to 67% (Fejer et al., 2006). It is the fourth leading cause of disability worldwide (Hoy et al., 2014). Annual incidence in UK primary care is approximately 15% of adult consultations involving neck-related complaints.

**Definition:** Neck pain of musculoskeletal origin without neurological involvement, arising from cervical structures including intervertebral discs, facet joints, muscles, ligaments, and associated fasciae. Reproduced or modified by cervical movement and mechanical loading.

**Pathological sources:**
- **Facet joints (zygapophyseal joints):** Most common identifiable pain source (~54% in chronic neck pain by Bogduk & McGuirk, 2006); intra-articular meniscoid inclusions, capsular strain, synovitis
- **Intervertebral disc (discogenic pain):** Annular tears, internal disc disruption; C5/6 and C6/7 most common
- **Muscles and myofascial tissue:** Trapezius, levator scapulae, splenius capitis, suboccipitals — trigger points, postural overload
- **Ligaments:** Posterior longitudinal, interspinous ligaments with repetitive strain
- **Atlantoaxial and atlanto-occipital joints:** Particularly relevant in upper cervical pain

**Classification (Guzman et al., 2008 — Bone and Joint Decade 2000–2010 Task Force):**
- **Grade I:** No signs of major pathology, does not interfere with daily activities
- **Grade II:** No signs of major pathology, does interfere with daily activities
- **Grade III:** Neck pain with neurological signs (radiculopathy)
- **Grade IV:** Neck pain with signs of major structural pathology

MNP broadly covers Grades I–II.

**Risk factors:**
- Sustained postures (sedentary work, screen use >6 hours/day)
- Psychosocial: high job demands, low control, poor support, depression, anxiety
- Previous neck pain episode (strongest predictor of recurrence)
- Female sex (RR ~1.4)
- Age 45–54 years peak
- Sleep disturbance

**Prognosis:** Most acute MNP (>50%) resolves within 6 weeks; however, ~40–50% develop persistent or recurrent symptoms at 1 year (Haldeman et al., 2008).

---

## Special Tests

Mechanical neck pain is primarily a **diagnosis of exclusion** — the primary role of clinical assessment is to rule out serious pathology and neurological involvement, then classify the mechanical presentation.

| Test | Sensitivity | Specificity | +LR | −LR | Evidence Level |
|------|-------------|-------------|-----|-----|----------------|
| Spurling's (negative — rules in MNP over radiculopathy) | 0.50–0.60 | 0.86–0.97 | — | — | High (ruling out radiculopathy) |
| Passive cervical rotation <32° (chronic pain predictor) | 0.77 | 0.69 | 2.5 | 0.33 | Moderate |
| PAIVMs (Maitland): segmental tenderness | ~0.70 | ~0.60 | — | — | Low–Moderate |
| Cervical Flexion-Rotation Test (C1/2 contribution to pain) | 0.91 | 0.90 | 9.1 | 0.10 | High (Hall et al., 2010) |
| Facet joint loading test (quadrant position) | 0.47 | 0.74 | 1.8 | 0.72 | Low |
| Active cervical ROM limitation (any plane) | 0.57 | 0.53 | 1.2 | 0.81 | Low |
| Palpation of upper cervical joints (C0–2) | 0.82 | 0.79 | 3.9 | 0.23 | Moderate (Jull et al., 1988) |
| Craniocervical flexion test (CCFT — deep neck flexor activation) | — | — | — | — | Construct validity |

**Clinical Prediction Rule for Cervical Manipulation (Childs et al., 2004):**
≥3 of 5 predictors → +LR 13.5 for success with HVLA manipulation:
1. Symptom duration <38 days
2. Positive expectation that manipulation will help
3. Side-to-side cervical rotation difference >10°
4. Pain with spring test at C3–5
5. Not performing sedentary work (>50% of day)

**Key assessments:**
- **Active ROM** (goniometry or CROM device): flexion, extension, lateral flexion, rotation — establish baseline and monitor
- **PPIVMs**: C2–7 intersegmental mobility
- **PAIVMs**: posteroanterior pressure C1–7 — identify comparable sign (pain provocation at symptomatic segment)
- **Deep neck flexor endurance**: CCFT or cranio-cervical flexion endurance test — assess neuromuscular control
- **Postural assessment**: forward head posture, thoracic kyphosis, scapular position

---

## Red Flags

Screen for serious pathology, including neurological/cord involvement, vascular pathology, significant trauma, fracture, infection, malignancy and inflammatory disease.

- Apply the Canadian C-Spine Rule only in an appropriate trauma presentation; it is not a general neck-pain screening rule.
- Use IFOMPT 2020 risk-based reasoning for possible vascular pathology. New cranial nerve dysfunction is concerning when it occurs within a vascular or neurological presentation; the historical “5Ds/3Ns” are not a clearance rule.
- Suspected meningitis, acute neurological deterioration or another emergency presentation requires emergency assessment. Other concerns require medical assessment with urgency matched to the presentation.

Assess psychosocial prognostic factors where useful, but do not use an unsupported fixed STarT MSK threshold to prescribe a treatment package.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Maitland Concept
- Identify **comparable sign** (the movement or palpation finding that most reproduces the patient's pain)
- **Symptom/resistance dominance**:
  - Pain-dominant (acute, irritable): Grade I–II oscillatory techniques for pain neurophysiology modulation
  - Resistance-dominant (subacute/chronic, stiff): Grade III–IV to increase mobility
- **Regular reassessment** ("asterisk sign"): track change in ROM, pain, function after each technique
- PAIVMs: C1–7 central PAs, unilateral PAs, transverse pressures
- PPIVMs: rotation and lateral flexion intersegmental mobility assessment
- Progression: add cervical rotation, extension, lateral flexion

### McKenzie / MDT
- Classify as **Derangement** (most common), **Dysfunction**, **Postural Syndrome**, or **Other**
- Repeated movement assessment (10 repetitions each direction)
- **Postural Syndrome**: pain only at end range/sustained posture; resolves with posture correction; no structural change
- **Dysfunction Syndrome**: pain at end range due to contracted/adherent tissue; intermittent pain at end range only; treatment: repeated stretching
- **Derangement**: most common; centralisation/peripheralisation determines direction; treat with directional preference exercises
- **Retraction** exercises (cervical retractions) as first-line for most cervical derangements

### Norwegian (Kaltenborn-Evjenth) Approach
- Segment-specific mobilisation based on concave-convex rules
- Traction grades I–III; glides in plane of facet joint

### Biopsychosocial / Stratified Care
- **STarT MSK (3-tier):**
  - Low risk: brief physiotherapy (2–3 sessions), self-management booklet
  - Medium risk: standard physiotherapy (6–8 sessions)
  - High risk: physiotherapy + targeted psychosocial intervention, consider psychological referral
- **Pain neuroscience education (PNE)**: Moseley & Butler framework — reduces catastrophisation and fear-avoidance

---

## Outcome Measures

### Neck Disability Index (NDI)
- Widely used measure of neck-related disability
- 10 items × 0–5 = 0–50 total (reported as percentage: score/50 × 100)
- **MCID**: 7.5 points (50-point scale) for neck pain; ~5 points for chronic non-specific neck pain (Pool et al., 2007)
- MDC (95% CI): 10.5 points
- High reliability ICC 0.89–0.93

### Numeric Pain Rating Scale (NPRS)
- 0–10; **MCID**: 1.5–2.5 points for non-specific neck pain (Cleland et al., 2008)
- Measure at rest, with movement, worst in 24 hours

### Patient-Specific Functional Scale (PSFS)
- 0–10 per activity (3 patient-identified activities)
- **MCID**: 2.0 points average across activities (Cleland et al., 2006)
- High responsiveness and patient-centredness

### Global Rating of Change (GROC)
- 15-point scale; use at 4 weeks and discharge
- +5 or above = meaningful improvement

### Keele STarT MSK / Örebro MSK Questionnaire (Short Form)
- **Stratification tool**, not outcome measure per se
- Re-administer at 4–6 weeks to confirm psychosocial risk trajectory

### Fear-Avoidance Beliefs Questionnaire (FABQ)
- Work and Physical Activity subscales (0–66 and 0–24)
- High scores (>29 work subscale) predict poor return-to-work; inform targeted management

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

**MNP is a clinical diagnosis** — imaging rarely changes management in uncomplicated cases.

**Imaging guidelines:**
- Imaging decisions require the clinical context, red-flag assessment and applicable local/specialist pathway; no condition-specific NICE neck-imaging recommendation is asserted here.
- X-ray: low utility for soft tissue pathology; use if fracture suspected (post-trauma, osteoporosis, malignancy)
- CT: bony anatomy, foraminal stenosis, OPLL when MRI contraindicated
- Normal imaging does NOT exclude pain; degenerative changes are ubiquitous and poorly correlate with symptoms

**Key evidence:**
- Asymptomatic degenerative changes on MRI present in: 87% at age 70, 50% at age 40 (Boden et al., 1990)
- Imaging findings should be interpreted within clinical context — avoid iatrogenic nocebo
- Systematic review (Rubinstein & van Tulder, 2008): clinical examination sufficient for diagnosis and management in non-complex cases

**Yellow Flag Screening (STarT MSK):**
- Prognostic stratification improves outcomes vs. one-size-fits-all care (Hill et al., 2011 — LBP evidence extrapolated to neck pain; Beneciuk et al., 2013 for cervical)

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Key Distinguishing Features |
|-----------|----------------------------|
| Cervical radiculopathy | Radicular arm pain in dermatomal pattern, positive Spurling's, neurological deficit |
| Cervicogenic headache | Headache referral from C1–3, positive FRT, unilateral, occipital to frontotemporal |
| Fibromyalgia | Widespread pain, multiple tender points, fatigue, sleep disturbance |
| Rheumatoid arthritis / inflammatory arthritis | Morning stiffness, bilateral, systemic features, elevated ESR/CRP, ANA positive |
| Ankylosing spondylitis | Young male, sacroiliac involvement, HLA-B27, bamboo spine on X-ray |
| Thoracic outlet syndrome | Positional vascular/neurogenic symptoms with arm elevation |
| Temporomandibular joint dysfunction | Jaw pain, crepitus, bruxism, ear pain, limited mouth opening |
| Occipital neuralgia | Sharp, stabbing occipital pain, positive Tinel over GON, distinct from cervicogenic headache |
| Whiplash associated disorder | Mechanism of injury (acceleration-deceleration), specific classification (WAD grade) |
| Muscle tension headache | Bilateral, pressure/band-like, no photophobia, not worsened by movement |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Mechanical neck pain without serious-pathology features is generally managed with education, activity, exercise and individualised adjunctive care. Access routes and service capacity vary; use the current local musculoskeletal pathway.

Psychosocial assessment may inform shared planning, but Version 1 does not prescribe fixed session allocations or a threshold-based treatment package.

---

### Conservative Management

**Exercise Therapy (Grade A — highest evidence for neck pain):**
- **Deep neck flexor (DNF) strengthening**: Craniocervical flexion test (CCFT) protocol — progressive pressure biofeedback (20–26–30 mmHg); Jull et al. (2002, 2009)
  - Proven superior to general neck exercise for chronic neck pain
  - 8–10 week programme; 3–5 × per week
- **Cervical and scapulothoracic strengthening**: Trapezius, rhomboids, serratus anterior — 3 × 10–15 RM, progressive
- **Aerobic exercise**: Walking, swimming; reduces disability and pain catastrophising; NICE recommendation
- **Proprioceptive/sensorimotor training**: Joint position error retraining; laser pointer head exercises; supports neuromuscular control deficits in chronic neck pain (Revel et al., 1994)
- **Stretching**: Suboccipital, levator scapulae, scalenes; limited standalone evidence but patient-valued and safe

**Manual Therapy (Grade B–A):**
- Combined manipulation + exercise superior to either alone (Hoving et al., 2002; Hurwitz et al., 2002)
- **Cervical mobilisation**: Safe and effective; Maitland Grade III–IV PAIVMs/PPIVMs
- **Cervical HVLA manipulation**: Any use requires informed consent, exclusion of contraindications and patient-specific reasoning under the IFOMPT 2020 framework; it is not selected by a prediction rule alone
- **Thoracic manipulation**: Level A evidence adjunct for cervical pain (Cleland et al., 2005, 2007)
- **Soft tissue therapy**: Trigger point release, myofascial release — adjunct; limited standalone evidence

**Patient Education (Grade A):**
- PNE: Explain pain as non-threatening; reassure re: natural history; de-medicalize
- Postural advice: Ergonomic screen height, chair adjustment, monitor distance, pillow height (cervical neutral)
- Activity modification: vary sustained postures and use tolerable movement breaks according to symptoms and task demands
- Return to activity encouraged: "motion is lotion" messaging

**Dry Needling / Acupuncture:**
- Trigger point dry needling: Moderate evidence for short-term pain relief in myofascial neck pain (Tough et al., 2009)
- NICE NG193 permits a limited course of acupuncture or dry needling for **chronic primary pain** under its specified conditions; applicability to an individual with mechanical neck pain requires confirmation that the chronic-primary-pain scope is met

### Pharmacological (Medical/Prescriber-Led)

No NICE guideline cited here supplies a mechanical-neck-pain dosing pathway. Medication selection, dose, duration and gastroprotection require an individual prescriber assessment and current prescribing guidance. NG193 recommendations about antidepressants, opioids, gabapentinoids, benzodiazepines, NSAIDs and paracetamol apply specifically to management of **chronic primary pain** and must not be extrapolated automatically to all mechanical neck pain.

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency or urgent assessment:** according to the actual suspected pathology, including acute neurological deterioration, meningitis, significant trauma, fracture or vascular emergency.
- **Prompt medical/specialist assessment:** suspected DCM, progressive neurological change, serious infection or malignancy.
- **Reassess and consider onward referral:** when diagnosis remains uncertain or symptoms remain substantially disabling despite an appropriate conservative plan.

Occupational, psychological and pain-service referrals should be based on individual need and the current local pathway, not a fixed treatment duration.

---

## Key References

1. Fejer R, Kyvik KO, Hartvigsen J. The prevalence of neck pain in the world population: a systematic critical review of the literature. *Eur Spine J*. 2006;15(6):834–848.

2. Hoy D, March L, Woolf A, et al. The global burden of neck pain: estimates from the global burden of disease 2010 study. *Ann Rheum Dis*. 2014;73(7):1309–1315.

3. Jull G, Trott P, Potter H, et al. A randomized controlled trial of exercise and manipulative therapy for cervicogenic headache. *Spine*. 2002;27(17):1835–1843.

4. Childs JD, Fritz JM, Flynn TW, et al. A clinical prediction rule to identify patients with low back pain most likely to benefit from spinal manipulation: a validation study. *Ann Intern Med*. 2004;141(12):920–928.

5. Cleland JA, Childs JD, Fritz JM, Whitman JM, Eberhart SL. Development of a clinical prediction rule for guiding treatment of a subgroup of patients with neck pain: use of thoracic spine manipulation, exercise, and patient education. *Phys Ther*. 2007;87(1):9–23.

6. Hoving JL, Koes BW, de Vet HC, et al. Manual therapy, physical therapy, or continued care by a general practitioner for patients with neck pain. *Ann Intern Med*. 2002;136(10):713–722.

7. Pool JJ, Ostelo RW, Hoving JL, Bouter LM, de Vet HC. Minimal clinically important change of the neck disability index and the numerical rating scale for patients with neck pain. *Spine*. 2007;32(26):3047–3051.

8. Hill JC, Whitehurst DG, Lewis M, et al. Comparison of stratified primary care management for low back pain with current best practice (STarT Back): a randomised controlled trial. *Lancet*. 2011;378(9802):1560–1571.

9. NICE. Neck pain – non-specific. Clinical Knowledge Summary. 2023. Available at: cks.nice.org.uk/topics/neck-pain-non-specific/

10. NICE. Chronic pain (primary and secondary) in over 16s: assessment of all chronic pain and management of chronic primary pain. NG193. 2021.

11. NICE. *Chronic pain (primary and secondary) in over 16s: assessment of all chronic pain and management of chronic primary pain*. NG193. 2021. [Recommendations](https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng193/chapter/recommendations). Scope must be confirmed before applying its treatment recommendations.

12. Rubinstein SM, van Tulder M. A best-evidence review of diagnostic procedures for neck and low-back pain. *Best Pract Res Clin Rheumatol*. 2008;22(3):471–482.

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Whiplash Associated Disorders (WAD) is the term describing the constellation of symptoms following an acceleration-deceleration injury to the cervical spine, most commonly from motor vehicle collisions (MVCs). The Québec Task Force (QTF) on Whiplash-Associated Disorders (Spitzer et al., 1995) provides the foundational classification framework.

**Epidemiology:**
- Annual incidence: 235–300 per 100,000 in Western countries
- UK: ~250,000–300,000 per year; high rates linked to insurance claims (CEGA/ABI data)
- Most common MVC injury type; estimated annual cost to NHS and insurance >£2 billion
- Female:Male ratio ~1.4:1
- Peak incidence: 20–40 years

**Mechanism of Injury:**
- Rear-end collision most common: initial extension of lower cervical spine followed by rapid flexion of upper cervical spine (S-curve deformation)
- This S-shaped motion is different from simple extension — it causes:
  - Stretching of anterior longitudinal ligament
  - Compression of posterior elements (facet joints, nerve roots)
  - Differential motion between segments with relative translation
  - Strain of zygapophyseal joint capsules (most consistent pain source)
- Side-impact and frontal collisions also produce WAD with different biomechanics
- No single lesion visible on standard imaging in most cases (hence "soft tissue injury")

**Pathological pain sources:**
1. **Zygapophyseal (facet) joints** — most consistently identified pain source; Barnsley et al. (1995) demonstrated by diagnostic nerve blocks that facet joint pain present in ~54% of chronic WAD
2. **Intervertebral discs**: Annular tears, especially C5/6 and C6/7
3. **Musculature**: Multifidus, semispinalis, suboccipitals — MRI evidence of fatty infiltration and atrophy in chronic WAD (Elliott et al., 2006)
4. **Neural tissue**: Dorsal root ganglia sensitisation; central sensitisation
5. **Ligamentous injury**: Alar, transverse, C2–3 ligaments (rare but important in higher grades)
6. **Sympathetic nervous system involvement**: autonomic dysregulation; links to widespread hypersensitivity

**Neurobiological Mechanisms:**
- Peripheral sensitisation: nociceptors in joint capsule, disc sensitised by mechanical and chemical stimuli
- Central sensitisation: key driver of chronic WAD — explains widespread hypersensitivity, cold hyperalgesia, psychological distress seen in Grade III WAD
- Psychosocial factors amplify and perpetuate sensitisation

**Québec Task Force Classification:**

| Grade | Clinical Presentation |
|-------|-----------------------|
| WAD 0 | No neck complaints, no physical signs |
| WAD I | Neck complaint (pain, stiffness, tenderness), no physical signs |
| WAD II | Neck complaint + musculoskeletal signs (reduced ROM, point tenderness) |
| WAD III | Neck complaint + neurological signs (reflex changes, weakness, sensory deficit) |
| WAD IV | Neck complaint + fracture or dislocation |

WAD III is analogous to cervical radiculopathy following whiplash mechanism.

---

## Special Tests

| Test | Sensitivity | Specificity | +LR | −LR | Evidence Level |
|------|-------------|-------------|-----|-----|----------------|
| Cold Hyperalgesia (cold pain threshold) as predictor of chronicity | 0.72 | 0.65 | 2.1 | 0.43 | Moderate (Chien et al., 2008) |
| Widespread Pressure Pain Hyperalgesia (PPT) | 0.68 | 0.64 | 1.9 | 0.50 | Moderate |
| Spurling's Test (for WAD III/radiculopathy) | 0.50–0.60 | 0.86–0.97 | 3.5–6.1 | 0.47 | High |
| ULNT1 (median nerve neurodynamic) | 0.72–0.97 | 0.22–0.33 | 1.1–1.3 | 0.12 | Moderate |
| Neck Distraction Test (WAD III) | 0.44 | 0.90 | 4.4 | 0.62 | Moderate |
| Cervical ROM < 40° rotation (prediction of non-recovery) | 0.43 | 0.78 | 1.95 | 0.73 | Moderate (Ritchie et al., 2015) |
| IES-R score ≥26 (Impact of Event Scale — psychological predictor) | 0.72 | 0.72 | 2.6 | 0.39 | Moderate (Buitenhuis et al., 2006) |
| Hyperalgesia to blunt pressure at cervical spine | 0.79 | 0.60 | 1.98 | 0.35 | Moderate (Walton et al., 2013) |
| Neck pain NRS ≥5/10 at 1 week (predicts chronicity) | 0.68 | 0.55 | 1.51 | 0.58 | Moderate |

**Prognostic Risk Stratification — Prescreening:**
Use **WAD Prognostic Screening Tool (Sterling 2010)** or **Neck Disability Index ≥30% at 1 month** as predictor of chronic outcome.

**Prediction of Non-Recovery (Sterling et al., 2005, 2010):**
Elevated psychological distress + high pain intensity at 4 weeks + sensory hypersensitivity → high risk for non-recovery at 12 months

**Assessment priorities in WAD:**
1. **ROM measurement** (CROM device or goniometer): all planes; identify pain provocation and end-range quality
2. **Neurological screening** (WAD III): reflexes, motor, sensation
3. **Quantitative Sensory Testing (QST)**: pressure pain thresholds (PPT) with algometer if available — identifies central sensitisation
4. **Psychological screening**: Impact of Event Scale-Revised (IES-R), Tampa Scale of Kinesiophobia (TSK), Örebro MSK Questionnaire (Short Form)
5. **Vascular-pathology clinical reasoning**: apply the International IFOMPT Cervical Framework (2020); do not use provocative positional testing as a safety clearance

---

## Red Flags

For suspected cervical spine injury after trauma, apply the Canadian C-Spine Rule as described in NICE NG41. Adults should receive CT when imaging is indicated by that rule. Do not use NEXUS as an alternative Version 1 rule unless it is separately sourced and governed.

- Suspected fracture/dislocation or neurological deficit after trauma: protect the cervical spine and use the emergency trauma pathway.
- Possible cervical artery dissection: use IFOMPT 2020 risk-based reasoning. Severe unusual head/neck pain with acute focal neurological, cranial nerve, ataxic or Horner features requires emergency medical assessment.
- Possible cord injury: bilateral or multisegmental neurological change, gait disturbance or new bladder/bowel disturbance requires prompt escalation; acute deterioration is an emergency.

Keep vascular, cord and trauma-imaging decisions separate. Do not use positional testing or the historical “5Ds/3Ns” as vascular clearance.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Maitland Concept
- Acute WAD (0–6 weeks): Grade I–II oscillatory techniques for pain modulation; gentle cervical mobilisation
- Subacute/chronic (>6 weeks): Progress to Grade III–IV techniques addressing stiffness; PAIVMs/PPIVMs
- Comparable sign methodology: identify and track comparable pain response
- Avoid techniques that peripheralise neurological symptoms (WAD III)

### McKenzie / MDT
- Acute WAD: pain frequently multidirectional initially; retraction exercises as starting point
- Look for directional preference (may be slower to emerge than in disc pathology)
- If centralisation occurs → directional exercise
- Education re: natural history and active recovery integral to MDT approach

### Graded Activity / Behavioural Approach
- Evidence supports active management over passive rest in WAD I–II (Rosenfeld et al., 2003)
- Graded activity exposure: progressive increase in meaningful activities despite pain
- Graded motor imagery (GMI) and mirror therapy: limited evidence; consider in complex chronic WAD

### Psychologically Informed Practice (PiP)
- Critical in medium-high risk WAD (IES-R ≥26, TSK ≥37)
- Address fear-avoidance, catastrophising, catastrophic thinking about injury
- CBT-based physiotherapy (Linton & Andersson, 2000 principles)
- Link with psychology services for high-risk presentations

### Sterling's Risk-Stratified Approach (2010)
- **Low risk (WAD I–II, low distress)**: Self-management advice, reassurance, brief physiotherapy
- **Moderate risk (WAD II–III, moderate distress)**: 8–10 sessions physiotherapy + progressive exercise + psychological support
- **High risk (high distress, widespread hyperalgesia)**: Intensive physiotherapy + psychology liaison + pharmacological management of central sensitisation

---

## Outcome Measures

### Neck Disability Index (NDI)
- Primary outcome for WAD disability
- **MCID**: 7.5 points (50-point scale); chronic WAD: 8.5 points (Jull et al., 2014)
- Use at baseline, 6 weeks, 3 months, 6 months, 12 months
- NDI ≥30% at 1 month predicts chronic disability

### Numeric Pain Rating Scale (NPRS)
- 0–10; **MCID**: 2.1 points in WAD (Sterling et al., 2010)
- Measure arm pain (if WAD III) and neck pain separately

### Patient-Specific Functional Scale (PSFS)
- Highly responsive to change; **MCID**: 2.0 points average
- Functional activities specific to patient's life/work

### Impact of Event Scale-Revised (IES-R)
- 22-item PTSD screening tool; score 0–88
- ≥26: moderate impact (risk of PTSD); ≥33: probable PTSD
- Not an outcome measure per se but critical prognostic screener in WAD
- Re-administer at 4 weeks if initially borderline

### Tampa Scale of Kinesiophobia (TSK)
- 17-item scale; 17–68; ≥37 = high kinesiophobia
- **MCID**: 4 points; use alongside FABQ to guide psychosocial management

### GROC (Global Rating of Change)
- 15-point scale; meaningful improvement ≥+5
- 6 weeks, 3 months, discharge

### Whiplash Disability Questionnaire (WDQ)
- 13-item WAD-specific disability questionnaire; 0–130
- **MCID**: 13.3 points; validated specifically in whiplash populations (Pinfold et al., 2004)

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

**No imaging required for WAD 0–II without high-risk features (Canadian C-Spine Rule).**

**Imaging guidelines:**
- X-ray: rule out fracture/dislocation; dynamic X-rays (flexion/extension) for suspected instability — limited sensitivity
- CT: preferred for high-energy trauma; superior bony detail; rule out fracture, SCIWORA
- MRI: WAD III (neurological signs) or if neurological deterioration; assess disc, cord, nerve root; also for suspected ligamentous injury
- Suspected traumatic cervical artery dissection requires urgent/emergency medical assessment; vascular imaging such as CTA or MRI/MRA should be selected within the acute medical/stroke pathway.

**Prognostic factors (strong evidence — Walton et al., 2013 systematic review):**
- High initial pain intensity (NRS ≥5): negative prognostic factor
- High initial disability (NDI ≥30%): negative prognostic factor
- Cold hyperalgesia: negative prognostic factor (central sensitisation marker)
- Psychological distress (IES-R, anxiety): negative prognostic factor
- Low self-efficacy: negative prognostic factor
- Compensation claims in some jurisdictions: controversial; complex psychosocial interaction

**Active management vs. rest:**
- Rosenfeld et al. (2003) RCT: Active treatment (early mobilisation) superior to collar/rest at 6 months and 3 years
- Söderlund & Lindberg (2001): early activation combined with education reduces progression to chronicity

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Key Distinguishing Features |
|-----------|----------------------------|
| Cervical fracture/dislocation (WAD IV) | High-energy mechanism, midline tenderness, neurological deficit, X-ray/CT confirms |
| Traumatic vertebral artery dissection | Severe occipital headache, brainstem signs, Horner's, ataxia — CTA/MRI |
| Traumatic disc herniation (WAD III) | Radicular symptoms, positive Spurling's/distraction, MRI confirmation |
| Concussion / mild TBI | Associated head impact, cognitive symptoms, headache, vestibular signs |
| TMJ injury (post-MVC) | Jaw pain, crepitus, limited mouth opening, pain with chewing |
| PTSD | Psychological re-experiencing, avoidance, hyperarousal — IES-R ≥33; requires psychology |
| Shoulder injury (rotator cuff) | Mechanism of arm bracing, shoulder-localised pain, arc test |
| Thoracic outlet syndrome | Vascular/neurogenic symptoms with arm elevation, positional |
| SCIWORA | Spinal cord injury without radiological abnormality — MRI required |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

After serious injury has been excluded, WAD I–II may be managed using education, activity, exercise and individualised physiotherapy. Psychological distress and possible PTSD should be assessed and referred according to clinical need and the current local pathway.

Progressive neurological deficit, suspected cord injury or possible cervical artery dissection requires condition-appropriate prompt or emergency assessment. Version 1 does not prescribe fixed pain-clinic, psychology or surgery timelines.

---

### Conservative Management

**Active Treatment — Grade A evidence:**

**Exercise:**
- Early active mobilisation: gentle cervical ROM exercises from day 1 post-injury (Rosenfeld et al., 2003 — significantly better outcomes than immobilisation)
- Progressive cervical and scapulothoracic strengthening — as per mechanical neck pain protocol
- Deep neck flexor activation (CCFT protocol) — impaired in acute and chronic WAD (Jull et al., 2008)
- Proprioceptive/sensorimotor exercises: laser pointer gaze stability, joint position error retraining

**Manual Therapy — Grade B:**
- Cervical mobilisation: Maitland Grade I–II → III–IV as irritability settles
- Thoracic manipulation: safer alternative to cervical HVLA; Grade A evidence as adjunct
- Cervical HVLA: do not proceed where instability, vascular pathology or another contraindication is suspected; apply the IFOMPT 2020 framework and an individual risk–benefit/consent process

**Education — Grade A:**
- Most powerful single intervention for acute WAD (with exercise)
- Reassurance: natural history is favourable in WAD I–II; >70% recover within 3 months
- Pain neuroscience education: address sensitisation, avoid catastrophising
- Activity continuation messaging: "keep moving, stay active"
- Whiplash patient information booklet (RACGP/Arthritis Australia evidence-based resources applicable to NI)

**Physical Modalities:**
- Heat/ice: minimal evidence; low harm; patient-directed short-term use
- TENS: limited evidence; may provide short-term pain relief
- Ultrasound/laser: insufficient evidence; not recommended as primary treatment
- Acupuncture: Moderate evidence for short-term pain relief in chronic neck pain; NICE allows referral

**Psychological Approaches (High-Risk WAD):**
- CBT-based physiotherapy: fear-avoidance model
- Eye Movement Desensitisation and Reprocessing (EMDR): for confirmed PTSD — psychology-delivered
- Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (ACT) principles incorporated into physiotherapy

### Pharmacological (Medical/Prescriber-Led)

**Acute (<6 weeks):**
- Analgesia and any medicine for acute WAD require an individual prescriber assessment and current prescribing guidance; this page does not specify drug doses or durations

**Subacute/Chronic (>6 weeks):**
- Sensory hypersensitivity alone does not establish a neuropathic-pain indication. NICE **CG173** applies to confirmed neuropathic pain, while NG193 drug recommendations apply specifically to chronic primary pain; neither is direct acute-WAD prescribing guidance

**Injections:**
- Medial branch blocks / radiofrequency neurotomy for confirmed facet joint pain (Barnsley et al., 1995; Lord et al., 1996): evidence-based for chronic WAD with confirmed facet pain; refer to Pain Clinic/Spinal service

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency:** suspected fracture/dislocation, acute spinal cord injury, or suspected cervical artery dissection/stroke with acute neurological features.
- **Prompt specialist assessment:** progressive neurological or motor deficit.
- **Individual onward referral:** consider referral when symptoms remain disabling, significant psychological distress is present, or PTSD is suspected, using the current local pathway.

Version 1 does not use fixed 4-week, 12-week, WAD-grade surgery or PiP/CBT timing rules.

---

4. Sterling M, Jull G, Vicenzino B, Kenardy J. Sensory hypersensitivity occurs soon after whiplash injury and is associated with poor recovery. *Pain*. 2003;104(3):509–517.

5. Sterling M. A proposed new classification system for whiplash associated disorders — implications for assessment and management. *Man Ther*. 2004;9(2):60–70.

6. Rosenfeld M, Gunnarsson R, Borenstein P. Early intervention in whiplash-associated disorders: a comparison of two treatment protocols. *Spine*. 2000;25(14):1782–1787.

7. Walton DM, Pretty J, MacDermid JC, Teasell RW. Risk factors for persistent problems following whiplash injury: results of a systematic review and meta-analysis. *J Orthop Sports Phys Ther*. 2009;39(5):334–350.

9. Lord SM, Barnsley L, Wallis BJ, McDonald GJ, Bogduk N. Percutaneous radio-frequency neurotomy for chronic cervical zygapophyseal-joint pain. *N Engl J Med*. 1996;335(23):1721–1726.

10. Jull G, Sterling M, Kenardy J, Beller E. Does the presence of sensory hypersensitivity influence outcomes of physical rehabilitation for chronic whiplash? — A preliminary RCT. *Pain*. 2007;129(1–2):28–34.

11. IFOMPT. *International Framework for Examination of the Cervical Region for potential of vascular pathologies of the neck prior to Orthopaedic Manual Therapy Intervention*. Accepted October 2020. ### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

The acromioclavicular joint (ACJ) is a diarthrodial joint between the distal clavicle and the acromion, stabilised by the acromioclavicular (AC) and coracoclavicular (CC) ligaments (trapezoid and conoid). It transmits forces between the upper limb and axial skeleton and allows approximately 20° of rotation during shoulder elevation.

### Conditions Covered

**ACJ Sprain / Separation**
Traumatic disruption of AC and/or CC ligaments, typically from a direct fall onto the point of the shoulder or onto an outstretched hand (FOOSH). The distal clavicle rides superiorly relative to the acromion proportional to ligamentous damage. Most common in males aged 20–40 and in contact sports (rugby, GAA, cycling).

**ACJ Osteoarthritis**
Degenerative joint disease of the ACJ, often presenting in middle-aged to older adults. Associated with repetitive overhead loading, prior trauma, and distal clavicle osteolysis. Cartilaginous narrowing, subchondral sclerosis, and osteophyte formation reduce the subacromial space and may contribute to secondary impingement.

**Distal Clavicle Osteolysis**
Stress-related resorption of the distal clavicle, classically in weight-lifters and overhead athletes. Histology shows microfractures, vascular proliferation, and osteoclastic resorption without infection. X-ray shows tapering and irregular resorption of the distal 2–3 cm of clavicle.

---

## Rockwood Classification (ACJ Separation)

| Grade | Structures Damaged | Clavicle Displacement | Management |
|-------|-------------------|----------------------|------------|
| **I** | AC ligament sprain only | None | Conservative |
| **II** | AC ligament torn, CC sprain | < 25% superior | Conservative |
| **III** | AC + CC ligaments torn | 25–100% superior | Controversial (usually conservative) |
| **IV** | AC + CC torn; clavicle displaced posteriorly into trapezius | Posterior | Surgical |
| **V** | AC + CC torn; deltotrapezial fascia stripped | 100–300% superior | Surgical |
| **VI** | Inferior dislocation (rare) | Inferior to coracoid | Surgical |

> **Clinical Note:** Rockwood I–II injuries are generally managed non-operatively. Grade III is usually managed non-operatively initially and individualised if symptoms persist. Grades IV–VI warrant orthopaedic assessment; this does not make surgery automatic.

---

## Special Tests

| Test | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| **O'Brien's Active Compression Test** | May reproduce pain localised to the AC joint | Also provokes other shoulder structures; not diagnostic alone |
| **Cross-Body Adduction Test** | Loads the AC joint and may reproduce familiar pain | Interpret with history and local examination |
| **Paxinos Test** | Direct AC-joint provocation | Not diagnostic alone |
| **Horizontal Adduction Stress Test** | AC-joint loading | Limited discrimination in isolation |
| **ACJ Palpation** | Helps localise symptoms | Tenderness alone does not establish the diagnosis |

> **Diagnostic Tip:** O'Brien's is most useful when pain is reproduced at the top of the shoulder AND relieved with palm-up position. Pain deep in the shoulder suggests SLAP lesion rather than ACJ pathology.

### Diagnostic Injection
A diagnostic injection may be considered by an appropriate specialist when uncertainty remains. It is not required for routine physiotherapy assessment, and the specialist determines the medicine, technique and interpretation.

---

## Red Flags

- Severe traumatic ACJ injury, open injury, skin compromise, associated fracture/dislocation or neurovascular compromise requires urgent or emergency assessment according to the presentation.
- Systemic inflammatory features across multiple joints should prompt appropriate medical/rheumatology assessment; do not use a rigid morning-stiffness threshold in isolation.
- Suspected infection or malignancy requires medical assessment under the applicable current pathway and criteria.

Distal-clavicle osteolysis, PVNS and metabolic bone disease are differentials, not automatic red-flag pathways. Investigation and referral should follow the clinical presentation and appropriate specialist guidance.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Diagnostic Algorithm

```
Mechanism of injury?
├─ Trauma (fall onto shoulder/FOOSH)
│   ├─ Check Rockwood grade clinically + X-ray (AP + Zanca view)
│   ├─ Grade I–II → Physiotherapy (see management)
│   ├─ Grade III → Shared decision-making; most → conservative
│   └─ Grade IV–VI → Orthopaedic referral (surgical)
└─ Insidious / overuse
    ├─ ACJ OA or distal clavicle osteolysis
    ├─ Cross-body adduction + Paxinos test
    ├─ X-ray (Zanca view preferred): OA changes, osteolysis, joint space narrowing
    └─ Diagnostic injection if diagnosis unclear
```

### ICF Framework

| Domain | Example |
|--------|---------|
| **Body structure/function** | ACJ instability, pain, reduced ROM, crepitus |
| **Activity limitation** | Difficulty dressing, reaching overhead, carrying |
| **Participation restriction** | Unable to return to sport, work overhead tasks |
| **Contextual factors** | Sport demands, occupation, psychosocial, activity goals |

---

## Outcome Measures

| Measure | MCID | MDC | Population | Notes |
|---------|------|-----|-----------|-------|
| **DASH (Disabilities of the Arm, Shoulder & Hand)** | 10–15 points | 12.7 | General upper limb | 30 items; 0=no disability, 100=most disabled |
| **QuickDASH** | 8–16 points | 15.9 | General upper limb | 11-item version; good for clinical use |
| **SPADI (Shoulder Pain & Disability Index)** | 8–18 points | 18 | Shoulder conditions | Pain + disability subscales (0–100) |
| **Oxford Shoulder Score (OSS)** | 5 points | — | Shoulder surgery/OA | 12 items; 0=worst, 48=best; validated for shoulder OA |
| **Numeric Pain Rating Scale (NPRS)** | 2 points | 1.74 | All | 0–10; use at every assessment |

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### Imaging Guidelines

| Modality | Indication | Findings |
|----------|-----------|---------|
| **X-ray (AP + Zanca 10° cephalic tilt)** | First-line; trauma/OA | CC distance (normal 11–13 mm), joint space narrowing, osteolysis, stress views for instability |
| **Ultrasound** | Dynamic assessment, guided injection | Joint effusion, capsular thickening, distal clavicle changes |
| **MRI** | Soft tissue assessment, planned surgery | CC ligament integrity, labral pathology, bone oedema (osteolysis) |
| **CT** | Complex/comminuted fractures, pre-surgical | Clavicle fracture pattern, posterior displacement (Grade IV) |

> **Zanca View:** 10–15° cephalic tilt reduces overlap with spine; reduces beam by 50% for better joint detail. Preferred over standard AP for ACJ assessment.

### Bilateral Stress Views
Weight-bearing X-rays with 10 lb weights hung from wrists: identifies Grade III vs IV by measuring CC distance bilaterally. Now used selectively (not routinely).

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Key Differentiating Features | Investigation |
|-----------|------------------------------|--------------|
| **Subacromial impingement** | Painful arc 60–120°, positive Neer/Hawkins | USS, MRI |
| **Rotator cuff tear** | Weakness on resisted rotation, positive drop arm | USS/MRI |
| **SLAP lesion** | O'Brien's positive with deep shoulder pain, biceps involvement | MRI arthrogram |
| **Sternoclavicular joint pathology** | Pain medial to ACJ, SCJ tenderness, swelling | X-ray, CT |
| **Cervical radiculopathy (C4–C5)** | Dermatomal distribution, neck movement reproduces symptoms | Cervical assessment, MRI |
| **Inflammatory arthropathy** | Bilateral, morning stiffness, systemic features | Blood (CRP, RF, anti-CCP), rheumatology |
| **Thoracic outlet syndrome** | Vascular/neurogenic symptoms with provocation | EAST test, vascular studies |
| **Clavicle stress fracture** | Localised clavicle tenderness, overhead athletes | X-ray, MRI bone oedema |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Use the current local shoulder pathway. Urgent escalation is based on neurovascular compromise, open injury, severe traumatic deformity or associated fracture/dislocation. Other ACJ presentations may begin with individualised conservative management when appropriate.

---

### Conservative Management (Grades I–III)

**Acute Phase (Week 0–3):**
- A sling may be used briefly for comfort after injury; avoid unnecessary prolonged immobilisation and progress according to symptoms and injury stability
- Ice (10–15 min, 3–4× daily), activity modification
- Analgesia (see pharmacological)
- Patient education: expected recovery timeline, activity modification, return-to-sport guidance

**Rehabilitation Phase (Week 2–12):**
- **Range of motion**: pendular exercises → active-assisted → full active ROM
- **Rotator cuff strengthening**: isometric → isotonic (bands → free weights)
- **Periscapular strengthening**: lower trapezius, serratus anterior — critical for ACJ load reduction
- **Proprioception / neuromuscular training**: closed-chain progression
- **Sport-specific rehabilitation**: graded return to impact/contact

**Evidence Base:**
- Mouhsine et al. (2003): Grade I–III — 90% satisfaction with conservative management at 2 years
- Mazzocca et al. (2011): No significant difference in outcomes between surgical and conservative Grade III at 5 years

### ACJ OA / Distal Clavicle Osteolysis
- **Load modification**: reduce overhead pressing, heavy bench press (modify grip width — wider grip reduces ACJ stress)
- **Periscapular strengthening** as above
- **Manual therapy**: ACJ mobilisation (Grade III–IV Maitland); may reduce pain (limited RCT evidence)
- **Distal clavicle osteolysis**: relative rest from causative activity for 3–6 months; most resolve

### Pharmacological Management

Analgesia and any AC-joint injection are prescriber/injection-service decisions. No NICE shoulder guideline has been identified that supports the previous fixed doses, duration, PPI logic, injection composition or annual limit; those details have been removed pending exact claim-source review.

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency/urgent assessment:** neurovascular compromise, open injury, threatened skin, severe traumatic ACJ injury or associated fracture/dislocation.
- **Specialist assessment:** persistent disabling symptoms, diagnostic uncertainty or a presentation requiring consideration of operative management.
- **Medical/rheumatology assessment:** systemic inflammatory features, infection concern or suspected malignancy under applicable criteria.

No fixed “within days”, injection-count, 6–12-week or 3–6-month threshold is asserted.

---

### Surgical Options (for reference)
- **Grade III**: ACCR (acromioclavicular coracoclavicular reconstruction) or Weaver-Dunn procedure
- **ACJ OA / Osteolysis**: Distal clavicle excision (Mumford procedure) — arthroscopic or open; high satisfaction rates (85–92%)

---

## Key References

1. **Rockwood CA, Williams GR, Young DC** (1998). Injuries to the acromioclavicular joint. *Fractures in Adults*. 4th ed. Lippincott-Raven. *(Classification system)*

2. **Hegedus EJ, Goode AP, Cook CE, et al.** (2012). Which physical examination tests provide clinicians with the most value when examining the shoulder? Update of a systematic review with meta-analysis of individual tests. *British Journal of Sports Medicine*, 46(14), 964–978. *(Special test accuracy)*

3. **Walton J, Mahajan S, Paxinos A, et al.** (2004). Diagnostic values of tests for acromioclavicular joint pain. *Journal of Bone and Joint Surgery (Am)*, 86(4), 807–812. *(Paxinos test; diagnostic injection reference standard)*

4. **Mazzocca AD, Arciero RA, Bicos J** (2007). Evaluation and treatment of acromioclavicular joint injuries. *American Journal of Sports Medicine*, 35(2), 316–329. *(Comprehensive management review)*

5. **Chronopoulos E, Kim TK, Park HB, et al.** (2004). Diagnostic value of physical tests for isolated chronic acromioclavicular lesions. *American Journal of Sports Medicine*, 32(3), 655–661. *(Cross-body adduction, horizontal adduction)*

6. **Mouhsine E, Garofalo R, Crevoisier X, Farron A** (2003). Grade I and II acromioclavicular dislocations: Results of conservative treatment. *Journal of Shoulder and Elbow Surgery*, 12(6), 599–602.

7. **NICE Clinical Knowledge Summary: Shoulder Pain** (2023). Available at: cks.nice.org.uk/topics/shoulder-pain *(HSC NI-applicable guidance)*

8. **Lemos MJ** (1998). The evaluation and treatment of the injured acromioclavicular joint in athletes. *American Journal of Sports Medicine*, 26(1), 137–144.

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Adhesive capsulitis (frozen shoulder) is a painful, progressive condition characterised by diffuse fibrosis and contracture of the glenohumeral joint capsule, resulting in significant restriction of active and passive range of motion in a capsular pattern. It is a distinct clinical entity from other shoulder conditions and follows a broadly predictable natural history.

**Prevalence:**
- Lifetime prevalence: 2–5% of the general population
- Peak incidence: 40–60 years (rare <40 or >70)
- Female preponderance: approximately 60–70% female
- Second shoulder affected in 20–30% of patients (usually contralateral; simultaneous bilateral uncommon)

**Associated Conditions:**

| Condition | Relative Risk / Prevalence |
|-----------|---------------------------|
| Diabetes mellitus (type 1 and 2) | 3–5× increased risk; prevalence 10–20% in frozen shoulder |
| Thyroid disease (hypo- and hyperthyroid) | 2–3× increased risk |
| Dupuytren's contracture | Associated fibrotic diathesis |
| Parkinson's disease | Increased risk; may be early feature |
| Cardiac disease / post-cardiac surgery | Prolonged immobility |
| Post-stroke ipsilateral shoulder | Hemiplegia + immobility |
| Cervical disc disease | Neurogenic inflammation proposed |
| Post-operative immobilisation | Rotator cuff repair; mastectomy |
| Hyperlipidaemia | Emerging association |

### Pathophysiology

The pathological process involves:

1. **Inflammatory phase:** Synovial inflammation with fibroblast proliferation; increased vascularity; cytokine release (IL-1β, TNF-α, IL-6)
2. **Fibrotic phase:** Dense fibrocollagenous tissue formation; capsule thickening (normal 2–4 mm → up to 7–10 mm in contracted phase)
3. **Key site:** Rotator cuff interval (between supraspinatus and subscapularis) — contracture here produces external rotation restriction first
4. **Axillary recess obliteration:** Normal joint volume 28–30 mL reduced to <10 mL; arthrographic finding is diagnostic
5. **Contractile myofibroblasts:** Responsible for progressive capsular shortening; similar mechanism to Dupuytren's

### Clinical Stages (Reeves, 1975 — validated by Hannafin & Chiaia, 2000)

| Stage | Name | Duration | Key Features |
|-------|------|----------|--------------|
| **Stage 1** | Freezing (Painful) | 2–9 months | Gradual onset severe pain; pain at rest and night; ROM begins to restrict |
| **Stage 2** | Frozen (Stiffening) | 4–12 months | Pain decreasing; ROM increasingly restricted; significant disability |
| **Stage 3** | Thawing (Resolution) | 5–26 months | Pain minimal; ROM gradually returning; slow recovery |
| **Total** | Full natural history | 12–42 months | Variable; not all patients fully resolve |

**Prognosis:**
- 20–50% of patients have persistent symptoms or restriction at 2–3 years without treatment
- Diabetic patients: significantly worse prognosis; longer duration; less complete resolution
- Natural history is broadly positive but prolonged — active management improves speed of recovery

---

## Special Tests

| Test | Sensitivity | Specificity | +LR | -LR | Evidence Level | Notes |
|------|------------|------------|-----|-----|----------------|-------|
| **Passive ER Restriction** | 92% | 75% | 3.68 | 0.11 | High | Best individual test; ER most restricted in capsular pattern |
| **Passive Abduction Restriction** | 85% | 68% | 2.66 | 0.22 | High | Second most restricted; combined with ER restriction highly diagnostic |
| **Capsular Pattern (Cyriax)** | ~75% | ~90% | 7.5 | 0.28 | Moderate | ER > abduction > IR restriction (proportional loss); distinguishes from other shoulder conditions |
| **External Rotation <50% of opposite side** | 88% | 84% | 5.5 | 0.14 | Moderate | Practical clinical threshold for clinical diagnosis |
| **Shoulder Hand Test (Apley's Scratch)** | — | — | — | — | Observational | Functional ROM screen — hand behind head + hand behind back; asymmetry |
| **Empty Can** | Variable | Variable | — | — | Low | Often limited by pain/stiffness — not useful for diagnosis |
| **Hawkins-Kennedy** | Variable | Variable | — | — | Low | May be positive due to pain but ROM restriction is diagnostic feature |

**Clinical diagnosis is primarily pattern recognition:**
- Global restriction in capsular pattern
- History consistent with staged progression
- Passive ROM restriction in ER > abduction > IR
- Exclusion of glenohumeral arthritis (X-ray), rotator cuff tear (USS), and other causes

---

## Red Flags

Consider alternative or serious pathology when the presentation suggests infection, unreduced dislocation, tumour/malignancy, inflammatory polyarthritis or an acute traumatic rotator-cuff tear. Escalate according to the actual presentation.

Progressive focal neurological deficit requires prompt medical/specialist assessment. Age at onset alone does not mandate MRI, and Version 1 does not prescribe Paget-specific testing or a separate CRPS pathway without an appropriate clinical indication and source.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Hannafin & Chiaia Staging System (2000)
Correlates histological findings with clinical stages:
- **Stage 1:** Scattered hypervascular synovium; fibrinous exudate
- **Stage 2:** Hypertrophic synovitis; early fibroplasia
- **Stage 3:** Mild synovitis; dense subsynovial fibrosis; collagen remodelling
- **Stage 4:** Mature collagen; avascular hypocellular tissue; minimal synovitis

### Treatment Decision Framework Based on Stage:

| Stage | Dominant Problem | Priority Intervention | Caution |
|-------|-----------------|----------------------|---------|
| Stage 1 (Painful/irritable) | Pain; sleep disruption | Symptom control; consider glenohumeral corticosteroid injection for short-term improvement; tolerable ROM/exercise | Dose exercise to irritability rather than forcing painful end range |
| Stage 2 (Frozen) | Stiffness; functional limitation | ROM exercises; joint mobilisation; hydrodilatation | Continue pain management |
| Stage 3 (Thawing) | Persistent restriction | Progressive strengthening; capsular stretching | Normal loading; return to function |

### Biopsychosocial Considerations
- Prolonged pain and disability → psychological impact; depression screening (PHQ-9) in chronic cases
- Impact on work and activities of daily living — occupational advice important
- Fear-avoidance behaviours can slow recovery — address early

---

## Outcome Measures

| Measure | Score Range | MCID | Clinical Application |
|---------|-------------|------|---------------------|
| **SPADI** | 0–100 (0 = best) | 8–13 pts | Best validated for shoulder pain and disability; practical |
| **OSS (Oxford Shoulder Score)** | 0–48 (48 = best) | 5 pts | Widely used in NI orthopaedic pathway for frozen shoulder |
| **Constant-Murley Score** | 0–100 (100 = best) | 10–16 pts | Objective ROM component useful for frozen shoulder tracking |
| **QuickDASH** | 0–100 (0 = best) | 11–16 pts | Upper limb function; practical for serial assessment |
| **ASES** | 0–100 (100 = best) | 12–17 pts | Post-procedural assessment (injection/hydrodilatation/MUA) |
| **ROM measurement** | Degrees | ER MCID: ~9°; Flex MCID: ~11° | Essential — sequential ROM measurement is primary outcome marker |

**Practical recommendation:** SPADI + Constant-Murley (ROM components) at baseline and follow-up. Serial ROM measurements (ER, abduction, flexion, IR) essential for monitoring stage progression.

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### Clinical Diagnosis

Frozen shoulder remains a clinical diagnosis based on:
1. Characteristic history (gradual onset pain; progressive stiffness)
2. Capsular pattern restriction on passive examination
3. Exclusion of other pathology

**Arthrography:** Historically used to assess capsular restriction but now largely replaced; it is not required for routine clinical diagnosis.

**USS:**
- Supports diagnosis by excluding structural pathology (RC tear, calcific tendinitis)
- May show thickened rotator cuff interval; increased blood flow (power Doppler)
- Cannot confirm capsular volume reduction directly

**MRI:**
- Thickened rotator cuff interval capsule; enhancing synovium on gadolinium
- Differentiates from glenohumeral OA; identifies associated pathology
- Not routinely required for clinical diagnosis

**X-ray:** Usually normal in primary frozen shoulder — important to exclude glenohumeral OA, calcific tendinitis, malignancy

### Key Evidence for Management:

**Corticosteroid injection:**
- Buchbinder et al. Cochrane 2003; Carette et al. (2003) NEJM: Short-term benefit (up to 6 weeks) — Level I
- Intra-articular corticosteroid superior to physiotherapy alone at 6 weeks; no difference at 12 months
- Best in Stage 1 (freezing) — reduces inflammatory component and provides window for rehabilitation

**Hydrodilatation (arthrographic distension):**
- Valadie et al. (2000); Buchbinder et al. (2004) BMJ: Significant short-term benefit over placebo
- Cochrane (Maund et al., 2012): Hydrodilatation + steroid superior to steroid alone at 6 weeks
- Hydrodilatation technique, medicines and volume are specialist/injection-service decisions; no fixed protocol is prescribed here
- Available via MSK interface services in some HSC NI trusts

**Manipulation Under Anaesthesia (MUA):**
- Evidence: retrospective series; no high-quality RCTs
- Effective for Stage 2/3 — breaks down adhesions; risk of fracture, nerve injury, haemarthrosis
- Post-MUA physiotherapy essential — begin same day

**Arthroscopic capsular release:**
- For refractory cases (>6 months no improvement); excellent outcomes reported (>90% satisfaction)
- Systematic review (Omari & Bunker, 2001): superior to MUA for recovery speed
- Available at NI orthopaedic centres

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Diagnosis | Key Differentiating Features | Differentiating Assessment |
|-----------|------------------------------|---------------------------|
| Glenohumeral osteoarthritis | Older age; crepitus; X-ray changes; gradual onset | X-ray — joint space loss, osteophytes |
| Rotator cuff tear with secondary stiffness | Lag signs; weakness; USS/MRI confirms tear | USS — full-thickness tear |
| Subacromial pain syndrome | Painful arc; strength variable; NOT globally restricted | ROM testing — not capsular pattern |
| Calcific tendinitis | Acute severe pain; X-ray calcification; moves through | X-ray — calcification |
| Glenohumeral inflammatory arthritis (RA/crystal) | Bilateral; systemic features; inflammatory bloods | Bloods (RF, anti-CCP, urate); X-ray |
| Shoulder impingement + secondary guarding | Painful arc; resisted tests positive; ROM pain-limited not structurally restricted | Passive ROM under distraction/supine |
| Avascular necrosis (AVN) | History steroids, alcohol, haemoglobinopathy; deep aching | MRI — AVN signal |
| Posterior shoulder dislocation (missed) | Trauma; fixed IR; limited ER; BEWARE | X-ray (axillary view essential); CT |
| Complex Regional Pain Syndrome | Vasomotor; trophic changes; allodynia; disproportionate | Specialist assessment |
| Cervical radiculopathy | Neck symptoms; referred pain; neurological signs | Cervical exam; ULTT; Spurling's |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Use the current BESS/local frozen-shoulder pathway. After serious or alternative pathology has been considered, care may include education, exercise and other individually assessed nonsurgical options. Investigation and intervention choices remain clinician/specialist-led.

---

### Conservative Management

**Stage 1 (Painful/Freezing) — Priority: pain management:**
- Education: condition, stages, natural history, prognosis
- Sleep position advice (pillow under arm; avoid lying on affected side)
- Dose stretching and ROM to irritability; do not force painful end range in Stage 1
- Gentle pendulum exercises; active-assisted ROM in pain-free range
- Pain-free active exercises (isometric, low-load)
- Early injection referral for pain management

**Stage 2 (Frozen/Stiffening) — Priority: ROM restoration:**

| Technique | Evidence | Dosage |
|-----------|----------|--------|
| Passive ROM exercises | Moderate | 3×10 reps, 3×/day within pain tolerance |
| Hold-relax (contract-relax) stretching | Moderate | 5 sec contract; 10 sec passive stretch; 3× each direction |
| Glenohumeral joint mobilisation (Grade III–IV) | Moderate | Posterior capsule; inferior glide |
| Physiotherapy-supervised stretching programme | High | 2–3× per week supervised + home programme |
| Codman's (pendulum) exercises | Low-moderate | Daily |

**Stage 3 (Thawing/Resolution) — Priority: function and strength:**
- Progressive strengthening: rotator cuff; scapular stabilisers
- Return to overhead activities: graded exposure
- Occupational/work activities — graded return
- Prognosis counselling — complete resolution may take 2+ years

### Pharmacological and injection care

The BESS/BOA Frozen Shoulder pathway supports individualised analgesia/NSAID consideration, intra-articular corticosteroid injection and physiotherapy in appropriate patients, but it does not validate every drug, dose, repeat-injection limit or hydrodilatation protocol previously stated here. Prescribing and injection details remain medical/injection-service decisions pending claim-level review.

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Urgent assessment:** infection, unreduced dislocation, acute traumatic cuff-tear presentation, neurovascular compromise or another serious presentation.
- **Seek medical/rheumatology assessment:** when inflammatory polyarthritis or systemic disease is suspected.
- **Consider specialist shoulder assessment:** when diagnosis remains uncertain or disabling restriction persists despite an appropriate conservative plan.

Investigation and intervention choices remain clinician/specialist-led; no universal fixed timeframe is asserted.

---

## Key References

1. Buchbinder R, Green S, Forbes A, Hall S, Lawler G. Arthrographic joint distension with saline and steroid improves function and reduces pain in patients with painful stiff shoulder: results of a randomised, double blind, placebo controlled trial. *Ann Rheum Dis.* 2004;63(3):302–309.
2. Carette S, Moffet H, Tardif J, et al. Intraarticular corticosteroids, supervised physiotherapy, or a combination of the two in the treatment of adhesive capsulitis of the shoulder. *Arthritis Rheum.* 2003;48(3):829–838.
3. Hannafin JA, Chiaia TA. Adhesive capsulitis: a treatment approach. *Clin Orthop Relat Res.* 2000;(372):95–109.
4. Maund E, Craig D, Suekarran S, et al. Management of frozen shoulder: a systematic review and cost-effectiveness analysis. *Health Technol Assess.* 2012;16(11):1–264.
5. Reeves B. The natural history of the frozen shoulder syndrome. *Scand J Rheumatol.* 1975;4(4):193–196.
6. BESS/BOA. *Frozen Shoulder Patient Care Pathway*. [BESS patient care pathways](https://bess.ac.uk/patient-care-pathways-and-guidelines/).
7. Rangan A, Hanchard N, McDaid C. What is the most effective treatment for frozen shoulder? *BMJ.* 2016;354:i4162.
8. Uppal HS, Evans JP, Smith C. Frozen shoulder: A systematic review of therapeutic options. *World J Orthop.* 2015;6(2):263–268.
9. Omari A, Bunker TD. Open surgical release for frozen shoulder: surgical findings and results of the release. *J Shoulder Elbow Surg.* 2001;10(4):353–357.
10. Lewis J. Frozen shoulder contracture syndrome: Aetiology, mystification, and a proposed evidence-based approach. *Man Ther.* 2015;20(1):2–9.

12. Prestgaard T, Wormgoor ME, Haugen S, Harstad H, Mowinckel P, Brox JI. Ultrasound-guided intra-articular and rotator interval corticosteroid injections in adhesive capsulitis of the shoulder: a double-blind, sham-controlled randomized study. *Pain.* 2015;156(9):1683–1691.
