### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Calcific tendinitis (CT) of the shoulder is characterised by the deposition of **calcium hydroxyapatite crystals** within the rotator cuff tendons, most commonly the supraspinatus (80–90% of cases), followed by infraspinatus (~10%) and subscapularis (~5%). It affects 2.7–8% of the adult population, with peak incidence in the 4th–5th decades, and a female predominance (F:M ≈ 2:1).

### Pathophysiology: Uhthoff Model

The most widely accepted pathophysiological model is **Uhthoff & Loehr's (1997) cell-mediated theory** — a fibrocartilaginous transformation of tendon tissue (not a degenerative process), distinct from dystrophic calcification seen in chronic tendinopathy.

**Uhthoff Stages of Calcific Tendinitis:**

| Stage | Phase | Histology | Clinical Features | Duration |
|-------|-------|-----------|-------------------|----------|
| **Pre-calcific** | Fibrocartilaginous transformation | Chondrocytic metaplasia of tendon fibrocytes; no calcium yet | Often asymptomatic; possible mild aching | Months |
| **Calcific — Formative** | Crystal deposition | Calcium crystals deposited in matrix vesicles; chalk-like, soft consistency | Mild–moderate pain; often episodic | Weeks–months |
| **Calcific — Resting** | Quiescent | Calcium consolidates; vascular ingrowth begins | Variable — may be asymptomatic | Variable |
| **Calcific — Resorptive** | ⚠️ Most painful | Phagocytic resorption; toothpaste-like consistency; highly vascular; intense local inflammation | **Severe acute pain**; often the presenting crisis | Days–weeks |
| **Post-calcific** | Remodelling | Fibroblast invasion; collagen remodelling; calcium resolves | Pain subsides; function recovers | Weeks–months |

> **Key clinical implication:** The **resorptive phase** is the most acutely painful (often presenting as apparent 'pseudo-acute' shoulder). Paradoxically, this phase has the best prognosis as calcium is being actively resorbed. Intervention (aspiration/barbotage) is most effective and technically easiest in this phase (fluid/toothpaste consistency).

**Gärtner Radiographic Classification:**

| Type | Appearance | Phase Correlation | Clinical Note |
|------|-----------|-------------------|---------------|
| **Type I** | Dense, homogeneous, well-defined border | Formative/resting | Harder; less likely to resolve spontaneously |
| **Type II** | Dense/translucent, heterogeneous or poorly defined | Transitional | Variable behaviour |
| **Type III** | Translucent, heterogeneous, poorly defined ('fluffy') | Resorptive | Soft; most amenable to barbotage; more likely to resolve spontaneously |

**Why calcium deposits in the critical zone:**
The supraspinatus is most affected in its "critical zone" — 1 cm proximal to the insertion — an area of relative avascularity and high mechanical stress (Rathbun & Macnab, 1970). Hypoxic conditions may trigger fibrocartilaginous metaplasia and initiate the calcification cascade.

**Spontaneous resolution rate:** 27–50% of deposits resolve within 3 years without intervention. Natural history is generally favourable, but 20–30% of patients develop chronic, recalcitrant symptoms.

---

## Special Tests

> **Clinical Note:** No shoulder special test is specific for calcific tendinitis. Tests below detect subacromial impingement and rotator cuff pathology — calcific tendinitis should be confirmed by imaging. A positive impingement cluster with acute-onset severe pain warrants X-ray.

### Impingement & Rotator Cuff Tests

| Assessment | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Painful arc, Neer and Hawkins-Kennedy | May reproduce subacromial pain | Non-specific for calcific tendinopathy |
| Empty/full can and lag/drop-arm tests | Explore rotator-cuff function and possible tear features | Do not confirm a calcium deposit |
| Cross-body adduction | Explores AC-joint contribution | Limited discrimination in isolation |

Use special-test findings as part of the differential examination. Imaging confirms the presence and location of a calcium deposit when that information will change management.

> **Calcific tendinitis-specific clinical pattern:** Sudden onset of exquisite shoulder pain (often overnight or following minor activity), severe movement restriction, marked tenderness over supraspinatus insertion, presentation mimicking septic arthritis. X-ray confirms diagnosis.

---

## Red Flags

Escalate genuine systemic/infective features, suspected malignancy, acute neurological deficit, fracture/dislocation or another serious presentation according to clinical urgency. Progressive focal weakness requires prompt medical/specialist assessment.

Version 1 does not infer reactive arthritis from recent dental, gastrointestinal or urinary symptoms, mandate MRI after a fixed interval, or prescribe a cortical-erosion pathway without separate evidence.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Diagnostic Framework

```
Acute/subacute shoulder pain, particularly if severe, sudden onset
     ↓
Screen red flags → if septic arthritis suspected → A&E SAME DAY
     ↓
Physical Examination:
  • Restricted ROM (all planes or primarily in arc 60–120°)
  • Tenderness over supraspinatus insertion (2 cm distal to anterior acromion)
  • Positive impingement signs (Neer, Hawkins-Kennedy)
  • Intact strength (unless pain-inhibited or concurrent RC tear)
     ↓
Plain X-ray (AP + axillary + outlet views) ← FIRST-LINE IMAGING
     ↓
Calcium deposit identified           No calcium identified
Classify: Gärtner I/II/III           → Consider USS/MRI for RC tear,
                                        AC pathology, other diagnosis
     ↓
Determine clinical phase (Uhthoff)
  Resorptive (Gärtner III, severe pain) → USS-guided barbotage ± aspiration
  Formative/Resting (Gärtner I/II) → Conservative physiotherapy → ESWT if failure
```

### Treatment Decision Tree (HSC NI)

```
Diagnosis confirmed (X-ray)
     ↓
Phase 1: Conservative (weeks 0–12)
  • Analgesia: NSAIDs + paracetamol
  • Subacromial corticosteroid injection (acute pain / resorptive phase)
  • Supervised physiotherapy
     ↓
Reassess at 6–12 weeks (X-ray + outcome measures)
     ↓
     Improving                            Not improving / resorptive phase
        ↓                                          ↓
  Continue rehab +               ESWT (1–3 sessions) OR USS-guided
  reassess at 3 months           barbotage/aspiration (if Gärtner III)
                                           ↓
                                Reassess at 3 months post-intervention
                                           ↓
                                     Persistent symptoms
                                     Arthroscopic removal
                                     (rare; <10% of cases)
```

---

## Outcome Measures

| Measure | MCID | MDC | Score Range | Best For | Validated? |
|---------|------|-----|-------------|----------|------------|
| **SPADI (Shoulder Pain & Disability Index)** | 8–13 points | 18 points | 0–100 (0=best) | **Recommended first-line** for CT; pain-heavy presentations; highly responsive to change | ✅ Yes |
| **DASH (Disabilities of Arm, Shoulder, Hand)** | 10.8 points | 12.7 points | 0–100 (0=best) | General UE function; good for tracking functional recovery across treatment phases | ✅ Yes |
| **QuickDASH** | 14.9–15.9 points | — | 0–100 (0=best) | Efficient screening at each contact; 11-item version | ✅ Yes |
| **OSS (Oxford Shoulder Score)** | 5 points | — | 0–48 (48=best) | Pre/post-op comparison; NHS standard for surgical referrals | ✅ Yes |
| **ASES (American Shoulder & Elbow Surgeons)** | 6.4–17 points | — | 0–100 (100=best) | Research; surgical outcomes | ✅ Yes |
| **NRS Pain (0–10)** | 1.5–2 points | — | 0–10 (0=no pain) | Rapid pain monitoring; supplement to functional measure | ✅ Yes |

> **HSC NI Practice Recommendation:** Use **SPADI** as primary outcome measure for calcific tendinitis (pain-dominant condition; more responsive than DASH in acute presentations). Supplement with **NRS pain** at each contact. Re-measure SPADI at 6 weeks, 12 weeks, and post-intervention.

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### Imaging Comparison

| Modality | Sensitivity | Specificity | Cost | First-Line? | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------------|------|-------------|-------|
| **Plain X-ray (AP + outlet)** | 66–93% | ~99% | Low | ✅ Yes | Mandatory first-line; identifies deposit, size, morphology (Gärtner classification) |
| **Ultrasound (USS)** | 79–98% | 88–99% | Low-Moderate | ✅ Yes (adjunct) | Dynamic assessment; guides intervention (barbotage); detects concurrent RC tear; operator-dependent |
| **MRI** | 70–82% | 98% | High | No (second-line) | Best for soft tissue; concurrent pathology (RC tear, AC joint); calcium may appear as signal void |
| **CT** | ~100% | ~100% | High/Radiation | No | Rarely needed; useful pre-op planning for large/complex deposits |

**Preferred imaging approach (HSC NI):**
1. **Plain X-ray** — confirm diagnosis, classify deposit (Gärtner I/II/III), measure size
2. **USS** — if intervention planned, or if concurrent RC pathology suspected (complementary to X-ray)
3. **MRI** — if X-ray/USS inconclusive, suspected full-thickness RC tear, or pre-surgical assessment

**Deposit characteristics associated with spontaneous resolution:**
- Gärtner Type III ('fluffy', poorly defined)
- Smaller deposits (<1.5 cm)
- Resorptive phase clinically (acute severe pain)

**Deposit characteristics predicting recalcitrance:**
- Gärtner Type I (dense, homogeneous, well-defined)
- Deposits >2 cm
- Long symptom duration (>12 months)
- Bursal-side deposits

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Differentiating Features | Key Tests | Imaging |
|-----------|--------------------------|-----------|---------|
| **Subacromial Impingement (without calcification)** | Painful arc; similar impingement signs; no sudden onset crisis; gradual onset | Neer, Hawkins-Kennedy, Empty Can | USS/MRI; X-ray normal or minor degenerative changes |
| **Rotator Cuff Tear (full-thickness)** | Weakness > pain (full thickness); Drop Arm positive; ER lag sign; older age | Drop Arm, ER Lag Sign, Belly Press | USS/MRI confirms; X-ray shows superior migration if massive |
| **Adhesive Capsulitis** | Global passive restriction (all planes); ER most restricted; capsular pattern | Capsular pattern ROM testing | MRI (capsular thickening, reduced joint volume); X-ray normal |
| **Septic Arthritis** | Fever, elevated CRP/ESR/WBC; erythema; systemically unwell; rapid onset | FBC/CRP/ESR, joint aspiration | USS shows effusion; X-ray may be normal early |
| **Acromioclavicular Joint Pathology** | Pain at AC joint; cross-body adduction positive; no calcium in supraspinatus | Cross-body adduction, AC palpation, O'Brien's (top pain) | X-ray AC views; USS |
| **Pseudogout (CPPD)** | Often older patient; knee/wrist also affected; synovial fluid analysis confirms | Joint aspiration — positive birefringent crystals | X-ray — chondrocalcinosis in fibrocartilage |
| **Cervical Radiculopathy (C5)** | Dermatomal radiation; neck movement provokes; reduced biceps reflex | Spurling's, ULTT, neurological exam | Cervical MRI |
| **Glenohumeral Osteoarthritis** | Older patient; global ROM loss; crepitus; stiffness > pain; end-stage | Passive ROM, crepitus | X-ray (joint space loss, osteophytes, subchondral sclerosis) |
| **Reactive Arthritis** | Preceding infection; other joints affected; systemic features | HLA-B27, ESR/CRP, urine culture | X-ray; MRI |
| **Os Acromiale (unfused secondary ossification centre)** | Young adult; anterior acromial tenderness; acromion mobile; X-ray diagnostic | X-ray (axillary view critical) | CT for surgical planning |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Begin with clinically appropriate conservative care when serious pathology has been excluded. NICE HTG645 retains extracorporeal shockwave therapy for calcific tendinopathy in research-only governance. Specialist procedures require separate assessment and evidence.

---

### Conservative Management

**Physiotherapy Principles:**

| Phase | Timeframe | Goals | Interventions |
|-------|-----------|-------|---------------|
| **Acute (Resorptive)** | Weeks 0–4 | Pain control; maintain ROM; patient education | Pendulum exercises; cryotherapy; gentle passive ROM; avoid provocative overhead loading |
| **Subacute** | Weeks 4–8 | Progressive ROM; begin RC strengthening | Active ROM; scapular stabilisation; isometric RC; posterior capsule stretching |
| **Functional** | Weeks 8–12+ | Full strength; return to function/work | Progressive resistance; proprioception; functional tasks; sport-specific if applicable |

**Manual Therapy:**
- Joint mobilisation (GH and thoracic) has low-level evidence as adjunct to exercise in subacromial pain — included in multimodal approach
- **Not recommended** as primary or standalone treatment

**Exercise:**
- Supervised exercise superior to home exercise alone for subacromial conditions
- Scapular stabilisation and rotator cuff strengthening are cornerstones
- Avoid provocative overhead loading in acute/resorptive phase

### Pharmacological

Analgesia, corticosteroid injection and any off-label medicine are prescriber/injection-service decisions. The previous fixed doses, durations, annual injection limits and opioid/colchicine suggestions did not have an exact authoritative claim-source relationship and have been removed pending human evidence review.

### Interventional: ESWT (Extracorporeal Shock Wave Therapy)

**Mechanism:** Acoustic pressure waves → mechanical disruption of calcium deposits → stimulate phagocytic resorption + neovascularisation

**Current NICE HTG645 position:** evidence of efficacy is inadequate in quality and quantity. ESWT for calcific tendinopathy in the shoulder should be used only in the context of research. The cited trials may explain why the procedure has been studied, but they do not override this current UK governance position or establish a routine protocol.

**ESWT contraindications:** Coagulation disorders/anticoagulants, local infection, malignancy, pregnancy, pacemaker, bony deposits overlying neurovascular structures

### Interventional: USS-Guided Barbotage / Needling & Lavage

**Mechanism:** Direct mechanical disruption of calcium deposit via needle; lavage removes calcium debris; induces local inflammatory response to accelerate resorption.

**Evidence Summary:**

| Parameter | Data |
|-----------|------|
| **Efficacy** | Significant pain reduction; superior to corticosteroid injection alone (de Witte et al., 2012) |
| **Best phase** | Resorptive (Gärtner III) — calcium has toothpaste-like consistency; easier aspiration |
| **Technique** | The specialist service determines image guidance, equipment, lavage method and medicines; no fixed procedure is prescribed here |
| **Combined with corticosteroid** | Corticosteroid injection after barbotage standard practice for post-procedure flare management |
| **Key RCT** | Randomised trial (de Witte et al., 2012): barbotage + corticosteroid vs corticosteroid alone — ASES score superior in barbotage arm at 12 months |
| **Success rate** | 60–80% significant improvement at 12 months; deposit clearance in 30–70% |
| **Local access** | Use the current local imaging or specialist pathway; do not assume a mandatory imaging step without checking the service criteria |

### Surgical Management (Arthroscopic Removal)

**Indication:** Specialist assessment after failed conservative management; ESWT cannot be treated as a required routine prerequisite while NICE restricts it to research.

**Specialist procedure:** Operative management may be considered in selected refractory cases. The surgeon determines the procedure after clinical and imaging review.

**Evidence:**
- Success rate 85–90% at 2 years
- This learner page does not specify an acromioplasty indication or estimate how often surgery is required
- Short-stay day-case procedure; physiotherapy essential post-operatively

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Urgent assessment:** infection/systemic illness, acute neurological deficit, fracture/dislocation or another serious presentation.
- **Suspected malignancy:** follow the current suspected-cancer pathway only when applicable NICE/local criteria are met.
- **Consider specialist shoulder assessment:** for persistent disabling symptoms, diagnostic uncertainty or when a specialist procedure is being considered.

No fixed imaging or procedure timeline is asserted.

---

## Key References

1. **Uhthoff HK, Loehr JW.** Calcific tendinopathy of the rotator cuff: pathogenesis, diagnosis, and management. *J Am Acad Orthop Surg.* 1997;5(4):183–191. *(Uhthoff model — foundational pathophysiology paper)*

2. **Gärtner J, Heyer A.** Calcific tendinitis of the shoulder. *Orthopade.* 1995;24(3):284–302. *(Radiographic classification — Gärtner Types I–III)*

3. **Rathbun JB, Macnab I.** The microvascular pattern of the rotator cuff. *J Bone Joint Surg Br.* 1970;52(3):540–553. *(Critical zone avascularity — historical basis)*

4. **Gerdesmeyer L, Wagenpfeil S, Haake M, et al.** Extracorporeal shock wave therapy for the treatment of chronic calcifying tendinitis of the rotator cuff: a randomized controlled trial. *JAMA.* 2003;290(19):2573–2580. *(Level I RCT — ESWT vs sham; n=144)*

5. **de Witte PB, Selten JW, Navas A, et al.** Calcific tendinitis of the rotator cuff: a randomized controlled trial of ultrasound-guided needling and lavage versus subacromial corticosteroids. *Am J Sports Med.* 2012;40(6):1419–1427. *(Level I RCT — barbotage vs corticosteroid alone)*

6. **Louwerens JK, Sierevelt IN, van Noort A, van den Bekerom MP.** Evidence for minimally invasive therapies in the management of chronic calcific tendinopathy of the rotator cuff: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *J Shoulder Elbow Surg.* 2014;23(8):1240–1249. *(Systematic review — ESWT and barbotage evidence)*

7. **Hegedus EJ, Goode A, Campbell S, et al.** Physical examination tests of the shoulder: a systematic review with meta-analysis of individual tests. *Br J Sports Med.* 2008;42(2):80–92. *(Special test diagnostic accuracy — Neer, Hawkins-Kennedy, Painful Arc)*

8. **Ark JW, Flock TJ, Flatow EL, Bigliani LU.** Arthroscopic treatment of calcific tendinitis of the shoulder. *Arthroscopy.* 1992;8(2):183–188. *(Surgical outcomes data)*

9. **Merolla G, Singh S, Paladini P, Porcellini G.** Calcific tendinitis of the rotator cuff: state of the art in diagnosis and treatment. *J Orthop Traumatol.* 2016;17(1):7–14. *(Comprehensive clinical review)*

10. **Cohen M, Kalichman L.** Calcific tendinitis of the shoulder: clinical diagnosis and treatment. *Harefuah.* 2013;152(11):658–662.

11. **Oudelaar BW, Schepers-Bok R, Ooms EM, Nelissen RG, Reijnierse M.** Needle aspiration of calcific deposits (NACD) is more effective than ultrasound-guided needling (UGN) but less effective than high-energy extracorporeal shockwave therapy (ESWT) for the treatment of rotator cuff calcific tendinitis. *Eur Radiol.* 2016;26(7):2226–2234.

12. **NICE HealthTech guidance HTG645.** *Extracorporeal shockwave therapy for calcific tendinopathy in the shoulder*. Recommendations restrict use to research because efficacy evidence is inadequate. [NICE HTG645](https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/htg645)

13. **Banerjee S, Gench NK, Bhattacharyya R, et al.** BESS consensus statement on the management of calcific tendinitis of the shoulder. *Shoulder Elbow.* 2022;14(3):247–257. *(BESS guideline — UK practice)*

14. **Papacrhistou G, Anagnostou S.** Calcific deposits in rotator cuff tendons: a review. *J Bone Joint Surg.* 2001;83-B(Suppl 1):2–4.

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Rotator cuff tears are among the most clinically significant shoulder conditions encountered in musculoskeletal physiotherapy. They represent a spectrum from partial-thickness tears through full-thickness (complete) tears to massive rotator cuff tears involving multiple tendons. The condition sits at the severe end of the tendinopathy continuum model.

**Prevalence:**
- Asymptomatic tears identified in 22% of adults overall; rises to 54% in those >60 years (Yamamoto et al., 2010)
- Symptomatic full-thickness tears: ~2–3% of the general population
- Prevalence doubles each decade after age 50
- Bilateral tears in ~35% of those with symptomatic unilateral tear

**Classification:**

| Classification | Description |
|---------------|-------------|
| **Partial-thickness** | Bursal-sided, articular-sided (PASTA lesion), or intratendinous |
| **Full-thickness small** | <1 cm; typically supraspinatus |
| **Full-thickness medium** | 1–3 cm |
| **Full-thickness large** | 3–5 cm |
| **Massive** | >5 cm or involving ≥2 tendons |
| **Irreparable massive** | With muscle atrophy, fatty infiltration (Goutallier Grade 3–4) |

### Pathophysiology

**Degenerative tears (>90% of all RC tears):**
- Culmination of the continuum model — reactive → dysrepair → degenerative tendinopathy → tear
- Critical zone of supraspinatus (1 cm from insertion): zone of relative avascularity
- Fatty infiltration (Goutallier classification) correlates with chronicity and repair prognosis
- Increasing tear size over time in 40–80% of untreated full-thickness tears (Moosmayer et al., 2013)

**Traumatic tears:**
- Acute forced abduction/ER against resistance (e.g., fall on outstretched arm)
- More common in younger patients with previously healthy tendons
- More likely surgically repairable; better healing rates

### Relevant Anatomy

- **Rotator cuff interval:** Between supraspinatus and subscapularis; contains biceps tendon and coracohumeral ligament; disruption affects inferior capsule stability
- **Subscapularis:** Largest and strongest cuff muscle; anterior stabiliser; tears often missed clinically
- **Fatty infiltration (Goutallier):** Grade 0 (normal) → Grade 4 (fat > muscle); Grade ≥3 = poor surgical prognosis
- **Pseudoparalysis:** Inability to actively elevate arm despite passive ROM due to massive cuff tear

---

## Special Tests

### Supraspinatus Tests

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Empty/full can | Explores supraspinatus-related pain and weakness | Pain or weakness is not specific for a tear |
| Drop arm and painful arc | Explore marked functional deficit and subacromial pain | Interpret with trauma history, active/passive movement and strength |

### Infraspinatus / Teres Minor Tests (ER weakness)

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| External-rotation lag, Hornblower's and external-rotation strength | Explore posterosuperior cuff dysfunction | A positive finding requires correlation with the whole assessment |

### Subscapularis Tests

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Lift-off, belly-press, bear-hug and internal-rotation lag | Explore subscapularis dysfunction | No one test establishes a tear in isolation |

### Full-Thickness Tear Pattern

The following combination may increase concern for a substantial tear, particularly after trauma, but is not a standalone diagnostic rule:
- Age >60
- Positive supraspinatus weakness
- Positive impingement sign
- Night pain

---

## Red Flags

- Chest, jaw or arm symptoms with dyspnoea or other acute cardiac features require emergency assessment/999.
- Marked acute weakness or pseudoparalysis after trauma requires urgent shoulder assessment for possible acute rotator-cuff tear or fracture.
- Fracture/dislocation, neurovascular compromise, infection or malignancy concern requires escalation according to the presentation.

Do not make ultrasound or MRI automatic in the learner wording; investigation is determined by the assessing clinician and pathway.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Surgical Decision Framework (Kuhn et al.)

The decision between conservative and surgical management for rotator cuff tears is specialist-led and considers:

1. **Tear characteristics:** Size, location, fatty infiltration (Goutallier), tendon retraction
2. **Patient factors:** Age, activity level, hand dominance, comorbidities
3. **Response to conservative management:** 3–6 months' trial first for most tears
4. **Chronicity:** An acute traumatic presentation with substantial weakness warrants timely specialist assessment

**Prognosis for conservative management:**
- Structured rehabilitation can be appropriate for selected tears; the treatment choice depends on the individual clinical and imaging context
- Massive tears: poorer conservative outcomes; augmentation strategies (superior capsule reconstruction, tendon transfer) emerging

### Lewis Framework Applied to RC Tears

- Differentiate symptomatic from asymptomatic (prevalence of asymptomatic tears is high)
- Functional deficit is the key clinical driver — not imaging findings alone
- Imaging should not drive surgery unless functional deficit correlates

---

## Outcome Measures

| Measure | Score Range | MCID | Best Utility |
|---------|-------------|------|--------------|
| **WORC (Western Ontario Rotator Cuff)** | 0–2100 (higher = better) | 275 pts | Disease-specific — most responsive for RC pathology |
| **ASES (American Shoulder and Elbow Surgeons)** | 0–100 (100 = best) | 12–17 pts | Surgical audit; combines pain VAS + function |
| **Constant-Murley Score** | 0–100 (100 = best) | 10–16 pts | Surgical outcomes; requires dynamometer |
| **OSS (Oxford Shoulder Score)** | 0–48 (48 = best) | 5 pts | Post-surgical; simple; patient-completed |
| **SPADI** | 0–100 (0 = best) | 8–13 pts | Pain + disability; responsive in conservative management |
| **QuickDASH** | 0–100 (0 = best) | 11–16 pts | Upper limb function; useful for work-related cases |

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### Imaging for RC Tears

**Ultrasound (USS):**
- Can assess cuff integrity dynamically when imaging is clinically indicated
- Advantages: dynamic assessment, bilateral comparison, real-time, no radiation, guided injection
- Limitations: operator-dependent; cannot assess labrum; poor visualisation under acromion

**MRI:**
- May support specialist assessment and surgical planning when clinically indicated
- Advantages: multiplanar; Goutallier grading; fatty infiltration; bone assessment; labrum
- MR arthrography may be considered by the specialist for selected partial-thickness or intra-articular questions

**X-ray:**
- Plain radiographs may show indirect chronic cuff-related changes but do not establish tear size
- Greater tuberosity irregularity/sclerosis
- Calcific deposits; glenohumeral arthritis

### Key Evidence

- **Moosmayer et al. (2010) JBJS-A:** RCT — surgery vs supervised exercise for small-medium full-thickness tears. No significant difference at 1 year; surgery superior at 2 years on Constant-Murley only.
- **Kukkonen et al. (2015) JBJS-A:** RCT — 3 groups (physio; physio + acromioplasty; physio + acromioplasty + cuff repair). No significant difference at 2 years for small tears.
- **Ainsworth et al. (2009):** Structured physiotherapy produces clinically meaningful outcomes in 75% of full-thickness tear patients avoiding surgery.
- Re-tear risk varies with tear and patient factors; discuss individual prognosis with the surgical team

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Diagnosis | Key Features | Differentiating |
|-----------|-------------|----------------|
| Rotator cuff tendinopathy | No lag signs; strength preserved (pain-limited) | Strength testing with lag signs |
| Subacromial bursitis | Pain without significant weakness | USS: isolated bursal pathology |
| Adhesive capsulitis | Global ROM restriction; no weakness | Capsular pattern; ROM assessment |
| Glenohumeral arthritis | Crepitus; age; gradual onset | X-ray: joint space narrowing |
| Cervical radiculopathy | Neck symptoms; neurological deficit; normal cuff strength | Neurological exam; ULTT; Spurling's |
| SLAP tear | Young overhead athlete; Popeye sign if associated biceps | O'Brien's; MR arthrogram |
| Acromioclavicular pathology | Direct AC tenderness; cross-body adduction pain | AC joint injection test; X-ray |
| Brachial neuritis | Severe acute pain → rapid weakness; no trauma | EMG; MRI neurography |
| Suprascapular nerve entrapment | Wasting infraspinatus; deep aching; ER weakness | EMG; MRI (ganglion cyst) |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Use the current local shoulder pathway. Acute traumatic marked weakness warrants urgent shoulder assessment. Other tears may be considered for individualised conservative care or specialist review according to function, progression, comorbidity and patient goals.

---

### Conservative Management

**Structured Rehabilitation Programme:**

| Phase | Duration | Goals | Key Exercises | Loading |
|-------|----------|-------|---------------|---------|
| **Phase 1: Protection** | Weeks 0–4 | Pain control; ROM; protect cuff | Pendulum; elbow/wrist AROM; scapular retraction; submaximal isometrics | Isometric only |
| **Phase 2: Activation** | Weeks 4–8 | Rotator cuff activation; scapular control | Side-lying ER; standing ER with band; prone Y/T/W; side-lying IR | Light isotonic |
| **Phase 3: Strengthening** | Weeks 8–16 | Strength and endurance; functional ROM | Progressive band ER/IR; scaption; shoulder press; serratus activation; HSR protocol | Progressive load 3×10–15 |
| **Phase 4: Function** | Weeks 16–24 | Return to activity/work/sport | Functional patterns; overhead mechanics; work simulation | Sport/task-specific |

**Evidence base:**
- Cochrane (Page et al., 2016): Supervised exercise superior to no treatment; comparable to surgery for small-medium tears in 2-year outcomes
- Specific exercises (ER, IR, scaption) preferred over non-specific — Kuhn et al. (2010)
- Heavy slow resistance (HSR): emerging evidence comparable to surgical outcomes for small-medium tears

### Pharmacological and injection care

Analgesia and any subacromial injection are individual prescriber/injection-service decisions. The cited rotator-cuff literature may inform a specialist discussion, but fixed drug, PPI, image-guidance and annual injection-limit claims require exact source verification before publication.

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency/urgent assessment:** cardiac emergency features, fracture/dislocation, neurovascular compromise, or marked acute weakness after trauma.
- **Prompt specialist assessment:** significant or progressive weakness suggesting a substantial tear.
- **Specialist assessment:** persistent disabling symptoms or consideration of operative care after an appropriate shared decision process.

Version 1 does not prescribe universal age cut-offs, fixed two-week surgery timing or automatic imaging.

---

## Key References

1. Yamamoto A, Takagishi K, Osawa T, et al. Prevalence and risk factors of a rotator cuff tear in the general population. *J Shoulder Elbow Surg.* 2010;19(1):116–120.
2. Moosmayer S, Lund G, Seljom US, et al. Tendon repair compared with physiotherapy in the treatment of rotator cuff tears: a randomized controlled study in 103 cases with a five-year follow-up. *J Bone Joint Surg Am.* 2014;96(18):1504–1514.
3. de Jesus JO, Parker L, Frangos AJ, Nazarian LN. Accuracy of MRI, MR arthrography, and ultrasound in the diagnosis of rotator cuff tears: a meta-analysis. *AJR Am J Roentgenol.* 2009;192(6):1701–1707.
4. Kukkonen J, Joukainen A, Lehtinen J, et al. Treatment of non-traumatic rotator cuff tears: A randomised controlled trial with one-year clinical results. *Bone Joint J.* 2014;96-B(1):75–81.
5. Page MJ, Green S, McBain B, et al. Manual therapy and exercise for rotator cuff disease. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev.* 2016;(6):CD012224.
6. Murrell GA, Walton JR. Diagnosis of rotator cuff tears. *Lancet.* 2001;357(9258):769–770.
7. Hegedus EJ, Goode AP, Cook CE, et al. Which physical examination tests provide clinicians with the most value when examining the shoulder? Update of a systematic review with meta-analysis of individual tests. *Br J Sports Med.* 2012;46(14):964–978.
8. Colvin AC, Egorova N, Harrison AK, Moskowitz A, Flatow EL. National trends in rotator cuff repair. *J Bone Joint Surg Am.* 2012;94(3):227–233.
9. BESS/BOA. *Shoulder Pain – Primary, Community and Intermediate Care Guidelines*. [Guideline page](https://bess.ac.uk/bess-boa-primary-intermediate-care-shoulder-pain-guidelines/).
10. Ainsworth R, Lewis JS, Conboy V. A prospective randomized placebo controlled clinical trial of a rehabilitation programme for patients with a diagnosis of massive rotator cuff tears of the shoulder. *Shoulder Elbow.* 2009;1(1):55–60.
11. Lenza M, Buchbinder R, Takwoingi Y, Johnston RV, Hanchard NC, Faloppa F. Magnetic resonance imaging, magnetic resonance arthrography and ultrasonography for assessing rotator cuff tears. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev.* 2013;(9):CD009020.
12. Goutallier D, Postel JM, Bernageau J, Lavau L, Voisin MC. Fatty muscle degeneration in cuff ruptures. Pre- and postoperative evaluation by CT scan. *Clin Orthop Relat Res.* 1994;(304):78–83.

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Rotator cuff tendinopathy is a broad term encompassing pain and dysfunction arising from the rotator cuff tendons — most commonly the supraspinatus, followed by infraspinatus and subscapularis. It represents one of the most prevalent musculoskeletal conditions in clinical practice, accounting for 44–65% of shoulder pain presentations in primary care.

**Prevalence:** Point prevalence of shoulder pain is approximately 7–26% in the general population, with rotator cuff pathology implicated in 65–70% of cases. Prevalence increases sharply with age, particularly after 50 years.

### Continuum Model of Tendinopathy (Cook & Purdam, 2009; revised 2016)

The continuum model — now the dominant explanatory framework — describes tendinopathy as a spectrum rather than a discrete entity:

1. **Reactive tendinopathy:** Acute response to compressive or tensile overload. Non-inflammatory cell-mediated matrix changes. Clinically: sudden onset after unaccustomed loading. Potentially reversible.
2. **Tendon dysrepair:** Failed or incomplete healing response. Disorganised matrix, increased ground substance and vascularity. Clinically: subacute/chronic presentation.
3. **Degenerative tendinopathy:** Cell death, lipid infiltration, calcification, disorganised collagen. High risk of tear. Clinically: chronic, often in older patients.

**Key principle:** Tendinopathy is a failed healing response, not classic inflammation. This drives management decisions — compressive loading and prolonged rest are counterproductive; progressive tensile loading is the evidence-based intervention.

### Relevant Anatomy

- **Supraspinatus:** Arises from the supraspinous fossa, inserts on the greater tuberosity (superior facet). Primary abductor and force-couple stabiliser.
- **Infraspinatus:** External rotator; inserts on greater tuberosity (middle facet).
- **Subscapularis:** Internal rotator; inserts on lesser tuberosity.
- **Teres minor:** External rotator; inserts on greater tuberosity (inferior facet).
- **Critical zone:** The avascular zone 1 cm from the supraspinatus insertion — primary site of degenerative change and tears.
- **Subacromial space:** Normal space 7–14 mm; reduction causes impingement on the bursa and rotator cuff.

### Mechanism

Multifactorial — intrinsic and extrinsic factors:

- **Intrinsic:** Age-related tendon degeneration, vascularity compromise, genetic predisposition (COL5A1 gene variants), tendon morphology.
- **Extrinsic:** Repetitive overhead activity, heavy lifting, acromion morphology (Bigliani Type II/III), scapular dyskinesis, altered glenohumeral kinematics, poor rotator cuff strength/endurance.
- **Compressive loading** at the subacromial space is now recognised as a key perpetuating factor, particularly for supraspinatus tendinopathy.

---

## Special Tests

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Neer, Hawkins-Kennedy and painful arc | May reproduce subacromial pain | Non-specific and not diagnostic alone |
| Empty/full can and resisted external rotation | Explore pain and cuff-related weakness | Interpret pain inhibition and true weakness separately |
| Lag, drop-arm, lift-off and bear-hug tests | Explore features of a substantial cuff tear | Positive findings require correlation with history, movement and imaging indications |
| Speed's and other adjacent-structure tests | Explore competing sources | Limited cuff discrimination |

**Clinical pearl — Cluster testing for rotator cuff tendinopathy:**
No single test is diagnostic. Use a coherent history and examination pattern, and distinguish pain inhibition from clinically important weakness.

---

## Red Flags

- Chest, jaw or arm symptoms with dyspnoea or other acute cardiac features require emergency assessment/999.
- Progressive weakness, rapid muscle wasting or another focal neurological deficit requires prompt medical/neurological assessment.
- Infection, malignancy, fracture/dislocation or neurovascular compromise requires escalation according to the presentation.
- Possible diaphragmatic referral should be assessed in its clinical context rather than assigned automatic imaging.

Do not rely on one provocative or special test in isolation to diagnose rotator-cuff tendinopathy; interpret findings with the history and the rest of the examination.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Lewis Shoulder Framework (2009, updated 2015)
Jeremy Lewis's clinical framework for shoulder assessment emphasises:
1. **Symptom behaviour** — directional preference, aggravating/relieving factors
2. **Movement assessment** — active ROM, painful arc, end-range pain
3. **Load assessment** — rotator cuff strength testing (ER, IR, abduction, flexion)
4. **Differentiation testing** — supine positioning to offload subacromial space
5. **Treatment-based classification** — responds better than diagnosis-based for directing exercise loading

### Cools Rehabilitation Framework (2016)
Ann Cools's evidence-based rehabilitation model for shoulder:
1. **Phase 1 — Pain management:** Activity modification, posture correction, scapular control
2. **Phase 2 — Motor control:** Low-load rotator cuff activation, scapular stabilisers
3. **Phase 3 — Strength:** Progressive loading, ER/IR ratio restoration (target ER:IR ≥ 0.66)
4. **Phase 4 — Function:** Sport/work-specific loading, overhead mechanics

### Cluster Testing Approach (Hegedus et al., 2012)
- Never rely on a single test
- Combine high-sensitivity tests (rule out) with high-specificity tests (rule in)
- Assess response to load — does isometric contraction reproduce/resolve pain?
- Musculoskeletal ultrasound is the recommended first-line imaging in most guidelines

---

## Outcome Measures

| Measure | Structure | Score Range | MCID | MDC | Clinical Utility |
|---------|-----------|-------------|------|-----|-----------------|
| **DASH (Disabilities of Arm, Shoulder and Hand)** | 30-item PRO; optional work and sport modules | 0–100 (0=best) | Use a population-appropriate change threshold | — | Comprehensive; time-consuming |
| **QuickDASH** | 11-item abbreviated DASH | 0–100 (0=best) | 11–16 points | 15.9 | Excellent for clinical use; highly correlated with DASH |
| **SPADI (Shoulder Pain and Disability Index)** | 13-item; pain (5) + disability (8) subscales | 0–100 (0=best) | 8–13 points | 18 | Simple; widely validated; best for shoulder-specific pain |
| **OSS (Oxford Shoulder Score)** | 12-item | 0–48 (48=best) | 5 points | — | Preferred by orthopaedic surgeons; post-surgical monitoring |
| **Constant-Murley Score** | 35-point subjective + 65-point objective | 0–100 (100=best) | Use a population-appropriate change threshold | — | Includes measured abduction strength |
| **WORC (Western Ontario Rotator Cuff)** | 21-item VAS-based | 0–2100 (2100=best) | 275 points | — | Disease-specific; most responsive for rotator cuff pathology |
| **PSFS (Patient-Specific Functional Scale)** | 3–5 self-selected activities | 0–10/activity | 2 points | — | Excellent for individualised goal tracking; highly responsive |

**Recommendation for routine practice:** SPADI + QuickDASH at baseline and discharge. Add Constant-Murley if post-surgical or when objective comparison required.

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### Imaging Guidelines

**Ultrasound (USS):**
- First-line imaging per BESS (British Elbow and Shoulder Society) and NICE guidelines
- Can assess cuff integrity dynamically when imaging is clinically indicated; accuracy varies with operator, tear type and reference standard
- Operator-dependent; limited assessment of labrum and glenohumeral joint

**MRI:**
- Preferred for labral pathology, bony assessment, surgical planning
- May support specialist assessment of cuff, labral and bony pathology when the result will change management
- MR arthrogram for suspected partial-thickness tear, labral tears, instability

**X-ray:**
- Limited for soft tissue; useful for: calcific deposits, acromioclavicular joint, glenohumeral arthritis, acromion morphology, greater tuberosity irregularity
- AP + axillary views minimum

### Key Systematic Reviews
- **Hegedus et al. (2012)** — BJSM: Most comprehensive meta-analysis of shoulder special tests. Conclusion: No single test has sufficient diagnostic accuracy; clusters recommended.
- **Lenza et al. (2013)** — Cochrane: USS and MRI comparable for full-thickness tears; MRI superior for partial tears.
- **Diercks et al. (2014)** — BESS: USS first-line before MRI for suspected RC pathology.

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Diagnosis | Key differentiating features | Clinical assessment |
|---|---|---|
| Subacromial pain syndrome | Painful arc and pain with overhead activity | Load response and muscle tests interpreted with the history |
| Full-thickness rotator-cuff tear | Significant weakness; lag signs may be present | Lag/drop-arm findings and traumatic or degenerative context |
| Adhesive capsulitis | Global restriction with a capsular pattern | Restriction across directions |
| Glenohumeral arthritis | Gradual restriction, crepitus and stiffness | Joint pattern and clinical context |
| Cervical radiculopathy | Arm symptoms, neurological signs and neck-related reproduction | Cervical and neurological examination |
| AC-joint pathology | Superior pain and direct AC-joint tenderness | AC palpation and cross-body loading |
| Biceps or calcific pathology | Anterior pain or acute severe presentation | Condition-appropriate assessment |
| Instability or thoracic outlet syndrome | Apprehension, hyperlaxity or positional neurovascular features | Context-specific instability or neurovascular assessment |
| Brachial neuritis | Acute severe pain followed by rapid weakness | Neurological pattern and medical assessment |

Investigation choice should follow the clinical context, red-flag assessment and appropriate specialist pathway; no imaging modality is an automatic confirmatory test for every differential.

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Use an exercise-led, individualised rotator-cuff tendinopathy plan consistent with the current clinical practice guideline and local pathway. Progressive motor deficit or marked traumatic weakness follows a separate prompt specialist pathway.

---

### Conservative Management

**Evidence-based exercise prescription:**

| Phase | Timeframe | Exercise Focus | Dosage | Evidence |
|-------|-----------|----------------|--------|----------|
| Acute (reactive) | 0–4 weeks | Pain-free isometrics; posture; scapular control | 3×10–15 reps, daily | Moderate (RCT) |
| Sub-acute | 4–8 weeks | Isotonic ER/IR; scapular stabilisers; progressive load | 3×12–15 reps, 3×/week | High (multiple RCTs) |
| Rehabilitation | 8–16 weeks | Functional strengthening; eccentric loading; overhead mechanics | 3×8–12 reps progressive | High (Cochrane) |
| Return to activity | 16–24 weeks | Sport/work-specific; plyometric if relevant | Graded exposure | Moderate |

**Key exercise principles:**
- **Eccentric loading** — most evidence base for patellar tendinopathy; emerging evidence for shoulder (Level II)
- **Heavy Slow Resistance (HSR)** — Beyer et al. (2015) AJSM: HSR comparable to eccentric; better adherence
- **ER:IR strength ratio** target ≥ 0.66 at 60°/s isokinetic testing
- **Scapular stabilisation:** Serratus anterior activation; lower/middle trapezius; avoid upper trapezius dominance

**Manual therapy:**
- Thoracic manipulation: reduces shoulder pain and improves ROM — moderate evidence (Haik et al., 2016)
- Glenohumeral joint mobilisation (posterior capsule): Grade III–IV for capsular restriction
- Soft tissue release: limited high-quality evidence as standalone intervention

**Education:**
- Activity modification (not rest); load management principles; graded return
- Posture correction; ergonomic advice (particularly for occupational factors)

### Nonsurgical medical care

The current evidence anchors for this condition are the BESS Subacromial Shoulder Pain pathway and the 2025 rotator-cuff-tendinopathy clinical practice guideline. They do not justify carrying forward unsupported fixed doses, PPI age thresholds, annual injection limits or mandatory post-injection waiting periods. Medicine and injection decisions remain prescriber-led and must be checked against the exact current recommendation and patient context.

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency:** acute cardiac features or another emergency presentation.
- **Prompt medical/specialist assessment:** progressive neurological/motor deficit, rapid wasting or marked traumatic weakness.
- **Specialist shoulder assessment:** persistent disabling symptoms or diagnostic uncertainty despite appropriate rehabilitation.

Investigation and procedure selection are clinician-led; no automatic MRI or fixed operative timeline is asserted.

---

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Shoulder instability refers to symptomatic excessive translation of the humeral head relative to the glenoid, ranging from subtle microinstability to recurrent dislocation. It is the most common joint dislocation, accounting for approximately 45% of all dislocations presenting to emergency departments. It represents a significant clinical challenge due to its heterogeneous aetiology, high recurrence rates, and impact on young, active populations.

**Prevalence / Incidence:**
- Annual incidence of primary glenohumeral dislocation: 11–27 per 100,000
- Peak incidence: males 20–30 years (contact sport); females 20–50 years (atraumatic)
- Recurrence rate after first dislocation: 55–97% in those <20 years; 10–40% in those >40 years
- Recurrence is the dominant clinical concern in young athletes

### Classification (TUBS / AMBRI / AIOS)

**TUBS (Traumatic Unilateral Bankart Surgery):**
- Traumatic onset (single event)
- Unilateral
- Bankart lesion (anteroinferior labral avulsion)
- Surgery often required for high recurrence risk

**AMBRI (Atraumatic Multidirectional Bilateral Rehabilitation):**
- Atraumatic onset (no single traumatic event)
- Multidirectional laxity (inferior ± anterior ± posterior)
- Bilateral (often)
- Rehabilitation primary treatment
- Inferior capsular shift if surgery required

**AIOS (Acquired Instability from Overuse in Sport):**
- Overhead throwing athletes
- Acquired internal impingement; microinstability
- Direction-specific; sport-related

**Stanmore Classification (Rowe & Zarins / Revised Bayley et al.):**
Three-point triangle:
- Type I: Structural (traumatic — Bankart, Hill-Sachs)
- Type II: Atraumatic structural (involuntary; dysplastic glenoid)
- Type III: Muscle patterning (non-structural; habitual; voluntary)

### Relevant Anatomy and Stabilisers

**Static stabilisers:**
- **Glenoid labrum:** Deepens glenoid cavity by ~50%; increases contact area; functions as chock-block for humeral head
- **Glenohumeral ligaments (GHL):**
  - Superior GHL: resists inferior translation in neutral and adduction
  - Middle GHL: resists anterior translation at 45° abduction
  - Inferior GHL (IGHL): primary restraint at 90° abduction — most important ligament
  - IGHL anterior band: resists anterior translation with ER
  - IGHL posterior band: resists posterior translation with IR
- **Rotator cuff interval:** Coracohumeral + superior GHL; resists inferior translation
- **Bony glenoid:** Pear-shaped; inverted glenoid version (>15° retroversion = risk for posterior instability)

**Dynamic stabilisers:**
- **Rotator cuff:** Compression into glenoid; concavity-compression mechanism
- **Long head of biceps:** Superior glenohumeral stability
- **Scapular rotators:** Position glenoid face; scapular dyskinesis → decreased stability

**Common Structural Lesions:**
| Lesion | Description | Direction | Imaging |
|--------|-------------|-----------|---------|
| Bankart lesion | Anteroinferior labral avulsion from glenoid | Anterior | MR arthrogram |
| Bony Bankart | Bankart + glenoid bone loss (>20–25% = critical) | Anterior | CT (glenoid morphology) |
| Hill-Sachs defect | Posterolateral humeral head impaction fracture | Anterior | CT; MRI |
| HAGL (Humeral Avulsion GHL) | GHL avulsion from humerus (not labrum) | Anterior | MR arthrogram |
| Reverse Bankart | Posteroinferior labral tear | Posterior | MR arthrogram |
| Reverse Hill-Sachs | Anteromedial humeral head (posterior dislocation) | Posterior | CT; MRI |
| SLAP tear | Superior labrum anterior-posterior | — | MR arthrogram |

---

## Special Tests

### Anterior Instability Tests

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Apprehension, relocation and anterior release | Explore symptomatic anterior instability; apprehension is more relevant than pain alone | Interpret as a coherent pattern, not a numeric rule |
| Load-and-shift, anterior drawer and fulcrum | Explore translation and symptom reproduction | Laxity alone does not establish symptomatic instability |

### Posterior Instability Tests

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Posterior apprehension, jerk, Kim and posterior stress | Explore symptomatic posterior instability | Performance varies; no single test is definitive |
| Posterior load-and-shift | Explores posterior translation | Examiner-dependent and must be related to symptoms |

### Multidirectional Instability / Laxity Tests

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Sulcus sign and hyperabduction | Explore inferior laxity | Laxity is not synonymous with instability |
| Beighton score | Screens generalised joint hypermobility | Does not diagnose shoulder instability or a connective-tissue disorder |

### Combined/Other Tests

| Test | Sensitivity | Specificity | Evidence | Notes |
|------|------------|------------|----------|-------|
| **Clunk Test** | 44% | 99% | Moderate | Full circumduction with axial load; clunk = labral tear |
| **O'Brien's Active Compression** | 47–67% | 37–89% | Moderate | Combined SLAP/ACJ test; limited alone |
| **Crank Test** | 91% | 93% | Moderate | Supine; axial load + rotation; for labral tears |

---

## Red Flags

- Unreduced/locked dislocation, fracture-dislocation or vascular compromise requires emergency assessment.
- Perform and document a post-reduction neurovascular examination. A persistent neurological deficit requires prompt specialist assessment.
- Recurrent or persistent instability requires specialist assessment according to the current BESS/local pathway.

Procedure choice, ISIS score, bone-loss thresholds and fixed multidirectional-instability timelines belong to specialist planning, not the Version 1 red-flag criteria.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Instability Severity Index Score (ISIS — Balg & Boileau, 2007)
Pre-operative risk tools may inform specialist discussion but do not determine a procedure on their own:

| Factor | Score |
|--------|-------|
| Age ≤20 | 2 pts |
| Competitive sport | 2 pts |
| Contact/overhead sport | 1 pt |
| Hyperlaxity (anterior apprehension <60° ER or sulcus >2 cm) | 1 pt |
| Hill-Sachs on AP X-ray | 2 pts |
| Loss of sclerotic inferior glenoid contour on AP X-ray | 2 pts |

- Score ≤3: Low recurrence risk — arthroscopic Bankart suitable
- Score ≥4: High recurrence risk — Latarjet procedure recommended
- Score ≥6: Very high risk — Latarjet mandatory

### FEDS Classification (Functional, Emotion, Direction, Severity)
Modern classification for MDI — integrates structural, neuromuscular, and psychological components.

### Rehabilitation Framework for Instability (Wilk & Macrina, 2012)

**Phase 1 — Acute protection (0–4 weeks):**
- Sling immobilisation (IR position — debate ongoing; ER immobilisation: reduced recurrence in young males)
- Pain management; rotator cuff isometrics; elbow/wrist AROM
- Early scapular control: retraction; no provocative positions

**Phase 2 — Intermediate (4–8 weeks):**
- Progressive ER; RC strengthening; scapular stabilisers
- Proprioception training (joint position sense)
- Activity modification — avoid apprehension position (90°ABD/ER)

**Phase 3 — Advanced strengthening (8–16 weeks):**
- Progressive loading; functional patterns; perturbation training
- Sport-specific preparation

**Phase 4 — Return to sport (4–6 months):**
- Graduated sport return; sport-specific exercises
- Bracing consideration for contact sports

---

## Outcome Measures

| Measure | Score Range | MCID | Application |
|---------|-------------|------|-------------|
| **WOSI (Western Ontario Shoulder Instability Index)** | 0–2100 (2100 = best) | 220–320 pts | Disease-specific; most responsive for instability |
| **ASES (American Shoulder and Elbow Surgeons)** | 0–100 (100 = best) | 12–17 pts | Surgical audit |
| **Rowe Score** | 0–100 (100 = best) | — | Recurrence rate; function; ROM; composite |
| **Melbourne Instability Shoulder Score** | 0–100 | — | Instability-specific; used in Australia/UK |
| **DASH / QuickDASH** | 0–100 (0 = best) | 11–16 pts | Upper limb function; useful for occupational impact |
| **PSFS** | 0–10/activity | 2 pts | Sport/activity-specific; individualised |

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### Imaging

**X-ray (first-line — mandatory after acute dislocation):**
- AP: Hill-Sachs; glenoid erosion; fracture
- Axillary lateral: confirms anterior/posterior direction; glenoid morphology
- Stryker Notch view: Hill-Sachs optimised
- West Point view: anterior glenoid rim (Bankart bony lesion)

**CT (glenoid bone loss assessment):**
- Mandated when bony Bankart or significant glenoid loss suspected
- Glenoid bone loss, lesion engagement, patient factors and activity demands inform specialist procedure selection; this learner page does not prescribe a numeric operative threshold
- CT arthrogram: bone loss + labral assessment combined

**MRI arthrogram:**
- May support specialist assessment of Bankart, HAGL and other labral lesions when imaging will change management
- Preferred over standard MRI for labral tears (gadolinium distension)
- Identifies: Bankart; HAGL; SLAP; Hill-Sachs; rotator cuff; posterior lesions

**USS:**
- Limited for instability; cannot adequately assess labrum
- Useful adjunct for associated RC pathology (especially in >40 years post-dislocation)

### Key Evidence

- **Itoi et al. (2007) JBJS:** ER immobilisation after primary dislocation reduces recurrence in young males (26% vs 42% at 2 years) — but difficult to sustain compliance
- **Balg & Boileau (2007):** ISIS score — validated prediction of recurrence and surgical decision-making
- **Burkhart & De Beer (2000):** Glenoid bone loss >25% = "engaging" Hill-Sachs; Bankart repair fails; Latarjet required
- **Bessière et al. (2014):** Latarjet superior recurrence rate vs arthroscopic Bankart in high-risk patients

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Diagnosis | Key Features | Differentiation |
|-----------|-------------|----------------|
| Rotator cuff tear (post-dislocation) | Weakness; lag signs; >40 years | USS/MRI post-reduction |
| Acromioclavicular dislocation | Direct impact; AC step deformity | X-ray; AC palpation |
| Multidirectional laxity without instability | Asymptomatic hypermobility | Beighton score; symptom correlation |
| SLAP tear | Overhead athlete; mechanical symptoms | MR arthrogram; special tests |
| SAPS/rotator cuff tendinopathy | Painful arc; no apprehension | Apprehension test negative |
| Cervical radiculopathy | Neck; neurological signs; referred | Cervical exam; ULTT |
| Brachial neuritis | Post-viral; acute severe pain; wasting | EMG; MRI |
| Glenohumeral OA | Older; crepitus; X-ray changes | X-ray |
| Posterior shoulder tightness (GIRD) | Overhead athlete; IR deficit | IR ROM measurement |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Use the current BESS/local shoulder instability pathway. Acute dislocation care includes appropriate reduction, post-reduction neurovascular assessment and individualised rehabilitation. Vascular compromise is an emergency. Persistent neurological deficit or recurrent/persistent instability requires specialist review.

---

### Conservative Management

**Structured Rehabilitation (Evidence Grade B–A):**

| Phase | Duration | Focus | Key Exercises |
|-------|----------|-------|---------------|
| Acute protection | 0–4 weeks | Sling; pain control; early activation | Pendulum; elbow/wrist AROM; isometrics |
| Activation | 4–8 weeks | RC activation; scapular control | Side-lying ER; scapular retraction; prone Y/T/W |
| Strengthening | 8–16 weeks | Progressive loading; proprioception | Band ER/IR; shoulder press; perturbation |
| Function/sport | 16–24 weeks | Return to sport/work | Sport-specific; dynamic stability |

**MDI specific (AMBRI):**
- Primary treatment is rehabilitation — 80% avoid surgery with structured programme (Burkhead & Rockwood, 1992)
- Focus: rotator cuff co-contraction; proprioception; scapular control
- Minimum 6 months rehabilitation trial before surgical consideration
- Avoid provocative positions during initial rehabilitation

### Pharmacological

Acute post-reduction analgesia is determined by the emergency/prescribing team. Recurrent instability management is not a drug pathway; any specific drug duration or injection claim requires its own source and specialist context.

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency:** vascular compromise, unreduced/locked dislocation or fracture-dislocation.
- **Prompt specialist assessment:** persistent post-reduction neurological deficit or marked traumatic weakness.
- **Specialist assessment:** recurrent/persistent instability or failure to regain acceptable function with appropriate rehabilitation.

Where a persistent nerve deficit is being investigated, test timing and referral route are determined by the specialist pathway. Version 1 does not prescribe fixed age, recurrence-count, ISIS, bone-loss, procedure or multidirectional-instability thresholds.

---

## Key References

1. Balg F, Boileau P. The instability severity index score: a simple pre-operative score to select patients for arthroscopic or open shoulder stabilisation. *J Bone Joint Surg Br.* 2007;89(11):1470–1477.
2. Waldt S, Burkart A, Imhoff AB, Bruegel M, Rummeny EJ, Woertler K. Anterior shoulder instability: accuracy of MR arthrography in the classification of anteroinferior labroligamentous injuries. *Radiology.* 2005;237(2):578–583.
3. Burkhead WZ Jr, Rockwood CA Jr. Treatment of instability of the shoulder with an exercise program. *J Bone Joint Surg Am.* 1992;74(6):890–896.
4. Itoi E, Hatakeyama Y, Sato T, et al. Immobilization in external rotation after shoulder dislocation reduces the risk of recurrence: a randomized controlled trial. *J Bone Joint Surg Am.* 2007;89(10):2124–2131.
5. Hegedus EJ, Goode AP, Cook CE, et al. Which physical examination tests provide clinicians with the most value when examining the shoulder? *Br J Sports Med.* 2012;46(14):964–978.
6. Burkhart SS, De Beer JF. Traumatic glenohumeral bone defects and their relationship to failure of arthroscopic Bankart repairs: significance of the inverted-pear glenoid and the humeral engaging Hill-Sachs lesion. *Arthroscopy.* 2000;16(7):677–694.
7. Bessière C, Trojani C, Carles M, Mehta SS, Boileau P. The open Latarjet procedure is more reliable in terms of shoulder stability than arthroscopic Bankart repair. *Clin Orthop Relat Res.* 2014;472(8):2345–2351.
8. Wilk KE, Macrina LC. Non-operative and post-operative rehabilitation for glenohumeral instability. *Clin Sports Med.* 2013;32(4):865–914.
9. Bayley I, Kemp G. Classification of shoulder instability in terms of muscle patterning. *Shoulder Elbow.* 2009;1(1):30–32.
10. Kuhn JE. A new classification system for shoulder instability. *Br J Sports Med.* 2010;44(5):341–346.
11. Robinson CM, Howes J, Murdoch H, Will E, Graham C. Functional outcome and risk of recurrent instability after primary traumatic anterior shoulder dislocation in young patients. *J Bone Joint Surg Am.* 2006;88(11):2326–2336.

12. BESS/BOA. *Traumatic Anterior Shoulder Instability* and *Atraumatic Shoulder Instability* patient care pathways. [BESS guidelines](https://bess.ac.uk/patient-care-pathways-and-guidelines/).

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

The glenoid labrum is a fibrocartilaginous rim attached to the periphery of the glenoid fossa, deepening the socket by approximately 50% and contributing significantly to glenohumeral stability. Labral tears fall into two main clinical categories encountered in MSK physiotherapy practice:

### SLAP Lesions (Superior Labrum Anterior to Posterior)

SLAP lesions involve the superior labrum at the biceps anchor (long head of biceps tendon inserts at the supraglenoid tubercle and superior labrum). They are common in overhead athletes, throwing sports, and following traction/compression injuries.

**Snyder Classification (1990):**

| Type | Description | Prevalence | Management Implication |
|------|-------------|-----------|------------------------|
| Type I | Superior labral fraying/degeneration; biceps anchor intact | ~11% | Conservative |
| Type II | Detachment of superior labrum and biceps anchor from glenoid (most common) | ~41% | Often surgical if conservative fails |
| Type III | Bucket-handle tear of superior labrum; biceps anchor intact | ~33% | Surgical resection |
| Type IV | Bucket-handle tear extending into biceps tendon | ~15% | Surgical repair/tenotomy |
| Type V–X | Extended classifications (Morgan/Maffet) including combined lesions | Variable | Specialist-led |

**Pathomechanics:**
- **Repetitive overhead loading**: Peel-back mechanism during late cocking phase — posterior biceps vector peels the labrum off the glenoid
- **GIRD (Glenohumeral Internal Rotation Deficit)**: Posterior capsule tightness shifts humeral head posterosuperiorly, increasing shear on superior labrum
- **Traction injury**: Sudden inferior distraction force (e.g., catching a heavy object)
- **Compression**: FOOSH (fall on outstretched hand) with superior shear force

### Bankart Lesions (Anterior Labral Tears)

Bankart lesions involve detachment of the anteroinferior labrum (typically 3–6 o'clock position) from the glenoid rim. They are the primary pathological lesion in traumatic anterior glenohumeral instability, present in **>85% of first-time anterior dislocations**.

**Subtypes:**
- **Classic Bankart**: Labrum and periosteum stripped from glenoid rim
- **Bony Bankart**: Associated anterior glenoid bone loss (>15–20% glenoid width indicates critical bone loss and alters surgical planning)
- **ALPSA (Anterior Labroligamentous Periosteal Sleeve Avulsion)**: Periosteum intact; labrum medially displaced — poorer proprioceptive outcome if untreated
- **HAGL (Humeral Avulsion of Glenohumeral Ligament)**: Less common; IGHL avulses from humeral attachment

**Associated lesions:** Hill-Sachs defect (posterolateral humeral head impression fracture) in 47–90% of dislocations; engaging Hill-Sachs lesions complicate surgical planning.

---

## Special Tests

> **Clinical Note:** No single special test confirms labral pathology with sufficient diagnostic certainty. Use the history, mechanism and a coherent examination pattern; specialist imaging may be required when the result will change management.

### SLAP-Specific Tests

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| O'Brien's, Crank, Biceps Load II and Anterior Slide | May reproduce symptoms associated with superior labral/biceps-anchor pathology | Performance varies and no single test confirms a SLAP lesion |
| Speed's and Yergason's | Explore biceps-related symptoms | Limited labral discrimination |
| Compression Rotation | May reproduce pain or clicking | Do not use in isolation |

### Instability / Bankart Tests

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Apprehension, relocation and anterior release | Explore symptomatic anterior instability; apprehension is more relevant than pain alone | Interpret as a coherent pattern, not a numeric diagnostic rule |
| Sulcus sign and load-and-shift | Explore laxity and direction | Laxity is not synonymous with symptomatic instability |

---

## Red Flags

- Unreduced dislocation, fracture-dislocation or neurovascular compromise requires urgent/emergency assessment.
- Progressive focal neurological deficit requires prompt medical/specialist assessment.
- After reduction, early mobilisation should follow the current BESS shoulder pathway; prolonged default immobilisation is not used to reduce recurrence.

Glenoid bone loss, Hill-Sachs lesions and procedure selection belong to specialist planning rather than the red-flag list. Version 1 does not prescribe Latarjet, remplissage or another procedure from a fixed threshold.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### SLAP Decision Framework

```
Presentation → Overhead athlete / throwing sport / traction mechanism
     ↓
History: Pain with late cocking phase, clicking, instability, biceps anchor tenderness
     ↓
Special Tests: Biceps Load II + O'Brien's + Crank (cluster ≥2 positive)
     ↓
           Positive Cluster                    Negative Cluster
                ↓                                    ↓
     Request MR Arthrography              Consider alternative diagnosis
     (selected specialist imaging)        (RC pathology, AC joint,
      misses 26–28% SLAP II)              cervical referral)
           ↓
     SLAP confirmed on imaging
           ↓
     Conservative trial 3–6 months:       Failure of conservative →
     Scapular control, rotator cuff,      Orthopaedic referral
     posterior capsule stretch,           (arthroscopic labral repair
     sport-specific rehab                  vs. biceps tenodesis age >35)
```

### Bankart / Instability Framework (HSC NI)

```
First-time traumatic anterior dislocation
     ↓
A&E reduction → Immobilisation (2–3 weeks) → Physiotherapy
     ↓
Risk stratification:
  • Age <25 + contact/overhead sport → HIGH recurrence (70–90%)
  • Age >40 + first dislocation → Rotator cuff tear more likely
  • Bone loss >20% glenoid OR engaging Hill-Sachs → SURGICAL
     ↓
Conservative physiotherapy (12–16 weeks):
  • Phase 1 (0–6 wk): Pendulum, scapular control, rotator cuff activation
  • Phase 2 (6–12 wk): Progressive strengthening, proprioception, neuromuscular control
  • Phase 3 (12–16 wk): Functional/sport-specific, return-to-play criteria
     ↓
Persistent or recurrent instability, neurological deficit or other concerning traumatic findings → context-specific specialist assessment
(Arthroscopic Bankart repair; Latarjet if bone loss)
```

### GIRD Management (SLAP prevention/rehab)

- **Sleeper stretch**: Side-lying on affected shoulder, horizontal adduction + IR — target ≥5° gain in IR compared to contralateral
- **Cross-body stretch**: Horizontal adduction in standing — posterior capsule release
- **Goal**: Achieve GIRD <18° or symmetric to contralateral shoulder (Wilk et al., 2011)

---

## Outcome Measures

| Measure | MCID | MDC | Score Range | Best For | Validated in Shoulder? |
|---------|------|-----|-------------|----------|------------------------|
| **DASH (Disabilities of Arm, Shoulder, Hand)** | 10.8 points | 12.7 points | 0–100 (0=best) | General UE function; instability, post-op | ✅ Yes |
| **SPADI (Shoulder Pain & Disability Index)** | 8–13 points | 18 points | 0–100 (0=best) | Pain-heavy presentations; mixed shoulder pathology | ✅ Yes |
| **OSS (Oxford Shoulder Score)** | 5 points | — | 0–48 (48=best) | Post-surgical outcomes; NHS standard | ✅ Yes |
| **WOSI (Western Ontario Shoulder Instability Index)** | Exact change threshold requires population-specific review | — | 0–2100 (0=best) | Condition-specific instability measure | ✅ Yes |
| **ASES (American Shoulder & Elbow Surgeons)** | 6.4–17 points | — | 0–100 (100=best) | Research/surgical outcomes | ✅ Yes |
| **QuickDASH** | 14.9–15.9 points | — | 0–100 (0=best) | Rapid screening; 11 items | ✅ Yes |

> **Outcome-measure note:** WOSI may be used for instability-related presentations, with DASH or QuickDASH as a general upper-limb measure. Select measures for the individual purpose and local pathway.

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### Imaging Hierarchy

| Modality | SLAP Sensitivity | SLAP Specificity | Notes |
|----------|-----------------|-----------------|-------|
| **MR Arthrography (MRA)** | 82–92% | 85–98% | Gold standard; intra-articular gadolinium required |
| **Plain MRI (3T)** | 63–82% | 79–94% | Adequate for Bankart; misses 18–26% SLAP II |
| **Plain MRI (1.5T)** | 44–68% | 66–87% | Inferior; request MRA if clinical suspicion high |
| **Ultrasound** | Poor for SLAP/Bankart | — | Not indicated for labral pathology |
| **CT Arthrography** | 78–91% | 91% | Alternative if MRI contraindicated; better for bone loss quantification |
| **Plain X-ray (AP/Axillary/Scapular-Y)** | N/A | N/A | Mandatory first-line to exclude fracture, bone loss, Hill-Sachs |

### Diagnostic Accuracy Summary

**For suspected SLAP lesions:** No standalone test is definitive. Use the history and a coherent examination pattern; specialist imaging may be considered when the result will change management.

**For Bankart/instability:** History of dislocation is the most powerful diagnostic factor. Apprehension + Relocation + Anterior Release triad achieves specificity of 0.99. Plain X-ray to exclude fracture/bone loss is mandatory first-line.

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Differentiating Features | Key Tests | Imaging |
|-----------|--------------------------|-----------|---------|
| **Subacromial Impingement / RC Tear** | Pain arc 60–120°, painful resisted abduction/ER; older age group | Neer, Hawkins-Kennedy, Empty Can, Lift-Off | USS first-line; MRI if full-thickness tear suspected |
| **AC Joint Pathology** | Pain localised to AC joint; positive cross-body adduction; O'Brien's positive with pain at top not deep in shoulder | Cross-body adduction, AC joint palpation | X-ray AC views, USS |
| **Biceps Tendinopathy / Rupture** | Anterior groove tenderness; "Popeye" deformity (rupture); speed's positive | Speed's, Yergason's, palpation | USS bicipital groove |
| **Adhesive Capsulitis** | Global passive ROM restriction (all planes); insidious onset; ER most restricted | Capsular pattern assessment | X-ray to exclude OA; MRI shows capsular thickening |
| **SLAC (Superior Labral Anterior-posterior Cyst)** | Paralabral cyst on imaging; suprascapular nerve compression; spinati wasting | EMG/NCS, suprascapular notch palpation | MRI |
| **Multidirectional Instability (MDI)** | Bilateral laxity; atraumatic onset; sulcus sign; Beighton score | Sulcus sign, load-and-shift all directions, Beighton score | MRI; often normal |
| **Cervical Radiculopathy (C5/C6)** | Dermatomal symptoms; neck movement provokes; reflexes affected | Spurling's, Upper limb tension test | Cervical MRI |
| **Glenohumeral OA** | Older age; end-stage reduced ROM; crepitus; loss of joint space | Crepitus, passive ROM | X-ray (joint space narrowing, osteophytes) |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Use the current BESS/local shoulder pathway. Acute dislocation care includes reduction through an appropriate emergency pathway, post-reduction neurovascular examination and individualised rehabilitation. Specialist imaging and operative planning are separate decisions.

---

### Conservative Management

**Phase 1 — Acute/Pain Control (Weeks 0–4)**
- Activity modification; avoid aggravating overhead/throwing positions
- Pendulum exercises, AROM within comfort
- Scapular retraction/depression activation
- Heat/ice PRN for symptom management
- Patient education: mechanism, prognosis, self-management

**Phase 2 — Strengthening (Weeks 4–12)**
- Rotator cuff strengthening: ER/IR with resistance band (supine → standing progression)
- Scapular stabiliser strengthening: serratus anterior (wall push-up plus), lower trapezius
- Posterior capsule stretching if GIRD present (sleeper stretch, cross-body stretch)
- Proprioception: closed-chain upper extremity exercises, unstable surface work

**Phase 3 — Sport/Function (Weeks 12–16+)**
- Sport-specific progressive loading
- Throwing athletes: interval throwing programme (ITP) — Andrews protocol
- Return-to-play criteria:
  - Full, pain-free ROM
  - ≥90% strength symmetry (isokinetic or HHD)
  - WOSI >85% of maximum score
  - Negative apprehension/biceps provocation tests
  - Functional/sport-specific tasks completed without limitation

### Pharmacological

No medicine treats a labral tear itself. Analgesia or injection decisions are prescriber/specialist-led; the previous fixed durations and unsupported treatment hierarchy have been removed pending claim-level evidence review.

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency/urgent assessment:** unreduced dislocation, fracture-dislocation or neurovascular compromise.
- **Prompt specialist assessment:** persistent neurological deficit, unreduced or traumatic instability, or other concerning traumatic findings.
- **Consider routine specialist shoulder assessment:** recurrent/persistent instability, diagnostic uncertainty or persistent disabling symptoms despite rehabilitation.

Age cut-offs, recurrence counts, ISIS scores, bone-loss thresholds and procedure choice are not universal Version 1 referral rules.

---

## Key References

1. **Snyder SJ, Karzel RP, Del Pizzo W, et al.** SLAP lesions of the shoulder. *Arthroscopy.* 1990;6(4):274–279. *(Original SLAP classification)*

2. **O'Brien SJ, Pagnani MJ, Fealy S, et al.** The active compression test: a new and effective test for diagnosing labral tears and acromioclavicular joint abnormality. *Am J Sports Med.* 1998;26(5):610–613.

3. **Kim SH, Ha KI, Ahn JH, et al.** Biceps Load Test II: A clinical test for SLAP lesions of the shoulder. *Arthroscopy.* 2001;17(2):160–164. *(Sens 0.90, Spec 0.97)*

4. **Meserve BB, Cleland JA, Boucher TR.** A meta-analysis examining clinical test utility for assessing superior labral anterior posterior lesions. *Am J Sports Med.* 2009;37(11):2252–2258.

5. **Lo IK, Nonweiler B, Woolfrey M, et al.** An evaluation of the apprehension, relocation, and surprise tests for anterior shoulder instability. *Am J Sports Med.* 2004;32(2):301–307.

6. **Wilk KE, Macrina LC, Fleisig GS, et al.** Correlation of glenohumeral internal rotation deficit and total rotational motion to shoulder injuries in professional baseball pitchers. *Am J Sports Med.* 2011;39(2):329–335.

7. **Bankart ASB.** The pathology and treatment of recurrent dislocation of the shoulder joint. *Br J Surg.* 1938;26:23–29. *(Original description)*

8. **Balg F, Boileau P.** The instability severity index score. *J Bone Joint Surg Br.* 2007;89(11):1470–1477. *(ISIS score — surgical decision tool)*

9. **Burkhart SS, De Beer JF.** Traumatic glenohumeral bone defects and their relationship to failure of arthroscopic Bankart repairs. *Arthroscopy.* 2000;16(7):677–694. *(Critical bone loss concept)*

10. **Maffet MW, Gartsman GM, Moseley B.** Superior labrum-biceps tendon complex lesions of the shoulder. *Am J Sports Med.* 1995;23(1):93–98. *(Extended SLAP classification Types V–VII)*

11. **Provencher MT, Bhatia S, Ghodadra NS, et al.** Recurrent shoulder instability: current concepts for evaluation and management of glenoid bone loss. *J Bone Joint Surg Am.* 2010;92(Suppl 2):133–151.

12. **Kirkley A, Griffin S, McLintock H, Ng L.** The development and evaluation of a disease-specific quality of life measurement tool for shoulder instability. *Am J Sports Med.* 1998;26(6):764–772. *(WOSI development and validation)*

13. **BESS/BOA.** *Traumatic Anterior Shoulder Instability* and *Atraumatic Shoulder Instability* patient care pathways. [BESS guidelines](https://bess.ac.uk/patient-care-pathways-and-guidelines/). These do not constitute a NICE labral-tear guideline.
