### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Subacromial Pain Syndrome (SAPS) is the currently preferred umbrella term for pain arising from the subacromial space, replacing the older — and now disputed — term "subacromial impingement syndrome" (SIS). SAPS encompasses pathology of the subacromial bursa, the rotator cuff tendons (particularly supraspinatus), and the coracoacromial arch, without implying a specific structural mechanism.

The terminology shift reflects a fundamental change in understanding: the mechanical impingement model (Neer, 1972) has been largely replaced by a biopsychosocial and load-based framework. SAPS accounts for 44–65% of all shoulder pain presentations.

### Historical Concepts vs Current Understanding

| Model | Historical (Neer, 1972) | Current (Lewis, 2009–2016) |
|-------|------------------------|---------------------------|
| Mechanism | Mechanical pinching of supraspinatus under coracoacromial arch | Multifactorial: load, motor control, biopsychosocial |
| Treatment | Acromioplasty | Exercise; surgical benefit not supported |
| Terminology | Subacromial Impingement Syndrome | Rotator Cuff Related Shoulder Pain / SAPS |
| Key evidence | Cadaveric studies | RCT: Beard et al. 2018 (CSAW trial) |

**CSAW Trial (Beard et al., 2018 — JAMA):** Landmark RCT demonstrating that arthroscopic acromioplasty + physiotherapy was NOT superior to physiotherapy alone or sham surgery. This fundamentally challenged the impingement model and shifted treatment toward exercise-based management.

### Pathophysiology

**Primary contributors:**
1. **Subacromial bursal inflammation/thickening:** The bursa (lying between rotator cuff and acromion) is highly innervated — a key pain source
2. **Rotator cuff tendinopathy:** Most commonly supraspinatus — on the continuum from reactive to degenerative
3. **Altered shoulder kinematics:** Scapular dyskinesis; reduced upward rotation; increased internal rotation during elevation
4. **Rotator cuff force couple imbalance:** Weakness/fatigue of external rotators and lower trapezius; relative upper trapezius overactivation

**Extrinsic factors:**
- Acromion morphology (Bigliani Type II — curved; Type III — hooked): associated but not causal
- Acromioclavicular joint osteophytes
- Coracoacromial ligament thickening
- Occupational overhead loading; sport (swimming, throwing, racquet sports)

**Intrinsic factors:**
- Rotator cuff tendon degeneration (age-related)
- Reduced vascularity in supraspinatus critical zone
- Tendon morphology and genetic factors

### Prevalence
- Most common shoulder diagnosis in primary care (44–65%)
- Annual incidence in UK general practice: ~14.7/1000 patients/year (Linsell et al., 2006)
- Peak incidence: 45–64 years; increasing with overhead occupational exposure

---

## Special Tests

| Test group | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Hawkins-Kennedy, Neer and painful arc | May reproduce subacromial pain | Non-specific and not diagnostic alone |
| Empty/full can and resisted external rotation | Explore pain and cuff-related weakness | Interpret pain inhibition and true weakness separately |
| Horizontal adduction and adjacent-structure tests | Explore competing pain sources | Limited subacromial discrimination in isolation |

### Combining Findings
No single test is sufficiently accurate for isolated diagnosis. Interpret a coherent pattern of pain provocation, strength, function and response to load, and consider competing diagnoses.

---

## Red Flags

- Chest, jaw or arm symptoms with dyspnoea or other acute cardiac features require emergency assessment/999.
- Progressive weakness, rapid muscle wasting or focal neurological deficit requires prompt medical/neurological assessment.
- Infection, malignancy, fracture/dislocation or neurovascular compromise requires escalation according to the presentation.
- Possible referred diaphragmatic pain requires medical assessment in context rather than automatic imaging.

Do not use disproportionate night pain plus age alone as an automatic suspected-cancer pathway rule; apply current NICE NG12/local criteria.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Lewis Shoulder Framework (2009, 2016)
Jeremy Lewis's pragmatic approach to shoulder assessment challenges the impingement model:

1. **Assessment Philosophy:** Pain provocation tests have poor diagnostic accuracy; use clusters not single tests
2. **Treatment Prioritisation:** Shoulder exercise regardless of specific diagnosis; acromioplasty not supported
3. **Staging by behaviour:**
   - Stage 1: Pain only at end range/high load — progressive exercise tolerated
   - Stage 2: Pain through range — modify loading; isometrics may reduce pain
   - Stage 3: Pain at rest — protect and manage; graded return to load
4. **Key principle:** The painful arc itself is not diagnostic — assess what provokes, what reduces, and response to load

### Cools Rehabilitation Model (2016)
**Scapular focus in SAPS rehabilitation:**
- Assess scapular kinematics: upward rotation deficit (SARD) strongly associated with SAPS
- Target serratus anterior and lower trapezius activation
- Correct upper trapezius dominance
- Posterior shoulder stretching for posterior capsule tightness (GIRD — Glenohumeral Internal Rotation Deficit)

**GIRD (Glenohumeral Internal Rotation Deficit):**
- IR deficit >25° compared to contralateral = significant
- Posterior capsule tightness → anterior-superior humeral head migration → subacromial space reduction
- Posterior capsule stretching (sleeper stretch, cross-body stretch) — evidence from throwing athletes

### Biopsychosocial Considerations
- Central sensitisation contributes to chronic SAPS — screen with PainDETECT
- Catastrophising, fear-avoidance, low self-efficacy are predictors of poor outcome
- Address from first consultation; avoid overmedicalising with imaging-driven diagnoses

---

## Outcome Measures

| Measure | Score Range | MCID | Clinical Application |
|---------|-------------|------|---------------------|
| **SPADI** | 0–100 (0 = best) | 8–13 pts | Best validated for SAPS specifically; pain (5 items) + disability (8 items) |
| **QuickDASH** | 0–100 (0 = best) | 11–16 pts | Quick screen; upper limb function |
| **DASH** | 0–100 (0 = best) | 10–15 pts | Comprehensive; work and sport modules |
| **OSS** | 0–48 (48 = best) | 5 pts | 12-item; used in NI orthopaedic pathway |
| **Constant-Murley** | 0–100 (100 = best) | 10–16 pts | For pre/post-surgical comparison; requires dynamometer |
| **PSFS** | 0–10 per activity | 2 pts | Patient-specific; most clinically responsive for individual goals |
| **ASES** | 0–100 (100 = best) | 12–17 pts | American standard; pain + function composite |

**NI HSC recommended practice:** SPADI at baseline and discharge as minimum; add Constant-Murley for surgical pathway cases.

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### CSAW Trial (Beard et al., 2018 — JAMA)
- Multicentre RCT; n=313; 3 arms: (1) arthroscopic acromioplasty + physio; (2) arthroscopic lavage (sham) + physio; (3) physiotherapy alone
- Primary outcome (Oxford Shoulder Score) at 6 months: **No significant difference between groups**
- Conclusion: Acromioplasty does not add benefit beyond physiotherapy
- Impact: Major shift in surgical practice; acromioplasty rates declining significantly in UK/Ireland

### Imaging Evidence
**USS (Ultrasound):**
- Identifies bursal thickening, rotator cuff changes, calcific deposits
- Dynamic assessment of subacromial space
- Not a routine first-line investigation under the BESS primary/community pathway; use when an agreed pathway or a clinical question will change management

**MRI:**
- Better for partial-thickness tears, labral pathology, bone marrow changes
- MR arthrogram where structural tear or instability suspected

**X-ray findings in SAPS:**
- Acromion morphology (AP + outlet view)
- Acromioclavicular joint changes
- Calcific deposits
- Greater tuberosity sclerosis/cyst

### Clinical Prediction Rules
- No single validated CPR for SAPS diagnosis equivalent to those in lumbar spine
- A combination of concordant history and examination findings is more useful than a single manoeuvre
- Response to injection can confirm subacromial source (functional diagnostic injection)

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Diagnosis | Key Features | Differentiating assessment |
|---|---|---|
| Rotator cuff tendinopathy | Overlaps significantly with SAPS | Load response and muscle tests interpreted with the history |
| Full-thickness rotator-cuff tear | Significant weakness; lag signs may be present | Lag/drop-arm findings and the overall traumatic or degenerative presentation |
| Adhesive capsulitis | Global restriction with a capsular pattern | Restriction across directions |
| AC-joint pathology | Direct AC tenderness and cross-body pain | AC palpation and cross-body loading |
| Calcific tendinopathy | Acute severe pain that can mimic infection | Clinical context and the presence of a calcium deposit when investigated |
| Glenohumeral OA | Gradual restriction, crepitus and stiffness | Joint pattern and clinical context |
| Cervical radiculopathy | Neck/arm symptoms and neurological signs | Cervical and neurological examination |
| Labral pathology or instability | Trauma, apprehension, catching or recurrent instability | Instability/labral assessment interpreted in context |
| Thoracic outlet syndrome | Positional neurovascular symptoms | Neurovascular assessment and subtype-specific specialist evaluation |
| Brachial neuritis | Severe pain followed by rapid weakness | Neurological pattern and medical assessment |

Investigation choice should follow the suspected diagnosis, red-flag assessment and appropriate specialist pathway; no imaging or electrodiagnostic test in this table is an automatic confirmatory step.

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Use the current local shoulder pathway and an individualised exercise-led plan when serious pathology has been excluded. Progressive neurological change or marked traumatic weakness follows a separate prompt specialist pathway.

---

### Conservative Management

**Exercise Programme (Evidence Grade A — multiple RCTs, Cochrane):**

| Exercise Type | Example | Dosage | Rationale |
|---------------|---------|--------|-----------|
| Scapular stabilisation | Prone Y/T/W; band retraction; push-up plus | 3×15 reps, daily | Lower trap/serratus activation; correct dyskinesis |
| RC activation | Side-lying ER; ER at 0°; band ER | 3×12–15 reps, daily | Restore ER:IR ratio; reduce superior migration |
| Progressive loading | Scaption; shoulder press; HSR | 3×8–12 reps progressive | Tendon adaptation; pain modulation |
| Posterior stretching | Sleeper stretch; cross-body stretch | 3×30 sec, daily | Correct GIRD; improve IR ROM |
| Thoracic mobility | Thoracic extension; rotation | Daily | Improve scapular platform |

**Manual therapy (Moderate evidence):**
- Thoracic spine manipulation: short-term improvement in pain and function (Bang & Deyle, 2000; Haik et al., 2016)
- Glenohumeral mobilisation (posterior capsule): Grade III–IV for internal rotation deficit
- NOT recommended as standalone; adjunct to exercise

**Education (Essential):**
- Explain SAPS framework — avoid impingement/wear-and-tear language (negative effects on prognosis)
- Activity modification: avoid provocative positions initially; graded return
- Self-management: posture, sleep position, ergonomics
- Prognosis: most SAPS resolves with structured exercise over 3–6 months (80% satisfactory outcome)

### Nonsurgical medical care

Use the current BESS Subacromial Shoulder Pain pathway and condition-specific evidence. Analgesia or corticosteroid injection may be discussed by an appropriate clinician, but this page does not specify a drug dose, PPI age threshold, image-guidance superiority figure, annual injection limit or mandatory waiting period because those exact claims have not yet been source-verified.

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency:** acute cardiac features or another emergency presentation.
- **Prompt medical/specialist assessment:** progressive neurological deficit, rapid wasting, marked traumatic weakness, infection or other serious pathology concern.
- **Specialist shoulder assessment:** persistent disabling symptoms or diagnostic uncertainty despite appropriate rehabilitation.

Version 1 does not mandate MRI or a fixed referral duration from a general red-flag label.

---

## Key References

1. Beard DJ, Rees JL, Cook JA, et al; CSAW Study Group. Arthroscopic subacromial decompression for subacromial shoulder pain (CSAW): a multicentre, pragmatic, parallel group, placebo-controlled, three-group, randomised surgical trial. *Lancet.* 2018;391(10118):329–338.
2. Lewis J. Subacromial impingement syndrome: a musculoskeletal condition or a clinical illusion? *Phys Ther Rev.* 2011;16(5):388–398.
3. Hegedus EJ, Goode AP, Cook CE, et al. Which physical examination tests provide clinicians with the most value when examining the shoulder? Update of a systematic review with meta-analysis of individual tests. *Br J Sports Med.* 2012;46(14):964–978.
4. Michener LA, Walsworth MK, Doukas WC, Murphy KP. Reliability and diagnostic accuracy of 5 physical examination tests and combination of tests for subacromial impingement. *Arch Phys Med Rehabil.* 2009;90(11):1898–1903.
5. BESS. *Subacromial Shoulder Pain – 2025*. [Patient care pathways and guidelines](https://bess.ac.uk/patient-care-pathways-and-guidelines/).
6. Koester MC, Dunn WR, Kuhn JE, Spindler KP. The efficacy of subacromial corticosteroid injection in the treatment of rotator cuff disease: a systematic review. *J Am Acad Orthop Surg.* 2007;15(1):3–11.
7. Cools AM, Johansson FR, Borms D, Maenhout A. Prevention of shoulder injuries in overhead athletes: a science-based approach. *Braz J Phys Ther.* 2015;19(5):331–339.
8. Haik MN, Alburquerque-Sendín F, Camargo PR. Short-term effects of thoracic spine manipulation on shoulder impingement syndrome. *Arch Phys Med Rehabil.* 2014;95(12):2229–2236.
9. Neer CS II. Anterior acromioplasty for the chronic impingement syndrome in the shoulder: a preliminary report. *J Bone Joint Surg Am.* 1972;54(1):41–50.

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Cubital tunnel syndrome (CuTS) is the second most common peripheral nerve entrapment syndrome (after carpal tunnel syndrome), accounting for approximately 20% of peripheral nerve compressions. Prevalence estimates range from 25–38 per 100,000 person-years; higher in manual workers, musicians, and those with habitual elbow flexion.

**Anatomy of the Cubital Tunnel:**
The cubital tunnel is a fibroosseous tunnel at the medial elbow through which the ulnar nerve passes. Key anatomical structures:
- **Floor:** Medial collateral ligament (ulnar head) and posterior capsule
- **Roof:** Osborne's fascia (arcuate ligament) — the most common site of compression
- **Walls:** Medial epicondyle (medial), olecranon (lateral)
- **Contents:** Ulnar nerve + accompanying vessels

**Other sites of ulnar nerve entrapment at the elbow:**
1. Medial intermuscular septum (proximal compression)
2. Arcade of Struthers (fascial band, 8 cm proximal to medial epicondyle)
3. Cubital tunnel (Osborne's fascia) — most common
4. Between heads of flexor carpi ulnaris (distal to cubital tunnel)

**Pathophysiology of compression:**
- During elbow flexion, the cubital tunnel narrows by 55% (MacNicol 1979), increasing intraneural pressure
- Normal nerve: 7–8 mmHg at rest; cubital tunnel pressure rises to 100–200 mmHg at 90° flexion
- Sustained compression → intraneural oedema → myelin loss → axonal degeneration (in severe/chronic cases)
- Nerve subluxation (snapping) over medial epicondyle in 20% of population → repetitive traction injury

**Risk factors:**
- Habitual elbow flexion (sleeping, driving, phone use)
- Prolonged leaning on medial elbow
- Repetitive elbow flexion-extension (overhead athletes, musicians)
- Medial epicondyle fracture/deformity
- Cubitus valgus deformity
- Medial elbow osteoarthritis, loose bodies
- Diabetes mellitus (peripheral neuropathy — double crush)
- Obesity, smoking

---

## Special Tests

| Assessment component | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Elbow-flexion, pressure-provocation and Tinel-type tests | May reproduce ulnar-distribution symptoms | No single provocation test confirms compression |
| Froment's, Wartenberg's and intrinsic-muscle inspection | Explore motor involvement | Abnormal findings require localisation and differential assessment |
| Sensory, grip and pinch assessment | Establish deficit and monitor function | Non-specific in isolation |

**Elbow Flexion Test (Wadsworth 1987):** Patient maximally flexes elbow with wrist extended, held for 60 seconds. Positive = tingling/numbness in ring and little finger. Best single test for CuTS.

**Combined Provocation Test:** Elbow flexion + wrist extension + pressure over the cubital tunnel may reproduce familiar ulnar-distribution symptoms. Interpret it with the full neurological examination.

**Scratch Collapse Test (Cheng et al. 2008):** Examiner scratches skin over suspected compression site; patient momentarily loses shoulder resistance. Highly specific for nerve compression location.

**Froment's Sign:** Patient holds paper between thumb and index finger; examiner pulls paper away. Positive = thumb IP joint flexes (compensating for adductor pollicis weakness with FPL) — indicates ulnar nerve motor involvement.

---

## Red Flags

- Marked or progressive ulnar motor weakness, intrinsic-muscle wasting, clawing or major sensory loss requires prompt neurological/specialist assessment.
- Trauma with fracture/dislocation or acute neurovascular compromise requires urgent/emergency assessment.
- Atypical systemic, malignancy or mass-lesion features require appropriate medical assessment.

Do not infer that two possible compression sites both require treatment. Evaluate each clinically. Do not make neurophysiology, imaging or decompression automatic from the red-flag statement.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### McGowan Classification of CuTS Severity

| Grade | Symptoms | Sensory | Motor | Management |
|-------|----------|---------|-------|------------|
| I (Mild) | Intermittent paraesthesia; minimal | Normal or intermittent loss | Normal intrinsics | Conservative (splinting, activity modification) |
| II (Moderate) | Persistent paraesthesia; aching | Constant sensory deficit | Moderate weakness | Neurological/specialist assessment according to progression and response |
| III (Severe) | Constant symptoms | Marked sensory deficit | Significant wasting; clawing | Surgical decompression; conservative unlikely to succeed |

### Dellon Classification
- Mild: Intermittent paraesthesia; positive elbow flexion test; no muscle weakness
- Moderate: Intermittent paraesthesia; positive provocative tests; weak interossei
- Severe: Persistent paraesthesia; clawing; intrinsic muscle atrophy

### Neurodynamic Assessment
Upper Limb Neurodynamic Test 3 (ULNT3 — ulnar bias):
- Position: Shoulder depression, wrist/finger extension, elbow flexion, shoulder abduction, cervical lateral flexion away
- Sensitising: Cervical lateral flexion toward; wrist extension/flexion
- Positive: Reproduction of concordant symptoms; asymmetrical response vs contralateral
- Use for: Screening, differentiating peripheral vs central sensitisation, guiding neural mobilisation

---

## Outcome Measures

### Patient-Reported Measures
**DASH / QuickDASH:**
- Most widely used for upper limb nerve compression
- MCID: DASH 10.2 points; QuickDASH 16 points
- Work module available for occupational contexts

**Boston/Levine CTS Questionnaire:** Not validated for CuTS; use DASH instead.

**Patient-Rated Ulnar Nerve Evaluation (PRUNE):** Validated specifically for ulnar neuropathy; 17 items; symptom and function subscales. Less widely available but more condition-specific.

### Objective Measures
**Grip Strength (Jamar):**
- Compare dominant vs non-dominant; affected vs unaffected
- MCID: 6.5 kg; ICC 0.97–0.98
- Serial measurement tracks motor recovery post-decompression

**Pinch Strength (lateral and tip pinch):**
- Tip pinch directly measures intrinsic function
- Lateral pinch tests adductor pollicis (ulnar nerve) + FPL (median nerve)

**Sensory Testing:**
- Semmes-Weinstein monofilament testing: quantifies sensory threshold in ring/little finger
- 2-point discrimination (static and moving): >6 mm static 2PD = abnormal; tracks recovery
- Vibration threshold (128 Hz tuning fork): reduced in ulnar distribution in moderate/severe CuTS

**Functional Dexterity:**
- Purdue Pegboard test (manual dexterity; sensitive to intrinsic muscle weakness)
- Nine-hole peg test: functional fine motor speed assessment

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

**Clinical diagnosis** based on symptom pattern + provocative tests is appropriate for initial management. NCS confirmation required before surgical intervention.

**Nerve Conduction Studies (NCS):**
- May support localisation and severity assessment; the neurophysiology service interprets thresholds in clinical context
- Compound Motor Action Potential (CMAP) amplitude: reduced in axonal degeneration
- Sensory nerve action potential (SNAP): reduced or absent in sensory fibre involvement
- Inching technique: localises compression to specific 2 cm segment
- **Referral pathway:** Use the current local neurological/neurophysiology pathway; service locations and waiting times must be confirmed locally

**EMG:**
- Detects denervation in FDI, ADM, FCU
- Chronic denervation: fibrillations, positive sharp waves, reduced recruitment
- Useful for severity stratification and surgical planning

**Ultrasound:**
- May demonstrate nerve enlargement or dynamic subluxation; measurements require trained interpretation and are not a standalone diagnosis
- Dynamic assessment: nerve subluxation over medial epicondyle
- Increasingly used as first-line imaging; lower cost than MRI

**MRI:**
- Nerve signal change, perineural fibrosis, intraneural oedema
- Identifies compressive lesions (ganglion, accessory muscle, post-traumatic fibrosis)
- Reserved for diagnostic uncertainty or surgical planning

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Distinguishing Features | Key Investigation |
|-----------|------------------------|-------------------|
| Cervical radiculopathy C8 | Neck pain; Spurling's positive; triceps weakness; absent triceps reflex | Cervical MRI; NCS shows normal forearm ulnar velocity |
| Thoracic outlet syndrome | Consider when symptoms are positional and suggest neurovascular compression | Diagnosis is primarily clinical; investigations depend on the suspected neurogenic, arterial or venous subtype and specialist pathway |
| Medial epicondylalgia | Tendinopathy signs; no sensory deficit; no intrinsic weakness | Clinical; NCS normal |
| Guyon's canal syndrome (ulnar at wrist) | Spares FCU (wrist flexion intact); NCS shows distal compression; dorsal ulnar sensory SNAP normal | NCS/EMG; wrist ultrasound |
| Double crush syndrome | Combined cervical + cubital compression | NCS both regions; MRI spine |
| Pancoast tumour | Shoulder girdle pain; Horner's; weight loss; smoking history | Chest X-ray; CT chest |
| Peripheral neuropathy (diabetic, alcoholic) | Bilateral; length-dependent; other nerves involved | NCS bilateral; bloods |
| Ulnar nerve tumour | Firm, fixed mass on nerve; Tinel's at mass | MRI; ultrasound |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

For clear ulnar compression neuropathy without root-lesion features, NICE NG127 recommendations 1.7.9–1.7.10 support pressure avoidance, orthotic referral and review after 6 weeks, with neurological assessment if there is no improvement.

Progressive motor loss, marked wasting or an atypical presentation requires more prompt specialist assessment. Investigation and operative decisions remain specialist-led.

---

### Conservative Management

#### Activity Modification (Grade B)
- **Avoid sustained elbow flexion >90°**: phone use (speaker/headset), sleeping position
- **Avoid repetitive elbow flexion-extension** under load
- **Avoid medial elbow pressure**: padding on work surfaces, armrests
- Workplace ergonomic assessment (HSE NI guidance)

#### Elbow Extension Splinting at Night (Grade A)
- NICE NG127 recommendation 1.7.9 supports referral to orthotic services for a splint in adults with clear ulnar compression neuropathy and no radiculopathy, followed by review at 6 weeks
- The exact splint angle, wear schedule and duration require separate evidence and orthotic/hand-therapy review; NG127 does not prescribe them

#### Nerve Gliding Exercises (Grade B)
The previous detailed neurodynamic prescription is not established by NICE NG127. Any neural mobilisation requires an individual physiotherapy assessment and remains a claim-level evidence review task.

#### Education
- Sleep position modification (pillow under arm to prevent elbow flexion >90°)
- Phone/device use modification
- Workplace assessment: seated ergonomics, tool use, forearm support

---

### Pharmacological

NG127 does not provide a cubital-tunnel drug or injection regimen. Medication or injection requires confirmation of the indication and an individual prescriber/specialist decision; the previous doses and procedure claims have been removed pending exact evidence review.

---

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Prompt specialist/neurological assessment:** marked or progressive motor weakness, wasting, clawing, major sensory loss or diagnostic uncertainty.
- **NICE NG127 pathway:** for clear compression neuropathy without radiculopathy, use pressure avoidance, orthotic referral and 6-week review; refer for neurological assessment if there is no improvement.
- **Emergency/urgent assessment:** trauma with fracture/dislocation or acute neurovascular compromise.

Version 1 does not prescribe automatic decompression, electrodiagnostic thresholds or a fixed operative timeline.

---

## Key References

1. Assmus H, Antoniadis G, Bischoff C, Hoffmann R, Martini AK, Preissler P, et al. (2015). Cubital tunnel syndrome — a review and management guidelines. *Central European Neurosurgery*, 72(2), 90–98.

2. Novak CB, Lee GW, Mackinnon SE, Lay L. (1994). Provocative testing for cubital tunnel syndrome. *Journal of Hand Surgery*, 19(5), 817–820.

3. Cheng CJ, Mackinnon-Patterson B, Beck JL, Mackinnon SE. (2008). Scratch collapse test for evaluation of carpal and cubital tunnel syndrome. *Journal of Hand Surgery*, 33(9), 1518–1524.

4. Dellon AL, Hament W, Gittelshon A. (1993). Nonoperative management of cubital tunnel syndrome: an 8-year prospective study. *Neurology*, 43(9), 1673–1677.

5. McGowan AJ. (1950). The results of transposition of the ulnar nerve for traumatic ulnar neuritis. *Journal of Bone and Joint Surgery*, 32(2), 293–301.

6. Svernlöv B, Larsson M, Rehn K, Adolfsson L. (2009). Conservative treatment of the cubital tunnel syndrome. *Journal of Hand Surgery (European)*, 34(2), 201–207.

7. Zlowodzki M, Chan S, Bhandari M, Kalliainen L, Schubert W. (2007). Anterior transposition compared with simple decompression for treatment of cubital tunnel syndrome. *Journal of Bone and Joint Surgery*, 89(12), 2591–2598.

8. Macadam SA, Gandhi R, Bezuhly M, Lefaivre KA. (2008). Simple decompression versus anterior subcutaneous and submuscular transposition of the ulnar nerve for cubital tunnel syndrome. *Journal of Hand Surgery*, 33(8), 1314.e1–12.

9. Beekman R, Schoemaker MC, Van Der Plas JP, et al. (2004). Diagnostic value of high-resolution sonography in ulnar neuropathy at the elbow. *Neurology*, 62(5), 767–773.

10. NICE. *Suspected neurological conditions: recognition and referral*. NG127, recommendations 1.7.9–1.7.10. [Compression neuropathy recommendations](https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng127/chapter/Recommendations-for-adults-aged-over-16#compression-neuropathy)

11. Practice parameter: electrodiagnostic studies in ulnar neuropathy at the elbow. (1999). *Neurology*, 52(4), 688–690. (American Academy of Neurology)

### Cubital Tunnel special evidence review

Exact obsolete bibliography entry: NICE CKS. (2023). Peripheral neuropathy. https://cks.nice.org.uk/topics/peripheral-neuropathy/ (removed because the URL is obsolete)

Decision: **PARTIAL SUPPORT — HUMAN EVIDENCE DECISION REQUIRED FOR THE HISTORICAL CLAIM BUNDLE**

NG127 1.7.9–1.7.10 IS NOW MAPPED ONLY TO SPLINT REFERRAL, SIX-WEEK REVIEW/NEUROLOGICAL ASSESSMENT, AND PRESSURE AVOIDANCE

NG127 explicitly supports those limited statements. It does not establish the former splint angle/duration, neurodynamic prescription, electrodiagnostic thresholds, operative thresholds or 3–6 month surgical-referral rule.

Candidate section: **Management & Treatment**

The complete Management & Treatment section is reproduced because it contains the potentially related splinting, activity-modification and referral statements; none is asserted to be mapped to the obsolete source.

```md
### Governed Care Pathway

For clear ulnar compression neuropathy without root-lesion features, NICE NG127 recommendations 1.7.9–1.7.10 support pressure avoidance, orthotic referral and review after 6 weeks, with neurological assessment if there is no improvement.

Progressive motor loss, marked wasting or an atypical presentation requires more prompt specialist assessment. Investigation and operative decisions remain specialist-led.

---

### Conservative Management

#### Activity Modification (Grade B)
- **Avoid sustained elbow flexion >90°**: phone use (speaker/headset), sleeping position
- **Avoid repetitive elbow flexion-extension** under load
- **Avoid medial elbow pressure**: padding on work surfaces, armrests
- Workplace ergonomic assessment (HSE NI guidance)

#### Elbow Extension Splinting at Night (Grade A)
- NICE NG127 recommendation 1.7.9 supports referral to orthotic services for a splint in adults with clear ulnar compression neuropathy and no radiculopathy, followed by review at 6 weeks
- The exact splint angle, wear schedule and duration require separate evidence and orthotic/hand-therapy review; NG127 does not prescribe them

#### Nerve Gliding Exercises (Grade B)
The previous detailed neurodynamic prescription is not established by NICE NG127. Any neural mobilisation requires an individual physiotherapy assessment and remains a claim-level evidence review task.

#### Education
- Sleep position modification (pillow under arm to prevent elbow flexion >90°)
- Phone/device use modification
- Workplace assessment: seated ergonomics, tool use, forearm support

---

### Pharmacological

NG127 does not provide a cubital-tunnel drug or injection regimen. Medication or injection requires confirmation of the indication and an individual prescriber/specialist decision; the previous doses and procedure claims have been removed pending exact evidence review.

---

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Prompt specialist/neurological assessment:** marked or progressive motor weakness, wasting, clawing, major sensory loss or diagnostic uncertainty.
- **NICE NG127 pathway:** for clear compression neuropathy without radiculopathy, use pressure avoidance, orthotic referral and 6-week review; refer for neurological assessment if there is no improvement.
- **Emergency/urgent assessment:** trauma with fracture/dislocation or acute neurovascular compromise.

Version 1 does not prescribe automatic decompression, electrodiagnostic thresholds or a fixed operative timeline.

---
```

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview

Elbow osteoarthritis (OA) is a degenerative joint disease characterised by progressive loss of articular cartilage, subchondral bone changes, osteophyte formation, and synovial inflammation within the elbow joint complex (humeroulnar, humeroradial, and proximal radioulnar joints). It is relatively uncommon compared to hip and knee OA, accounting for approximately **2% of all OA presentations**, but causes significant functional impairment given the elbow's role in positioning the hand in space.

### Epidemiology

- Prevalence: ~2% of the general population; higher in heavy manual workers and throwing athletes
- Peak onset: 5th–6th decade; male:female ratio approximately 4:1 in primary OA
- **Primary (idiopathic) OA** accounts for ~50% of elbow OA; often bilateral
- **Secondary OA** follows prior trauma (post-traumatic), inflammatory arthropathy, osteochondral defects, or repetitive occupational loading

### Pathoanatomy

Elbow OA characteristically produces:
- Osteophyte formation at the **coronoid fossa, olecranon fossa, and radial head**
- **Loose body formation** — a cardinal feature; fragments from osteophytes or osteochondral surfaces cause mechanical symptoms
- Progressive loss of terminal flexion and extension (terminal arc most affected first)
- Relative preservation of the mid-arc (60°–120°) until advanced disease
- Capsular contracture contributing to stiffness

### Typical Clinical Presentation

| Feature | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Pain** | End-range aching; may be sharp with loose body impingement |
| **Stiffness** | Loss of terminal extension > terminal flexion initially |
| **Functional arc** | Typically 30°–130° preserved in mild–moderate disease |
| **Locking/catching** | Suggests loose body; episodic, position-dependent |
| **Crepitus** | Coarse, palpable; reproducible with passive ROM |
| **Swelling** | Effusion palpable in posterolateral triangle |
| **Occupation/sport history** | Heavy manual work, throwing athletes, combat sports |

> **Normal elbow ROM for reference:** Flexion 0°–145°; Pronation 75°; Supination 85°. Functional arc: 30°–130° flexion-extension; 50° pronation–50° supination (Morrey et al., 1981).

---

## Red Flags

Findings atypical for elbow osteoarthritis—such as a hot acutely inflamed joint, systemic illness, progressive neurological deficit, rapidly changing swelling/mass, major trauma or suspected malignancy—require medical or specialist assessment according to urgency.

Suspected septic arthritis requires prompt urgent assessment. Do not make aspiration, antibiotics, nerve-conduction testing, imaging or a fixed specialty route automatic in the learner wording.

General NICE NG226 osteoarthritis recommendations must be labelled as general OA guidance where elbow-specific evidence is unavailable.

---

## Special Tests

> Sensitivity (Sn) and specificity (Sp) data are derived from published validation studies. Most elbow OA-specific test accuracy data are limited; values reflect best available evidence and are presented with appropriate caution.

### Range of Motion Examination (Essential)

Active and passive ROM measurement with a standard goniometer is the cornerstone of elbow OA assessment. Loss of terminal extension is the earliest and most consistent finding.

### Provocative & Diagnostic Tests

| Test | Technique | Positive Finding | Sensitivity | Specificity | Clinical Use in Elbow OA |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Terminal arc pain** (Outerbridge–Kashiwagi sign) | Passive flexion–extension through full range; note where pain is provoked | Pain at end-range extension (<30°) and/or end-range flexion (>120°) | ~85% (expert consensus) | Moderate | Confirms terminal arc impingement — hallmark of elbow OA |
| **Loose body provocation test** | Examiner passively moves elbow through arc while applying axial compression | Reproducible locking, catching, or sharp pain | Not formally validated | — | Suggests intra-articular loose body; consider imaging |
| **Posterolateral rotatory instability (PLRI) test** (lateral pivot shift) | Supine; shoulder abducted 90°, forearm supinated; apply valgus + supination + axial compression through elbow flexion | Apprehension or subluxation of radial head | Sn 38%–100% (wide range; highly examiner-dependent) | Sp ~96% (O'Driscoll et al., 1991) | Rules in ligamentous instability contributing to OA pattern |
| **Valgus stress test** (UCL integrity) | Elbow at 15°–30° flexion; apply valgus force; compare medial joint opening | Pain ± gapping medially | Sn 65% (Safran & Ahmad, 2005) | Sp 60% | UCL insufficiency secondary to valgus overload; relevant in throwing athletes |
| **Milking manoeuvre** (UCL) | Traction on thumb with elbow >90° flexion and shoulder ER; valgus stress applied | Medial elbow pain | Sn 82% (O'Driscoll et al., 2005) | Sp 91% | UCL integrity; differentiates medial pain source |
| **Tinel's sign at cubital tunnel** | Percussion over ulnar nerve in cubital tunnel (medial epicondyle groove) | Paraesthesia in ring/little finger distribution | Sn 54–70% | Sp 77–98% (Novak et al., 1994) | Detects cubital tunnel syndrome — common OA co-pathology |
| **Elbow flexion test** | Maximal elbow flexion + wrist extension sustained 60 seconds | Paraesthesia ulnar nerve distribution | Sn 75% | Sp 99% (Novak et al., 1994) | Sensitive screen for cubital tunnel syndrome |
| **Radiocapitellar compression test** | Elbow slightly flexed; passive forearm rotation (pronation/supination) with axial compression | Lateral elbow pain | Not formally validated for OA | — | Screens radiocapitellar joint involvement; also used in radial head pathology |
| **Extension impingement test** | Rapid passive extension of elbow | Posterior elbow pain at end-range | Not formally validated | — | Posterior osteophyte / olecranon impingement |
| **Crepitus assessment** | Palpation during passive ROM | Coarse crepitus throughout arc or at terminal range | Moderate examiner agreement | — | Consistent feature of cartilage degeneration |

> **Note:** Elbow OA diagnosis is primarily **clinical + radiological**. No single provocative test is pathognomonic. Imaging (plain radiograph) is required for confirmation and surgical planning.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Outerbridge–Kashiwagi (OK) Classification (Radiological/Intraoperative)

Widely used for surgical planning but informs clinical staging:

| Grade | Description |
|---|---|
| I | Osteophytes at coronoid/olecranon tip; joint space preserved |
| II | Moderate osteophytes; mild joint space narrowing; loose bodies may be present |
| III | Severe osteophytes; significant joint space narrowing; multiple loose bodies |
| IV | End-stage: complete joint space loss; gross deformity |

### Broberg & Morrey Functional Rating System

Used in outcome assessment for elbow OA interventions:
- **Score 0–100** based on pain (45 pts), motion (25 pts), stability (10 pts), function (20 pts)
- Excellent: 96–100 | Good: 80–95 | Fair: 60–79 | Poor: <60

### Gschwend Classification (Clinical Staging)

| Stage | Clinical Features |
|---|---|
| 1 | Mild stiffness; normal or near-normal plain film |
| 2 | Moderate stiffness + pain; osteophytes; mild joint space narrowing |
| 3 | Severe stiffness; pain throughout arc; significant radiological change |
| 4 | End-stage; gross deformity; instability possible |

### ICF Framework Application (HSC NI)

| ICF Domain | Elbow OA Application |
|---|---|
| **Body structure/function** | ROM loss, pain, crepitus, effusion, muscle strength deficit |
| **Activity** | Reaching overhead, carrying, fine motor tasks, driving |
| **Participation** | Work capability (especially manual workers), sports/leisure, ADLs |
| **Contextual factors** | Heavy occupational exposure, prior trauma, BMI, psychological factors |

---

## Outcome Measures

### 1. DASH (Disabilities of the Arm, Shoulder and Hand)

- **Type:** Patient-reported outcome measure (PROM); 30-item questionnaire
- **Scale:** 0–100 (0 = no disability; 100 = maximum disability)
- **Domains:** Physical function/symptoms (mandatory); optional work and sport/performing arts modules
- **MCID:** **10–15 points** (Gummesson et al., 2006; Franchignoni et al., 2014)
- **MDC (90%):** 12.7 points
- **Recall period:** Past week
- **Time to complete:** 5–10 minutes
- **Strengths:** Well-validated across upper limb conditions; widely used in NHS and HSC NI
- **Limitations:** Less sensitive than QuickDASH for detecting change in elbow-specific conditions; ceiling effects in mild-moderate disease

### 2. QuickDASH

- **Type:** 11-item abbreviated DASH
- **Scale:** 0–100
- **MCID:** **15.9 points** (Mintken et al., 2009)
- **MDC:** 21.1 points
- **Note:** Acceptable alternative to DASH where brevity required; strong correlation (r=0.97) with full DASH

### 3. Oxford Elbow Score (OES)

- **Type:** Elbow-specific PROM; 12 items across 3 domains
- **Domains:** Elbow function (4 items), pain (4 items), social-psychological (4 items)
- **Scale:** 0–48 (higher = better outcome) OR converted to 0–100 scale
- **MCID:** **~11 points** (on 0–100 scale) (Dawson et al., 2008; Baker et al., 2012)
- **MDC:** Data limited; approximately 12–14 points suggested
- **Strengths:** Elbow-specific; validated for total elbow arthroplasty and elbow OA; sensitive to change post-intervention
- **Limitations:** Less widely known than DASH; less normative data outside surgical populations

### 4. ASES Elbow Score (American Shoulder and Elbow Surgeons)

- **Type:** Combined physician-rated + patient-reported; 100-point scale
- **Domains:** Pain (Visual Analogue Scale, 50 pts) + Function/ADLs (50 pts)
- **Scale:** 0–100 (higher = better)
- **MCID:** **11–13 points** (Jayakumar et al., 2017; formally validated in arthroplasty but extrapolated to conservative management)
- **Strengths:** Used in most elbow surgical outcome literature; allows direct comparison with operative cohorts; separates pain from function
- **Limitations:** Physician component requires trained administration; less validated for conservative physiotherapy outcomes than DASH

### Recommended Outcome Measure Protocol (HSC NI)

| Timepoint | Recommended Measures |
|---|---|
| Initial assessment | DASH (or QuickDASH) + OES + NRS pain (0–10) + ROM (goniometry) |
| 6-week review | DASH/QuickDASH + NRS pain + ROM |
| Discharge | DASH/QuickDASH + OES + Global Rating of Change (GRC) |
| Surgical referral | DASH + OES + ASES (for surgical baseline) |

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

### Diagnostic Criteria (Clinical + Radiological)

Elbow OA diagnosis requires **at least two of the following** (adapted from OARSI and clinical consensus):

1. **Age ≥40 years** + insidious or post-traumatic onset
2. **Elbow pain** provoked by terminal ROM
3. **Crepitus** on passive ROM
4. **Radiological changes**: joint space narrowing, subchondral sclerosis, osteophytes, loose bodies

No validated standalone clinical diagnostic criteria exist analogous to ACR criteria for knee/hip OA. Imaging remains the standard for confirmation.

### Imaging

| Modality | Indication | Key Findings |
|---|---|---|
| **Plain radiograph (AP + lateral)** | First-line for all suspected elbow OA | Osteophytes (coronoid, olecranon, radial head); joint space narrowing; subchondral sclerosis; loose bodies |
| **CT** | Pre-surgical planning; loose body localisation | 3D osteophyte mapping; accurate loose body count and location |
| **MRI** | Suspected soft tissue co-pathology; UCL assessment; AVN; early cartilage changes | Cartilage loss, synovitis, ligamentous injury, nerve assessment |
| **Ultrasound** | Guided injection; effusion assessment; dynamic tendon assessment | Effusion; synovitis; osteophyte margins (limited joint visualisation) |

### Nerve Conduction Studies / EMG

Elbow OA can coexist with or contribute to ulnar neuropathy. If ulnar neuropathy is suspected, assess clinically and consider electrodiagnostic testing when needed to clarify diagnosis or severity, or to support specialist planning.

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Key Differentiating Features | Investigation |
|---|---|---|
| **Rheumatoid arthritis (elbow)** | Bilateral; morning stiffness >1hr; systemic features; warm joint; raised CRP/ESR; RF+/anti-CCP+ | Bloods: CRP, ESR, RF, anti-CCP; hand/wrist X-ray |
| **Psoriatic arthritis** | Psoriasis history/family history; DIP involvement; enthesitis; dactylitis | Dermatology review; HLA-B27; imaging |
| **Gout / pseudogout** | Acute episodic flares; olecranon tophi (gout); hot swollen joint; hyperuricaemia | Serum urate; synovial fluid for crystals; X-ray |
| **Septic arthritis** | Fever; raised WBC/CRP; very hot/swollen joint; systemically unwell; rapid onset | **Emergency**: joint aspiration; blood cultures; WBC |
| **Cubital tunnel syndrome** | Medial elbow pain + ulnar paraesthesia (ring/little finger); intrinsic wasting | NCS/EMG; Tinel's; elbow flexion test |
| **Lateral epicondylalgia** | Lateral pain on resisted wrist extension/gripping; point-tender lateral epicondyle; no ROM loss initially | Clinical diagnosis; USS if uncertain; exclude radial nerve entrapment |
| **Medial epicondylalgia** | Medial pain on resisted wrist flexion/pronation; no joint line tenderness | Clinical diagnosis |
| **Osteochondritis dissecans (OCD)** | Younger patient (<25); lateral elbow pain; activity-related; radiocapitellar on X-ray/MRI | Plain X-ray; MRI for lesion stability |
| **Radial head fracture/non-union** | Trauma history; lateral pain; limited supination/pronation; X-ray confirms | X-ray; CT if occult |
| **Posterior interosseous nerve entrapment** | Weakness of finger/wrist extensors without sensory loss; lateral elbow pain; no joint line crepitus | EMG/NCS; MRI |
| **Cervical radiculopathy (C6/C7)** | Neck pain; dermatomal distribution; Spurling's +ve; pain not localised to joint | Cervical X-ray/MRI; NCS |
| **Olecranon bursitis** | Posterior swelling over olecranon tip; joint ROM preserved; fluctuant; may be septic | Aspiration if septic suspected; USS |
| **Haemophilic arthropathy** | Known haemophilia; recurrent haemarthroses; X-ray changes similar to OA | Clotting screen; haematology |
| **PVNS (pigmented villonodular synovitis)** | Recurrent haemarthrosis without adequate trauma; young adult; MRI characteristic | MRI (blooming artefact on T2*); synovial biopsy |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Apply general NICE NG226 osteoarthritis principles cautiously where elbow-specific evidence is unavailable. Use the current local pathway, individualised education/exercise and symptom management. Atypical, inflammatory, neurological or infective presentations require separate assessment.

---

### Conservative Management

#### 1. Education & Self-Management (NICE NG226 — Core Treatment)

All patients with elbow OA should receive:
- **Explanation of the diagnosis and natural history**: Elbow OA is manageable; most patients maintain function without surgery
- **Activity modification**: Avoid provocative end-range loading; adapt occupational activities where possible
- **Joint protection principles**: Ergonomic advice for work and daily tasks
- **Self-management resources**: Arthritis Research UK / Versus Arthritis patient information

#### 2. Exercise Therapy (NICE NG226 — general OA core treatment; applied to the elbow)

- **Strengthening**: Progressive resistance exercise targeting elbow flexors/extensors, forearm pronators/supinators, shoulder girdle musculature
  - Evidence: Strengthening reduces pain and improves function in peripheral joint OA (NICE NG226; Fernandes et al., 2013)
  - Frequency: 2–3x/week progressive loading; 8–12 weeks minimum
- **Range of motion / flexibility**: Daily active and active-assisted ROM exercises to maintain functional arc
  - Focus: Maintaining 30°–130° functional arc
  - Caution: Avoid aggressive passive stretching in the presence of significant osteophytes — can exacerbate impingement
- **Aerobic exercise**: NG226 supports exercise for OA generally; the elbow-specific programme and dosage must be individualised
- **Hydrotherapy**: May be considered for patients with significant stiffness and pain limiting land-based exercise (moderate evidence)

#### 3. Manual Therapy

- May be used **adjunctively** with exercise (not as a standalone treatment — NICE NG226)
- Mobilisation with movement (Mulligan concept) for ROM restoration: limited but positive evidence in elbow stiffness
- Soft tissue techniques for capsular tightness: some benefit in improving ROM; evidence extrapolated from post-traumatic elbow stiffness literature
- **Evidence level**: Low–moderate; insufficient elbow OA-specific RCT data

#### 4. Thermotherapy

- **Heat (moist heat/hot pack)**: Pre-exercise; may improve tissue extensibility and reduce pain; patient preference guides use
- **Ice**: Post-exercise; for acute inflammatory flares; olecranon bursitis management adjunct
- Thermotherapy is not presented as an elbow-specific NICE recommendation; any use is an adjunct based on patient preference and local practice

#### 5. Orthoses / Splinting

- **Dynamic splinting for flexion contracture**: Turnbuckle or static progressive splints — evidence from post-traumatic elbow contracture literature; may apply in severe OA contracture
- **Activity-modification bracing**: Limited role in elbow OA specifically; may reduce load during heavy occupational tasks
- **Referral to occupational therapy**: For adaptive equipment, workplace assessment, ADL strategies

#### 6. TENS

- TENS is not presented as an elbow-specific NICE recommendation; the evidence and any use require separate review

---

### Pharmacological Management (NICE NG226, 2022)

> Physiotherapists should be aware of the NICE NG226 pharmacological pathway for co-ordinated MDT management. Prescribing remains with the GP/prescribing clinician.

| Step | Agent | NICE NG226 Recommendation | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Topical NSAID** | NG226 says to **consider** a topical NSAID for OA affecting joints other than the knee | Elbow-specific efficacy and the prescription schedule are not established by that recommendation |
| **Oral NSAID** | Consider if topical treatment is ineffective or unsuitable, after individual GI, renal, liver and cardiovascular risk assessment | NG226 recommends gastroprotection while an NSAID is taken; prescribing remains clinician-led |
| **Paracetamol / weak opioid** | Do not routinely offer; NG226 permits only infrequent short-term use when other pharmacological treatments are contraindicated, not tolerated or ineffective | Not an elbow-specific endorsement |
| **Intra-articular corticosteroid injection** | Consider when other pharmacological treatments are ineffective/unsuitable or to support exercise; explain short-term relief (2–10 weeks) | Evidence was largely from other joints and is being applied cautiously; agent, dose and frequency are not specified here |
| **Strong opioid** | Do not offer for OA management | General NG226 recommendation |
| **Avoid** | **Glucosamine / chondroitin** | NG226: Do not offer | No clinically meaningful benefit demonstrated |
| **Avoid** | **Intra-articular hyaluronic acid** | Not recommended by NICE NG226 | Insufficient evidence of benefit in peripheral joint OA |

> **NICE NG226 core principle**: Pharmacological management should always be adjunctive to core treatments (education + exercise). Do not use pharmacology as a substitute for exercise-based management.

---

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Urgent assessment:** suspected septic arthritis, acute neurovascular compromise or significant traumatic injury.
- **Prompt medical/specialist assessment:** progressive neurological change, atypical systemic/inflammatory features, rapidly changing mass or suspected malignancy under applicable criteria.
- **Elective specialist assessment:** persistent disabling pain, stiffness or functional loss despite an appropriate conservative plan.

Investigation and operative choice remain clinician/specialist-led; Version 1 does not prescribe fixed operative thresholds.

---

## Key References

> Evidence base for elbow OA is predominantly Level III–IV (case series, expert consensus, extrapolation from other joints). RCT data are very limited.

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Lateral epicondylalgia (LE) — commonly termed "tennis elbow" — is the most prevalent elbow disorder in primary care, with a population prevalence of 1–3% and peak incidence in the 35–54 age group. It accounts for approximately 5% of all shoulder and arm complaints presenting to GPs, with an estimated annual incidence of 4–7 per 1000 patients.

**Pathophysiology:** Contemporary understanding has shifted decisively away from an inflammatory model. Histological studies (Nirschl & Pettrone 1979; Khan et al. 2002) consistently demonstrate angiofibroblastic hyperplasia rather than inflammatory infiltrate — absence of inflammatory cells, disordered collagen (Type III predominance), neurovascular in-growth, and failed healing response. The condition is therefore better classified as a **tendinopathy** rather than tendinitis.

**Primary structure involved:** Extensor carpi radialis brevis (ECRB) origin at the lateral epicondyle — specifically the anterior and superior aspect. ECRB is most vulnerable due to:
- Mechanical impingement against the capitellum during elbow extension and forearm pronation
- High tensile load during wrist extension under load
- Relatively avascular region at the origin contributing to failed healing

**Contributing structures:** Extensor digitorum communis, extensor carpi radialis longus, extensor carpi ulnaris.

**Risk factors:** Manual occupations, repetitive gripping >2 kg, keyboard/mouse use, racquet sports (backhand technique errors), age 40–50, smoking, obesity, previous episode.

**Natural history:** Spontaneous resolution expected in 80–90% within 12–18 months without treatment. However, recurrence rates are high (up to 72% at 5 years in some cohorts).

---

## Special Tests

| Assessment component | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Resisted wrist extension and gripping | Reproduces load-related lateral elbow symptoms | Not specific in isolation |
| Passive wrist-flexion stretch | May reproduce familiar tendon-related pain | Must be interpreted with the history and palpation |
| Middle-finger resistance | May provoke lateral elbow pain | Also loads structures associated with radial-tunnel symptoms |

**Cozen's Test Protocol:** Stabilise the elbow; patient makes fist with wrist in neutral. Examiner resists wrist extension. Positive = pain at lateral epicondyle.

**Mill's Test Protocol:** Elbow extended, forearm pronated. Examiner passively flexes wrist fully. Positive = pain at lateral epicondyle.

**Maudsley's Test Protocol:** Patient resists examiner pressing down on middle finger with elbow extended. Positive = lateral epicondyle pain.

**Clinical note:** No single test is diagnostic in isolation. A cluster of positive tests (≥2 of: lateral epicondyle tenderness, positive Cozen's, positive Mill's) increases diagnostic confidence. Exclude radial nerve compression (radial tunnel syndrome) as concurrent or alternative diagnosis.

---

## Red Flags

- Acute vascular compromise after limb injury—such as an absent pulse, cold/pale limb or acute sensorimotor compromise—requires emergency assessment. Do not use the classic “5 Ps” as a stand-alone diagnostic rule.
- Significant trauma with suspected fracture/dislocation requires urgent assessment.
- Progressive motor deficit, rapid neurological change, serious infection or malignancy concern requires prompt medical/specialist assessment.

Apply current suspected-cancer criteria rather than age plus night pain as an automatic cancer-pathway rule.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Tendinopathy Continuum Model (Cook & Purdam 2009; updated Cook et al. 2016)

The continuum model provides a framework for staging tendinopathy and guiding management:

| Stage | Pathology | Clinical Features | Management Approach |
|-------|-----------|-------------------|---------------------|
| **Reactive tendinopathy** | Short-term adaptive response; increased proteoglycan, water content | Acute onset after sudden load increase; swelling possible; pain with all load | Load reduction; relative rest; isometrics may reduce pain |
| **Tendon dysrepair** | Failed healing; early matrix disruption; neurovascular in-growth | Sub-acute; pain with higher loads; some thickening on US | Moderate load modification; graduated isotonic loading |
| **Degenerative tendinopathy** | Cell death, matrix disruption, failed collagen; angiofibroblastic changes | Chronic; pain with daily activities; may have pain-free window mid-activity | Graded loading; accept some pain during exercise (NRS 0–4); longer rehab timeline |

### Pain Monitoring Model
- **0–4/10 NRS during loading** — acceptable; continue programme
- **5–7/10 NRS** — monitor closely; modify load
- **>7/10 NRS or pain lingering >24 hours post-exercise** — reduce load; reassess stage

### Biopsychosocial Considerations
Central sensitisation is prevalent in chronic LE (>3 months). Assess:
- Pain catastrophising (PCS >30 → elevated risk of poor outcome)
- Fear-avoidance (FABQ)
- Work-related psychosocial factors (job demands, control, support)
- Sleep quality

---

## Outcome Measures

### Patient-Rated Tennis Elbow Evaluation (PRTEE)
- **Format:** 15 items; 0–100 scale (10 pain items + 5 function specific + 10 usual activities)
- **Score interpretation:** 0 = no pain/disability; 100 = worst possible
- **MCID:** 11 points (Stratford et al. 2001)
- **MDC:** 17 points
- **Time to complete:** 5 minutes

### DASH / QuickDASH
- **DASH:** 30 items; 0–100 (0 = no disability)
- **QuickDASH:** 11 items; 0–100
- **MCID for LE:** DASH 10.2 points; QuickDASH 16 points
- **Limitation:** Less condition-specific; useful for cross-condition comparison

### Grip Strength (Jamar Dynamometer)
- **Protocol:** Elbow at 90°, neutral forearm; 3 trials; mean recorded
- **Normative comparison:** Compare to contralateral limb (expect 5–10% dominant hand advantage)
- **Pain-free grip strength** often more clinically useful than maximum grip
- **MCID:** Approximately 6.5 kg for meaningful change
- **Reliability:** ICC 0.97–0.98 for Jamar at standardised handle position

### Global Rating of Change (GRC)
- 15-point scale (−7 to +7); clinically meaningful change = ±3 points

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

**Clinical diagnosis** is appropriate in typical presentations. Imaging is not routinely required for initial diagnosis.

**Ultrasound (US):**
- May show tendon changes, but imaging findings do not determine symptom relevance by themselves
- Useful to confirm diagnosis in atypical cases, guide injection, rule out partial/full-thickness tear
- Power Doppler demonstrates neovascularisation but correlation with symptoms is inconsistent
- **Indication:** Persistent symptoms >6 months despite conservative management, prior to injection, surgical planning

**MRI:**
- May characterise tendon or competing pathology when imaging will change management
- T2 signal increase at ECRB origin diagnostic of tendinopathy
- Reserve for surgical planning or diagnostic uncertainty
- Not routinely indicated in primary/community physiotherapy

**X-ray:**
- Not required for diagnosis; may show calcification (25–30% of chronic cases) or reveal alternative pathology
- Indicated with trauma history or red flags

**NCS/EMG:**
- Not indicated for lateral epicondylalgia
- Consider if radial tunnel syndrome or posterior interosseous nerve palsy suspected

**Clinical Prediction Rule:** No validated CPR exists for LE. Diagnosis is clinical based on:
1. Lateral epicondyle pain
2. Reproduced by resisted wrist extension and/or passive wrist flexion
3. Tenderness on palpation of the lateral epicondyle

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Distinguishing Features | Key Test |
|-----------|------------------------|----------|
| Radial tunnel syndrome | Pain 3–5 cm distal to lateral epicondyle; deep ache; nocturnal pain; weakness; positive resisted supination | Resisted supination at 0°; Frohse arcade tenderness |
| Posterior interosseous nerve palsy | Finger/wrist drop; weakness of finger extensors; may be painless | Neurological examination; NCS/EMG |
| Cervical radiculopathy (C6/C7) | Neck pain; dermatomal sensory loss; reduced reflexes; Spurling's positive | Spurling's test; cervical spine examination |
| Lateral elbow instability (PLRI) | Clicking, locking, giving way; trauma history; positive lateral pivot shift | Lateral pivot shift test; MRI |
| Synovial plica irritation | Intermittent snapping sensation; pain with full extension | Clinical; MRI |
| Elbow OA | Age >50; stiffness; crepitus; full ROM loss; X-ray changes | X-ray |
| Osteochondral loose body | Locking, catching, extension block | X-ray; MRI |
| Referred pain from shoulder/neck | Absence of local signs; pain not reproduced by local provocation | Full UL neurodynamic screen |
| Lateral epicondyle stress fracture | Rare; athletes; local bony tenderness; positive imaging | X-ray; MRI |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Use the current BESS Tennis Elbow/local pathway. In the absence of serious pathology, management is primarily conservative and exercise/load-management based. Progressive neurological change or significant trauma follows a separate specialist pathway.

---

### Conservative Management

#### Load Management (Grade A evidence)
- **Acute phase:** Relative rest from provocative activities; avoid sustained gripping >2 kg; modify keyboard/mouse ergonomics
- **Wrist splinting:** Cockup wrist splint (20–30° wrist extension) reduces ECRB load; evidence for symptom reduction in acute phase (moderate); not recommended long-term
- **Counterforce brace (epicondyle strap):** Moderate evidence for short-term pain reduction with activity; mechanism = force dampening at muscle belly

#### Exercise Therapy (Grade A evidence — strongest recommendation)
**Eccentric Exercise Programme (Svernlöv & Adolfsson 2001; Tyler et al. 2010):**
- Wrist extension eccentrics with forearm pronated over table edge
- 3 × 15 repetitions; 2× daily; 12-week programme
- Progress via increase in load (dumbbell/putty/theraband)
- Pain up to 5/10 acceptable

**Isometric Exercise (Stasinopoulos et al. 2017; Rio et al. 2015):**
- Wrist extension isometric holds: 5 × 45-second holds at 70% MVC
- Analgesic effect demonstrated; useful in reactive/irritable tendinopathy
- Can precede isotonic loading programme

**Heavy Slow Resistance (HSR) Protocol (Beyer et al. 2015 — adapted for LE):**
- Wrist extension with load: 3 sets; 15 reps → 8 reps → 6 reps over 12 weeks
- Load progressed to near-maximum effort
- Shown superior to eccentric-only in Achilles; extrapolated to upper limb

**Elbow Manipulation + Exercise (Bisset et al. 2006 — landmark RCT):**
- Mulligan's Mobilisation with Movement (MWM): lateral glide of elbow combined with gripping
- Combined with exercise showed superior outcomes to corticosteroid injection at 12 months
- 8 sessions over 8 weeks; pain-free grip force primary outcome

#### Ergonomic Advice (Grade B)
- Keyboard positioning; mouse size; tool grip modification
- Avoiding repetitive wrist extension under load
- Occupational therapy assessment if work-related

---

### Pharmacological and injection care

The BESS Tennis Elbow 2023 pathway recommends physiotherapy and recommends against corticosteroid injection for lateral elbow tendinopathy. Other medication decisions remain clinician/prescriber-led; no fixed application schedule or PPI threshold is asserted without an exact source.

**Platelet-Rich Plasma (PRP):**
- Emerging evidence; some RCTs show benefit over placebo/corticosteroid at 6–12 months
- Not currently commissioned within HSC NI (patient-funded in private sector)
- Systematic review (Arirachakaran et al. 2016) shows possible superiority over CSI at 6+ months
- Insufficient evidence to recommend routinely

**Autologous Blood Injection:**
- Similar rationale to PRP; limited high-quality RCT evidence
- Not routinely commissioned

**Nitric Oxide Patches (GTN):** Evidence and applicability remain under human review; no dose is prescribed by this page.

---

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Emergency/urgent assessment:** acute vascular compromise or suspected significant fracture/dislocation.
- **Prompt medical/specialist assessment:** objective progressive motor deficit, serious infection, mass lesion or suspected malignancy under applicable criteria.
- **Specialist assessment:** persistent disabling symptoms or diagnostic uncertainty despite appropriate conservative care.

No automatic imaging, injection or operative threshold is asserted.

---

1. Bisset L, Beller E, Jull G, Brooks P, Darnell R, Vicenzino B. (2006). Mobilisation with movement and exercise, corticosteroid injection, or wait and see for tennis elbow: randomised trial. *BMJ*, 333(7575), 939. DOI: 10.1136/bmj.38961.584653.AE

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Medial epicondylalgia (ME) — historically termed "golfer's elbow" or medial epicondylitis — is a common cause of medial elbow pain, accounting for approximately 10–20% of elbow tendinopathies presenting in clinical practice. True incidence is lower than lateral epicondylalgia; estimated at 0.4% of the general population per year, with peak onset in the 4th–5th decade.

**Pathophysiology:** As with lateral epicondylalgia, histological evidence confirms angiofibroblastic tendinosis rather than true inflammation. Nirschl (1992) described the same pathological changes: disorganised collagen, fibroblast proliferation, neovascularisation, absence of inflammatory cells. The condition represents failed tendon healing at the common flexor-pronator origin.

**Primary structures involved:**
- **Pronator teres** — most commonly affected (originates from medial epicondyle)
- **Flexor carpi radialis** — frequently co-involved
- **Flexor carpi ulnaris, palmaris longus, flexor digitorum superficialis** — less commonly

The origin is on the anterior aspect of the medial epicondyle; valgus stress during acceleration phases of throwing or golfing creates repetitive tensile overload.

**Mechanism of injury:**
- Repetitive wrist flexion and forearm pronation under load
- Valgus loading of medial elbow (throwing, golf, racquet sports)
- Occupational exposure: carpentry, plumbing, manual handling

**Important association — Ulnar collateral ligament (UCL):**
- UCL insufficiency co-exists in up to 60% of throwing athletes with ME
- Distinguish clinically as management differs (UCL reconstruction vs tendinopathy rehabilitation)

**Concurrent cubital tunnel syndrome:**
- Ulnar nerve involvement in 20–60% of ME cases
- Critical to assess for ulnar neuropathy at every consultation
- Failure to identify ulnar nerve involvement leads to suboptimal outcomes

---

## Special Tests

| Assessment component | Role | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Palpation, resisted wrist flexion and pronation | Reproduce load-related medial elbow symptoms | Not specific in isolation |
| Passive wrist-extension stretch | May reproduce familiar tendon-related pain | Interpret with load response and function |
| Valgus-stress assessment | Explores possible UCL involvement in the appropriate sporting context | Requires careful technique and is not a diagnosis alone |
| Ulnar-nerve provocation | Explores coexisting neural symptoms | Requires a full neurological assessment |

**Resisted Wrist Flexion Protocol:** Elbow extended, forearm supinated. Examiner resists wrist flexion. Positive = medial epicondyle pain reproduction.

**Moving Valgus Stress Test:** In an appropriate throwing-athlete assessment, this manoeuvre may reproduce symptoms associated with UCL loading. It does not establish a UCL injury in isolation.

**Elbow Flexion Test:** Maximum elbow flexion with wrist neutral maintained for 60 seconds. Positive = tingling/numbness in ring/little finger distribution. Best test for cubital tunnel syndrome.

---

## Red Flags

- Significant weakness, wasting or progressive neurological change requires prompt reassessment and specialist evaluation; “urgent surgical referral” is not assumed without a defined diagnosis and source.
- Suspected physeal/avulsion injury in a young athlete, acute unstable UCL injury, fracture/dislocation or neurovascular compromise requires appropriate imaging/specialist assessment.
- A mass lesion, serious infection or malignancy concern requires medical assessment according to urgency.

Hypothenar-hammer/ulnar-artery thrombosis is a separate vascular differential and should only be pursued when the presentation supports it.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Tendinopathy Continuum Model (Cook & Purdam 2009)
Apply same staging as lateral epicondylalgia:
- **Reactive:** Acute load spike; manage with relative rest, isometrics, provocative load avoidance
- **Dysrepair:** Subacute; graduated isotonic loading; monitor 24-hour pain response
- **Degenerative:** Chronic; accept working pain (NRS 0–4); long-term loading programme; psychosocial screen

### Ulnar Nerve Assessment Framework
In all ME cases, systematically assess:

1. **Subjective:** Paraesthesia/numbness in ring/little finger; nocturnal symptoms (elbow flexion in sleep); pain radiating down medial forearm
2. **Objective:** Froment's sign (intrinsic weakness); first dorsal interosseous wasting; clawing (ring/little finger); sensory testing (light touch, 2-point discrimination) in ulnar nerve territory
3. **Special tests:** Elbow flexion test, Tinel's at cubital tunnel
4. **Severity classification:**
   - Mild: Intermittent paraesthesia; no motor deficit
   - Moderate: Persistent paraesthesia; early motor weakness
   - Severe: Constant symptoms; significant weakness/wasting; consider urgent surgical referral

### Valgus Extension Overload Assessment (throwing athletes)
- Screen for UCL insufficiency (moving valgus stress test)
- Screen for posterior impingement (valgus extension overload test)
- Screen for radiocapitellar pathology

---

## Outcome Measures

### Patient-Rated Tennis Elbow Evaluation (PRTEE)
- Although named for tennis elbow, validated for use in medial epicondylalgia
- **Score:** 0–100; 0 = no pain/disability
- **MCID:** 11 points

### DASH / QuickDASH
- **DASH MCID:** 10.2 points (LE/ME combined upper limb)
- **QuickDASH MCID:** 16 points
- Useful for occupational/functional tracking

### Grip Strength (Jamar Dynamometer)
- Protocol: Elbow at 90°; 3 trials; dominant vs non-dominant comparison
- **Pain-free grip** is more clinically relevant than maximum grip in ME
- Expect deficit on affected side; monitor improvement over 6–8 weeks

### Numeric Pain Rating Scale (NPRS)
- **MCID:** 2 points (0–10 scale) for musculoskeletal pain
- Assess: worst pain (last 7 days), average pain, pain during resisted testing

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

**Diagnosis is primarily clinical** based on:
1. Medial elbow pain ± radiation into proximal forearm
2. Reproduction with resisted wrist flexion and/or forearm pronation
3. Medial epicondyle tenderness on palpation

**Ultrasound:**
- May show tendon changes, but imaging findings do not determine symptom relevance by themselves
- Power Doppler: neovascularisation correlates with symptom severity
- Indicated for diagnostic uncertainty, injection guidance, pre-surgical planning
- Can also assess UCL integrity (dynamic valgus stress view)

**MRI:**
- May characterise tendon, ligament or neural pathology when imaging will change management
- Indicated if UCL injury suspected, pre-surgical planning, diagnostic uncertainty
- MRI arthrogram superior for UCL assessment in throwing athletes

**NCS/EMG:**
- Indicated if ulnar neuropathy suspected (Elbow flexion test positive, Tinel's positive, motor deficit)
- Neurophysiology referral via GP or consultant (HSC NI: Belfast Trust, Southern Trust, Western Trust)
- NCS findings: reduced ulnar nerve conduction velocity across elbow (<50 m/s diagnostic); reduced compound motor action potential

**X-ray:**
- Not diagnostic for tendinopathy; may show medial epicondyle calcification
- Essential with trauma history to exclude fracture
- Valgus stress views for UCL assessment (comparative)

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Distinguishing Features | Key Investigation |
|-----------|------------------------|-------------------|
| Cubital tunnel syndrome | Paraesthesia in ring/little finger; positive elbow flexion test; motor deficit | NCS/EMG |
| UCL injury/instability | Valgus instability; throwing athlete; positive moving valgus stress test | MRI arthrogram |
| Medial epicondyle stress fracture | Young throwing athlete; X-ray/MRI changes | X-ray; MRI |
| Cervical radiculopathy C8/T1 | Neck pain; dermatomal pattern; positive Spurling's; reflex changes | Cervical X-ray; MRI cervical spine |
| Thoracic outlet syndrome | Positional symptoms; vascular signs; overhead provocation | Adson's; RROOS; vascular duplex |
| Pronator teres syndrome | Forearm pain; pronation-aggravated; tenderness distal to medial epicondyle; no elbow flexion test | Resisted pronation; NCS |
| Pancoast tumour | Progressive ulnar-distribution symptoms; shoulder pain; Horner's syndrome; smoker | Chest X-ray; CT chest |
| Medial plica irritation | Snapping sensation; no tendinopathy signs | MRI |
| Elbow OA | Diffuse pain; stiffness; crepitus; X-ray changes | X-ray |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Use individualised load management and rehabilitation when serious pathology has been excluded. Objective motor deficit, suspected UCL/avulsion injury or a mass lesion requires condition-appropriate specialist assessment.

---

### Conservative Management

#### Load Management (Grade A)
- Relative rest from provocative activity during acute phase
- Activity modification: avoid sustained wrist flexion under load; modify grip size/tool handles
- Wrist splint (neutral position) may reduce flexor-pronator load during acute phase

#### Exercise Programme (Grade B–A)
**Isometric Exercise (Phase 1 — Reactive):**
- Wrist flexion isometric holds: 5 × 45 seconds at 70% MVC
- Forearm pronation isometric holds
- Pain ≤5/10 acceptable; aim for immediate analgesic effect

**Eccentric Loading Programme (Phase 2 — Main Rehabilitation):**
- Eccentric wrist flexion over table edge (dumbbell/theraband): 3 × 15 reps × 2/day
- Eccentric forearm pronation with light resistance
- Progress load over 8–12 weeks; monitor 24-hour pain response
- Pain 0–4/10 during exercise is acceptable

**Progressive Resistance (Phase 3):**
- Wrist flexion, forearm pronation concentric-eccentric loading
- Grip strengthening (putty, hand exerciser)
- Elbow stability exercises (forearm plank, rotation under load)

#### Mobilisation (Grade B)
- Mulligan's Medial Glide + wrist flexion (adapted MWM): limited evidence but rationale similar to lateral epicondylalgia
- Neural mobilisation if ulnar nerve involvement identified (submaximal range; 3 × 10 reps)

#### Ergonomic Advice (Grade B)
- Wrist flexion avoidance; tool handle modification
- Sleep positioning (avoid elbow flexion >90° at night) — particularly important if ulnar neuropathy
- Elbow extension orthosis for sleep if cubital tunnel concurrent diagnosis

---

### Pharmacological and injection care

The prior fixed NSAID, injection and GTN regimens were not linked to an exact condition-specific authoritative recommendation and have been removed. Medication or injection is prescriber/specialist-led; extrapolation from lateral elbow or lower-limb tendinopathy must be labelled and human-reviewed.

---

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Prompt specialist assessment:** objective motor deficit, significant wasting, suspected UCL/physeal/avulsion injury or a mass lesion.
- **Urgent assessment:** fracture/dislocation, acute neurovascular compromise or serious infection.
- **Specialist assessment:** persistent disabling symptoms or diagnostic uncertainty despite appropriate conservative care.

Detailed operative approaches, fixed nerve/surgery timings, return-to-sport thresholds, outcome percentages and recovery timelines are outside Version 1 and remain future evidence expansion.

---

## Key References

1. Nirschl RP. (1992). Elbow tendinosis/tennis elbow. *Clinics in Sports Medicine*, 11(4), 851–870.

2. O'Driscoll SW, Lawton RL, Smith AM. (2004). The "moving valgus stress test" for medial collateral ligament tears of the elbow. *American Journal of Sports Medicine*, 32(4), 888–892.

3. Descatha A, Leclerc A, Chastang JF, Roquelaure Y. (2003). Medial epicondylitis in occupational settings: prevalence, incidence and associated risk factors. *Journal of Occupational and Environmental Medicine*, 45(9), 993–1001.
