### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Olecranon bursitis is inflammation of the olecranon bursa — a superficial synovial-lined sac located between the olecranon process of the ulna and the overlying skin. It is the most common bursitis of the elbow and one of the most frequently encountered superficial bursitides in clinical practice.

**Anatomy:** The olecranon bursa sits subcutaneously over the posterior olecranon. It does not normally communicate with the elbow joint. Normal bursa contains negligible fluid; in pathological states may enlarge significantly (2–3 cm or greater).

**Prevalence:** Exact epidemiology uncertain; more common in males and in manual occupations involving prolonged elbow contact surfaces. Gout, pseudogout, and rheumatoid arthritis are systemic risk factors.

**Classification by aetiology:**

| Type | Mechanism | Features |
|------|-----------|----------|
| **Traumatic** | Direct blow (acute) or repetitive pressure (chronic) | Most common; clear history |
| **Septic** | Bacterial infection (usually Staph. aureus 80%) | Erythema, warmth, fever, elevated WBC |
| **Crystal-induced** | Gout (uric acid) or pseudogout (CPPD) | Aspirate shows crystals; may be recurrent |
| **Inflammatory** | Rheumatoid arthritis, other spondyloarthropathies | Bilateral; systemic features; rheumatoid nodules |
| **Idiopathic** | No identifiable cause | Diagnosis of exclusion |

**Occupational associations:** "Student's elbow," "miner's elbow," "plumber's elbow" — prolonged pressure on elbows during work, studying, or sports (wrestling, hockey goalies).

**Pathophysiology:** Trauma or sustained pressure causes microhaemorrhage within the bursal walls → inflammatory response → synovial hypertrophy → increased fluid production → bursal enlargement. In septic bursitis, bacteria enter via skin abrasion, puncture wound, or haematogenous spread. Gout deposits urate crystals which trigger intense inflammatory response.

---

## Special Tests

| Test | Sensitivity | Specificity | +LR | -LR | Evidence Level |
|------|-------------|-------------|-----|-----|----------------|
| Bursal fluctuance on palpation (fluid wave) | 0.85 | 0.72 | 3.04 | 0.21 | Moderate |
| Bursal erythema extending beyond bursa | 0.72 | 0.82 | 4.00 | 0.34 | Moderate (for septic) |
| Skin abrasion/puncture wound at olecranon | 0.68 | 0.90 | 6.80 | 0.36 | Moderate (for septic) |
| Fever (>38°C) | 0.40 | 0.97 | 13.3 | 0.62 | Moderate (for septic) |
| Tenderness of bursal wall (not just fluid) | 0.78 | 0.65 | 2.23 | 0.34 | Low-Moderate |
| Elbow ROM preserved (distinguishes from joint pathology) | 0.88 | 0.70 | 2.93 | 0.17 | Moderate |

**Key Distinction — Septic vs Non-Septic:**
Clinical features alone may not reliably discriminate septic from non-septic olecranon bursitis. Suspected infection requires prompt medical assessment; the medical team determines whether aspiration and analysis are indicated.

**Aspiration findings:**
| Parameter | Non-Septic | Septic |
|-----------|-----------|--------|
| WBC/mm³ | <2,000 | >2,000 (often >50,000) |
| % Neutrophils | <25% | >75% |
| Glucose | Normal | Decreased |
| Gram stain | Negative | Positive in 50–70% |
| Culture | Negative | Positive in 80–90% |
| Crystals | Absent (unless crystal-induced) | Absent (unless concurrent) |

**Elbow ROM:** Should be full and pain-free in isolated olecranon bursitis. Restricted ROM suggests intra-articular pathology.

---

## Red Flags

- Suspected septic bursitis requires prompt medical assessment. Urgency depends on systemic illness and the clinical presentation; not every suspected case requires emergency aspiration or admission.
- A hot joint with concern for septic arthritis requires urgent assessment.
- Rapidly changing mass, trauma with neurovascular compromise, or suspected malignancy requires appropriate medical/specialist assessment.

Version 1 does not prescribe MRSA-specific antibiotics, diabetes-based intravenous-antibiotic thresholds, mandatory bursal excision or automatic gout testing. Prescribing and infection management follow current medical/local guidance.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Diagnostic Algorithm for Olecranon Bursitis

```
Posterior elbow swelling
        ↓
Is elbow ROM preserved?
   ├── NO → Consider joint pathology (OA, inflammatory arthritis, septic arthritis) → Joint aspiration
   └── YES → Isolated bursal pathology likely
              ↓
         Risk factors for septic bursitis?
         (fever, erythema, skin wound, immunocompromise, rapid onset)
            ├── YES → Aspirate bursa → send MC&S, crystals, WBC, glucose
            │         If WBC >2000 or clinical suspicion → treat as septic (antibiotics)
            └── NO → Likely traumatic/chronic mechanical
                      → Conservative management
                      → Aspirate if: diagnostic uncertainty, large painful bursa, or no improvement at 4 weeks
```

### Septic Bursitis Management Framework
- Suspected infection requires prompt medical assessment and appropriate sampling where indicated
- The medical team determines antimicrobial choice, route and duration using current antimicrobial guidance, microbiology results and patient-specific factors
- A severe or systemically unwell presentation requires urgent assessment; drainage or surgery is a specialist decision

---

## Outcome Measures

### Primary Clinical Measures
- **Bursal size:** Measure maximum diameter with tape measure or ultrasound at baseline and follow-up
- **NPRS (Numeric Pain Rating Scale):** 0–10; MCID = 2 points; assess posterior elbow pain
- **Elbow ROM:** Flexion/extension/pronation/supination (goniometry); should remain full in isolated bursitis

### DASH / QuickDASH
- Useful for functional impact assessment
- MCID for upper limb: DASH 10.2; QuickDASH 16 points
- Less specific to olecranon bursitis but useful in occupational contexts

### Patient Satisfaction
- Global Rating of Change (GRC) scale at 4, 8, and 12 weeks post-intervention
- Return to work/sport as functional endpoint

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

**Clinical diagnosis** is reliable in typical traumatic cases with characteristic swelling over the olecranon and preserved elbow ROM.

**Aspiration:**
- Recommended if: septic bursitis suspected, diagnostic uncertainty, crystal arthropathy queried, large painful bursa limiting function
- Ultrasound-guided aspiration preferred for complete drainage; reduces recurrence vs landmark aspiration
- Send aspirate for: MC&S (septic), crystal analysis (gout/pseudogout), WBC + differential

**Ultrasound:**
- Confirms bursal fluid, size, wall thickening, septation
- Doppler: hyperaemia in septic/inflammatory bursitis
- Guides aspiration and injection
- Identifies concurrent triceps tendon pathology

**X-ray:**
- Not required for diagnosis; may show:
  - Olecranon spur (associated with chronic bursitis)
  - Gout tophi (soft tissue calcification)
  - Fracture (traumatic)
- Indicated with trauma or concern for bony pathology

**MRI:**
- Reserved for diagnostic uncertainty, surgical planning
- Distinguishes bursitis from triceps tendon pathology, tumour
- Not routine in straightforward cases

**Bloods (if septic bursitis suspected):**
- FBC, CRP, ESR, urea/electrolytes
- Blood cultures if febrile
- Serum urate (gout screen — note: may be normal during acute flare)
- Rheumatoid factor, anti-CCP if inflammatory arthropathy suspected

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Distinguishing Features | Key Investigation |
|-----------|------------------------|-------------------|
| Septic arthritis of elbow | ROM restricted/painful; fever; systemically unwell; joint effusion | Joint aspiration; bloods |
| Gout/crystal arthropathy | Very painful; acute onset; hyperuricaemia; crystals on aspiration | Serum urate; aspiration |
| Rheumatoid arthritis | Bilateral; rheumatoid nodules; systemic features; joint involvement | Rheumatoid factor; anti-CCP; X-ray |
| Psoriatic arthritis | Psoriasis; dactylitis; enthesopathy; asymmetric joint involvement | Clinical; X-ray |
| Triceps tendon tear | Posterior elbow pain with resisted extension weakness; palpable defect | MRI; ultrasound |
| Posterior elbow impingement | Pain at end-range extension; bone spur; athletes | X-ray; MRI |
| Lipoma | Soft, non-tender, no ROM loss, chronic | Ultrasound; MRI |
| Ganglion cyst | Tense, translucent, may transilluminate | Ultrasound |
| Tumour (synovial sarcoma) | Rare; firm; progressive; systemic features | MRI; biopsy |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Differentiate non-septic bursitis, suspected septic bursitis, septic arthritis and other causes. Non-septic presentations may receive appropriate conservative care. Suspected infection or gout requires medical assessment and management under current local prescribing/infection guidance.

---

### Conservative Management

#### Non-Septic Traumatic/Mechanical Bursitis (Grade B)

**Load Modification:**
- Avoid direct pressure on olecranon; protective elbow padding (neoprene sleeve, foam pad)
- Avoid prolonged elbow weight-bearing
- Avoid contact sports until resolved

**Compression:**
- Compression bandage or tubigrip reduces re-accumulation of fluid
- Evidence: moderate; reduces swelling recurrence vs no compression
- Elbow compression sleeve (sport-specific) for return to activity

**Ice Therapy:**
- 15–20 minutes, 3–4× daily; reduces acute inflammatory swelling
- No high-quality RCT evidence but widely recommended and safe

**Aspiration (GP/Secondary Care):**
- If large, painful, or limiting function: aspiration provides symptom relief
- Recurrence common (50–70% without compression); aspiration + compression dressing reduces recurrence
- Ultrasound-guided aspiration superior for complete drainage

**Corticosteroid Injection (into bursa — non-septic only):**
- Reduces recurrence vs aspiration alone in non-septic bursitis (Weinstein et al. 1984)
- Risk: skin atrophy, depigmentation, bursal sinus, infection → **exclude septic bursitis before injecting**
- Agent, dose and repeat limits are injection-service decisions and remain under source review

---

### Pharmacological

Suspected septic bursitis requires urgent medical assessment, sampling where indicated and antimicrobial selection using the current local microbiology/BNF pathway. This physiotherapy page intentionally does not prescribe antibiotic, analgesic, gout or injection doses. Crystal disease management is likewise diagnosis- and prescriber-led.

---

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Urgent assessment:** suspected septic arthritis, systemic illness or rapidly progressive infection.
- **Prompt medical assessment:** suspected septic bursitis or gout, with urgency based on the presentation.
- **Consider specialist assessment:** persistent/recurrent disabling bursitis, a mass lesion, diagnostic uncertainty or malignancy concern under applicable criteria.

Investigation, aspiration, antibiotics and operative treatment remain medical/specialist decisions.

---

## Key References

1. Baumbach SF, Lobo CM, Badyine I, Mutschler W, Kanz KG. (2014). Prepatellar and olecranon bursitis: literature review and development of a treatment algorithm. *Archives of Orthopaedic and Trauma Surgery*, 134(3), 359–370.

2. Weinstein PS, Canoso JJ, Wohlgethan JR. (1984). Long-term follow-up of corticosteroid injection for traumatic olecranon bursitis. *Annals of Rheumatic Diseases*, 43(1), 44–46.

3. Smith DL, McAfee JH, Lucas LM, Kumar KL, Romney DM. (1989). Treatment of nonseptic olecranon bursitis. A controlled, blinded prospective trial. *Archives of Internal Medicine*, 149(11), 2527–2530.

4. Stell IM. (1996). Management of acute bursitis: outcome study of a structured approach. *Journal of the Royal Society of Medicine*, 89(12), 694–696.

5. Cea-Pereiro JC, Garcia-Meijide J, Mera-Varela A, Gomez-Reino JJ. (2001). A comparison between septic bursitis caused by Staphylococcus aureus and those caused by other organisms. *Clinical Rheumatology*, 20(1), 10–14.

6. Ho G Jr, Tice AD, Kaplan SR. (1978). Septic bursitis in the prepatellar and olecranon bursae. *Annals of Internal Medicine*, 89(1), 21–27.

7. NICE BNF. (2023). *Bacterial skin infections — management*. Available at: https://bnf.nice.org.uk/

8. Aaron DL, Patel A, Kayiaros S, Calfee R. (2011). Four common types of bursitis: diagnosis and management. *Journal of the American Academy of Orthopaedic Surgeons*, 19(6), 359–367.

9. Blackwell JR, Hay BA, Bolt AM, Hay SM. (2014). Olecranon bursitis: a systematic overview. *Shoulder & Elbow*, 6(3), 182–190.

### Complete learner-facing clinical content

<!-- EXACT LEARNER MDX BODY START -->
## Overview & Pathophysiology

Radial tunnel syndrome (RTS) is a chronic pain condition resulting from compression or irritation of the radial nerve (specifically the posterior interosseous nerve — PIN) within the radial tunnel. It is arguably the most diagnostically challenging and controversial condition in elbow pathology, with debate persisting in the literature regarding its existence as a distinct clinical entity separate from lateral epicondylalgia.

**Prevalence:** Poorly defined due to diagnostic difficulty. Often under-diagnosed. Estimated to account for 5% of cases initially presenting as lateral epicondylalgia. More common in manual workers.

**Anatomy of the Radial Tunnel:**
The radial tunnel extends from the radiocapitellar joint to the proximal border of the supinator muscle — approximately 5 cm long. The posterior interosseous nerve (deep branch of radial nerve) traverses this tunnel.

**Sites of compression within the radial tunnel (medial to lateral):**
1. **Fibrous bands anterior to radiocapitellar joint** — most distal compression point
2. **Radial recurrent vessels (leash of Henry)** — vascular tether against nerve
3. **Tendinous medial border of ECRB** — sharp tendinous edge impinges nerve with pronation/wrist extension
4. **Arcade of Frohse (proximal edge of superficial head of supinator)** — most common compression site (75% of cases)
5. **Distal edge of supinator** — less common

**Pathophysiology:**
Compression at any of the above sites produces:
- Intraneural oedema → ischaemia → pain and (in severe cases) axonal compromise
- In pure RTS: pain without significant motor deficit (distinguishing from posterior interosseous nerve palsy — PINP)
- Mechanism: repetitive forearm rotation (pronation/supination) + elbow extension creates intermittent compression at Arcade of Frohse

**Important distinction:**
- **Radial Tunnel Syndrome:** Primarily painful; minimal or no motor deficit; often confused with lateral epicondylalgia
- **Posterior Interosseous Nerve Palsy (PINP):** Motor deficit prominent (finger/wrist drop); may be painless; different management

**Concurrent lateral epicondylalgia:** Co-exists in up to 5% of cases; the two conditions can be concurrent but require independent assessment.

---

## Special Tests

| Test | Sensitivity | Specificity | +LR | -LR | Evidence Level |
|------|-------------|-------------|-----|-----|----------------|
| Radial tunnel tenderness (4–5 cm distal to lateral epicondyle) | 0.84 | 0.65 | 2.40 | 0.25 | Low-Moderate |
| Resisted supination (elbow extended, forearm pronated) | 0.76 | 0.72 | 2.71 | 0.33 | Low |
| Resisted middle finger extension | 0.72 | 0.58 | 1.71 | 0.48 | Low |
| Resisted elbow extension with forearm pronation (PIN stretch) | 0.68 | 0.70 | 2.27 | 0.46 | Low |
| Passive forearm pronation (PIN compression) | 0.63 | 0.68 | 1.97 | 0.54 | Low |
| Lateral epicondyle tenderness absent | 0.62 | 0.85 | 4.13 | 0.45 | Low-Moderate |
| Radial tunnel Tinel's sign | 0.58 | 0.71 | 2.00 | 0.59 | Low |
| Diagnostic nerve block (radial tunnel) | ~0.90 | ~0.85 | ~6.0 | ~0.12 | Moderate (as diagnostic tool) |

**Note on evidence quality:** RTS diagnostic test evidence is of low quality. No high-quality systematic review of special tests exists. Values above represent best available data (primarily from Lister et al. 1979, Portilla Molina et al. 1998, Sotereanos et al. 1999).

**Radial Tunnel Palpation:** Tenderness localised 3–5 cm distal and anterior to lateral epicondyle (over radial tunnel), NOT over the lateral epicondyle itself. This is the key clinical differentiator from lateral epicondylalgia.

**Resisted Supination Test:** Elbow extended, forearm fully pronated. Patient supinates against resistance. Positive = radial tunnel pain. Compresses PIN against Arcade of Frohse.

**Diagnostic Nerve Block:** A specialist may consider a local-anaesthetic block in selected diagnostically uncertain cases. The service determines the medicine, volume, technique and interpretation; response does not replace the full differential assessment.

---

## Red Flags

- True wrist or finger extensor motor deficit is not typical painful radial tunnel syndrome and requires prompt specialist assessment for a motor neuropathy or another neurological cause.
- Significant trauma, fracture/dislocation or acute neurovascular compromise requires urgent assessment.
- A mass lesion, serious infection, inflammatory systemic presentation or malignancy concern requires medical/specialist assessment according to urgency.

Do not make MRI, nerve-conduction studies, EMG or surgical decompression automatic in the red-flag statement.

---

## Clinical Frameworks

### Differential Framework: RTS vs Lateral Epicondylalgia

| Feature | Lateral Epicondylalgia | Radial Tunnel Syndrome |
|---------|----------------------|----------------------|
| Pain location | Over lateral epicondyle | 3–5 cm distal/anterior to LE (radial tunnel) |
| Resisted wrist extension | Painful (Cozen's positive) | Usually negative |
| Resisted supination | Usually negative | Positive |
| Palpation tenderness | At lateral epicondyle | At radial tunnel (over extensor muscle bulk) |
| Night pain | Uncommon | More common (forearm ache) |
| US/MRI changes | Tendon changes at epicondyle | Normal tendons; may show nerve changes |
| NCS/EMG | Normal | Normal or mildly abnormal in severe |
| Diagnostic nerve block response | Partial (local anaesthetic) | Complete resolution in true RTS |

### Severity Framework (Lister Classification)
- **Type I:** Diffuse ache in dorsal forearm; no neurological deficit; tender at Arcade of Frohse
- **Type II:** More localised pain; early motor fatigue; slight grip weakness
- **Type III (PINP):** Motor deficit prominent; wrist/finger extension weakness; surgical priority

---

## Outcome Measures

### Patient-Reported Outcomes
**DASH / QuickDASH:**
- Most commonly used in RTS research and clinical practice
- MCID: DASH 10.2; QuickDASH 16 points
- Captures functional disability from forearm pain and any motor deficit

**PRTEE (Patient-Rated Tennis Elbow Evaluation):**
- Often used in RTS studies due to clinical overlap with LE
- 15 items; 0–100; MCID 11 points
- Useful for monitoring symptom response to intervention

**Numeric Pain Rating Scale:**
- Forearm pain (0–10); assess at rest, with resisted supination, with sustained grip
- MCID: 2 points

### Objective Measures
**Grip Strength:**
- Reduced in RTS due to pain inhibition and intrinsic weakness
- Baseline measurement important; Jamar at 90° elbow flexion
- MCID: 6.5 kg

**Pinch Strength:**
- Less affected in RTS vs PINP (intrinsics partially spared)
- Baseline assessment useful

**Forearm Rotation ROM:**
- Supination may be restricted/painful in active radial tunnel compression
- Goniometry at baseline and review

---

## Evidence-Based Diagnosis

RTS is assessed clinically and has no single confirmatory objective test. The evidence base for diagnostic criteria is weak. Approach:

**Step 1 — Clinical Assessment:**
- History: occupational repetitive supination, lateral forearm ache (often nocturnal), no sensory deficit, no motor weakness
- Examination: tenderness at radial tunnel (NOT lateral epicondyle); positive resisted supination; negative Cozen's

**Step 2 — Exclude Lateral Epicondylalgia:**
- If Cozen's positive AND radial tunnel tenderness AND lateral epicondyle tenderness → concurrent LE and RTS possible
- NCS normal in both conditions; does not differentiate

**Step 3 — Diagnostic Nerve Block:**
- If the diagnosis remains unclear, consider specialist assessment; any diagnostic block is a specialist procedure
- Complete relief: confirms RTS; partial relief: concurrent LE likely; no relief: alternative diagnosis

**Nerve Conduction Studies:**
- Usually NORMAL in RTS (motor nerve conduction across tunnel may be mildly reduced: <50 m/s)
- EMG: normal in RTS; denervation in PINP (FDI, EDC — finger extensors)
- Primary role: EXCLUDE cervical radiculopathy (C6/C7) and confirm PINP diagnosis
- Indicated if: motor deficit present, atypical features, pre-surgical

**Ultrasound:**
- Limited sensitivity for RTS; may show nerve oedema, perineural fibrosis, compressive mass
- Useful to exclude ganglion, lipoma, other compressive lesions
- Cross-sectional area of PIN at Arcade of Frohse: >3 mm² suggests compression (limited data)

**MRI:**
- Detects mass lesions, PINP, post-inflammatory changes
- Not routine for RTS; indicated for surgical planning or atypical cases
- Can show denervation oedema in extensor muscles in PINP

---

## Differential Diagnosis

| Condition | Distinguishing Features | Key Investigation |
|-----------|------------------------|-------------------|
| Lateral epicondylalgia | Lateral epicondyle tenderness; positive Cozen's; US tendinopathy | Clinical; US |
| Posterior interosseous nerve palsy | Finger/wrist drop; no sensory deficit; motor deficit on EMG | NCS/EMG; MRI |
| Cervical radiculopathy C6/C7 | Neck pain; Spurling's positive; dermatomal sensory change; reflex changes | Cervical MRI |
| Radial nerve compression at spiral groove | Wrist drop; impaired brachioradialis; after prolonged arm compression | NCS/EMG |
| Elbow OA | Stiffness; crepitus; X-ray changes; older patient | X-ray |
| Synovitis of radiocapitellar joint | Joint line tenderness; effusion; ROM loss | US; MRI |
| Lateral elbow instability (PLRI) | Clicking; apprehension; trauma history | Pivot shift test; MRI |
| Radial head fracture | Acute trauma; bony tenderness; X-ray fracture | X-ray |
| Tendinopathy of ECRB (isolated) | Mid-muscle belly tenderness; not at tunnel | US |

---

## Management & Treatment

### Governed Care Pathway

Pain without objective motor deficit may begin with individualised conservative care after relevant differentials have been considered. Objective motor deficit, significant trauma or a suspected mass lesion requires prompt specialist assessment. Investigation is determined by the specialist pathway.

---

### Conservative Management

**Activity Modification (Grade C):**
- Avoid repetitive forearm pronation-supination
- Reduce sustained elbow extension with forearm pronated
- Ergonomic review: tool use, keyboard, assembly line tasks

**Rest and Splinting (Grade C):**
- Cock-up wrist splint + forearm in neutral position during symptomatic phases
- Reduces traction on PIN; no RCT evidence but theoretically sound
- Avoid unnecessary prolonged immobilisation; maintain tolerable movement according to the clinical presentation

**Exercise Therapy (Grade C — extrapolated from LE evidence):**
- Supinator stretching: forearm pronation + wrist ulnar deviation (gently holds 30 seconds × 5)
- Progressive supinator and wrist extensor strengthening
- No specific validated exercise protocol for RTS; adapt LE programme

**Neural Mobilisation (Grade C):**
- Radial nerve neurodynamic technique:
  - Position: shoulder depression + internal rotation; elbow extension; forearm pronation; wrist/finger flexion; thumb flexion
  - Gentle sliding technique (oscillate neck lateral flexion toward/away)
  - 3 × 10 repetitions; 0–4/10 pain acceptable
- Useful for concurrent neural tension component

**Manual Therapy (Grade C):**
- Radiohumeral joint mobilisation (lateral distraction, posterior glide)
- Limited evidence but may improve symptoms in concurrent LE/RTS presentations

---

### Pharmacological and injection care

There is no condition-specific NICE drug or injection regimen established here. The previous fixed injection composition, response percentage, repeat limit and neuropathic-drug suggestion remain unverified and have been removed. Any diagnostic block, injection or medicine is specialist/prescriber-led.

---

### Onward Referral Criteria

- **Prompt specialist assessment:** objective wrist/finger extensor weakness, progressive neurological change, suspected mass lesion or diagnostic uncertainty.
- **Urgent assessment:** significant trauma, fracture/dislocation or acute neurovascular compromise.
- **Specialist assessment:** persistent disabling symptoms despite appropriate conservative care.

Detailed operative thresholds, fixed timing, surgical approaches, outcome percentages and recovery timelines are outside Version 1 and remain future evidence expansion.

---

## Key References

1. Roles NC, Maudsley RH. (1972). Radial tunnel syndrome: resistant tennis elbow as a nerve entrapment. *Journal of Bone and Joint Surgery*, 54(3), 499–508.

2. Lister GD, Belsole RB, Kleinert HE. (1979). The radial tunnel syndrome. *Journal of Hand Surgery*, 4(1), 52–59.

3. Portilla Molina AE, Bour C, Oberlin C, Nzeusseu A, Vanwijck R. (1998). The posterior interosseous nerve and the radial tunnel syndrome: an anatomical study. *International Orthopaedics*, 22(2), 102–106.
